# PSB Contractor Content Package for gondaliyacpa.ca

Prepared October 3, 2026 for Sharad Gondaliya, CPA. Responsible person for all build, publishing and reporting tasks: Navneet. Delivery order: Output 1, Output 2, Output 5, Output 3 (10 pages), Output 4 (10 posts), then the decisions, rate changes and existing page fixes.

All rates were checked on October 2, 2026. The current combined Ontario small business rate is 11.2% (from July 1, 2026), so every piece compares 11.2% with 44.5%.

| Section | Contents |
|---|---|
| Output 1 | Roadmap, 3 weeks from Monday, October 5, 2026, to Friday, October 23, 2026 |
| Output 2 | Email to Navneet, ready to send |
| Output 5 | SEO strategy: cluster map, keyword map, E-E-A-T, AI answer engine plan, llms.txt, Rank Math settings, measurement, table CSS |
| Output 3 | 10 pages with briefs, full copy, FAQs, internal links and JSON-LD; page 7 includes the calculator code |
| Output 4 | 10 posts with briefs, full drafts, FAQs, internal links, JSON-LD and LinkedIn posts |
| Lists | Decisions on the 14 approval items; Rate changes found; Existing page fixes and rate correction rules |

# Output 1: Roadmap (3 weeks, Monday, October 5, 2026 to Friday, October 23, 2026)

Pages go live on Wednesday, October 14, 2026. Posts 1 to 5 go live on Friday, October 16, 2026. Posts 6 to 10 go live on Tuesday, October 20, 2026. The 30-day and 60-day reviews stay time-based, because Google needs that time to index and rank the pages.

# Output 2: Email to Navneet

**To:** Navneet

**Subject:** PSB Contractor Content: 10 Pages and 10 Posts Live by October 23, 2026

Good morning Navneet,

Kindly start the PSB contractor content project on Monday, October 5, 2026. It covers 10 new pages and 10 new blog posts for incorporated IT and finance contractors. The full drafts, JSON-LD, LinkedIn posts, the calculator code and the Existing Page Fixes file are in the attached ZIP file.

**Project Rules**

| # | Rule |
|---|---|
| 1 | Ownership. You are the responsible person for every task in this project, from building and publishing to tracking and reporting. Kindly send every update to me directly. |
| 2 | Additive only. Do not change any existing page content. The only exceptions are rules 7, 8 and 9 below. |
| 3 | Slugs are final. We are moving to Astro with no URL changes, so kindly use every slug exactly as listed below. |
| 4 | CPA review first. Every page and post comes to me for CPA review before publishing. Nothing goes live without my written approval. |
| 5 | Deadline. All 20 URLs must be live and checked by Friday, October 23, 2026. |
| 6 | Rates. The combined Ontario small business rate is 11.2% from July 1, 2026. Every draft uses 11.2% vs 44.5%. Kindly do not change any figure in the drafts. |
| 7 | Page 7 replaces our old calculator. On Wednesday, October 14, once page 7 is live, kindly set a 301 redirect from /personal-services-business-tax-calculator/ to /psb-tax-cost-calculator/ in Rank Math Redirections and move the old page to draft. Do not delete it. Kindly add this redirect to the Astro migration redirect list too. |
| 8 | Kindly add one link from /software-development-it-consultants/ to /accountant-for-it-contractors/. Link only, no wording change. |
| 9 | Existing page fixes. I will review the before and after of the 23 fixes in the Existing Page Fixes file by Tuesday, October 6. Kindly make them live by Thursday, October 8. Do not run a search-and-replace on the bare text 12.2%. |
| 10 | Rate correction. The other existing pages that still show 12.2% or 3.2% as the current rate will be corrected from Monday, October 26 to Friday, November 6, 2026. Kindly send me the before and after in daily batches of 50 URLs, starting October 26. |

## 5.4 AI answer engine plan

| Item | How it is built |
|---|---|
| Direct-answer block | A 40 to 60 word answer sits directly under every H1. Keep it as the first paragraph; do not put a banner or image above it. |
| Question-format H2s | Most H2s are written as the question a contractor types or asks an assistant. Keep the wording. |
| FAQ schema | One FAQPage node per URL, text identical to the visible FAQs. If an Elementor FAQ widget or Rank Math FAQ block also outputs FAQ schema, turn that output off so each URL has one FAQPage. |
| Entity consistency | Name, address, phone, email, hours and Firm ID below must read the same in copy, JSON-LD, Google Business Profile and footer. |

llms.txt entries (ready to paste):

```
## Personal services business (PSB) risk for incorporated contractors
- [Personal Services Business Risk for Ontario Contractors](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-risk-incorporated-contractors/): Personal services business risk in Ontario, explained by a CPA: 11.2% vs 44.5% tax, denied expenses, CRA tests, and how we help you support your position.
- [Accountant for IT Contractors With Ontario Corporations](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/accountant-for-it-contractors/): Accountant for IT contractors with an Ontario corporation: T2, bookkeeping, payroll, GST/HST and tax planning with PSB risk in view. Please book a call.
- [Accountant for Finance Contractors in Ontario](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/accountant-for-finance-contractors/): Accountant for finance contractors in Ontario: T2, payroll and tax planning for FP&A, controller, audit and risk contractors, with PSB risk in view.
- [40 Hour Contract PSB Risk for Ontario Contractors](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/40-hour-contract-psb-risk/): On a 35, 37.5 or 40 hour contract? Hours are one control fact, not the answer. See what CRA weighs, what to document, and how a Toronto CPA reviews it.
- [PSB Contract Review for Ontario IT and Finance Contractors](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/contract-review-psb-indicators/): PSB contract review for Ontario contractors: we read your agency agreement, SOW and onboarding papers, then compare them to how you really work.
- [Staffing Agency Contractor Tax in Ontario: A CPA Guide](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/staffing-agency-contractor-tax/): Staffing agency contractor tax in Ontario: agency paperwork, invoicing, 13% HST, and why CRA looks past the agency to the end client for PSB risk.
- [PSB Tax Cost Calculator: 11.2% vs 44.5% in Ontario](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-tax-cost-calculator/): Estimate the extra corporate tax if CRA treats your Ontario corporation as a PSB: 11.2% vs 44.5%, denied expenses and a salary scenario.
- [Salary vs Dividend for Incorporated Contractors in Ontario](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/salary-vs-dividend-incorporated-contractor/): Salary vs dividend for incorporated contractors in Ontario: 11.2% vs 44.5% PSB rates, salary under 18(1)(p), eligible dividends, RRSP room and CPP.
- [CRA PSB Review in Ontario: What to Expect and How We Help](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/cra-psb-review-support/): Has CRA contacted your corporation about PSB status? See each stage of a CRA PSB review, what CRA asks for, and how we act as your representative.
- [Incorporated Contractor Accountant Toronto and the GTA](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/incorporated-contractor-accountant-toronto/): Toronto and GTA accountant for incorporated IT and finance contractors: T2, bookkeeping, payroll, GST/HST and tax planning that includes a PSB risk review.
- [What Is a Personal Services Business in Canada? CPA Guide](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/what-is-a-personal-services-business-canada/): What is a personal services business? A CPA walks IT contractors through the ITA 125(7) definition, both exceptions and a $171,000 Ontario example.
- [Does a 40-Hour Contract Make You an Incorporated Employee?](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/40-hour-contract-incorporated-employee/): A 40-hour contract alone does not make you an incorporated employee. A CPA explains section 125(7), the 10% shareholder link and the tax at 11.2% vs 44.5%.
- [Contract Hours Control Test: 35 vs 37.5 vs 40 Hours](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/contract-hours-cra-control-test/): What 35, 37.5 and 40 hour contract weeks signal to CRA under the control test, why hours never decide PSB status alone, and the 11.2% vs 44.5% cost.
- [PSB Tax Rate in Ontario for 2026: 44.5% vs 11.2% Explained](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-tax-rate-ontario-2026/): The PSB tax rate in Ontario is 44.5% vs 11.2% for small business income from July 1, 2026. See how 44.5% is built, the 11.70% blend and worked examples.
- [Wiebe Door Test: The Four Tests CRA Uses for Contractors](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/wiebe-door-sagaz-tests-contractors/): The Wiebe Door test and Sagaz in plain words: control, tools, profit and loss, and integration, applied step by step to a hypothetical IT contractor.
- [PSB Employee Exception: Who Qualifies and Who Does Not](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-more-than-five-employees-exception/): The PSB employee exception needs more than five full-time employees all year. See who counts, who does not, and why it rarely helps a solo contractor.
- [12 Contract Clauses That Raise PSB Risk for Contractors](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/contract-clauses-psb-risk/): 12 contract clauses that raise PSB risk for Ontario IT and finance contractors, why each one matters, what can weigh the other way, and what PSB costs.
- [Finance Contractor PSB Risk at Banks Through Agencies](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/finance-contractor-bank-agency-psb/): Finance contractor PSB risk: why the agency does not shield you, how CRA reads bank controls, and what 44.5% vs 11.2% costs on a $163,200 contract.
- [PSB Reassessment in Ontario: Denied Deductions, Double Tax](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-reassessment-denied-deductions/): In a PSB reassessment, CRA denies the small business deduction and most expenses, and taxes the income at 44.5% in Ontario. See the numbers, line by line.
- [PSB Risk Checklist for Incorporated Contractors in Ontario](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-risk-reduction-checklist/): A yearly PSB risk checklist for Ontario IT and finance contractors: contract, work, tools, clients, records and pay mix, and what to keep on file.
```

## 5.7 Table gridlines (one-time CSS)

Add this once in Astra, Customize, Custom CSS, then add the CSS class psb-cluster to the Elementor container that holds the copy on each of the 20 URLs. It affects only those containers, so existing pages do not change.

```css
.psb-cluster table{border-collapse:collapse;width:100%;margin:16px 0}
.psb-cluster th,.psb-cluster td{border:1px solid #c9d3df;padding:8px 10px;text-align:left;vertical-align:top}
.psb-cluster th{background:#f3f6fa;font-weight:600}
@media (max-width:767px){.psb-cluster table{display:block;overflow-x:auto}}
```

## Page copy

H1: Personal Services Business Risk for Incorporated Contractors in Ontario

Personal services business risk is the risk that CRA decides your Ontario corporation is really supplying an employee, you, to its client. If that happens, the corporation loses the small business deduction, pays 44.5% tax instead of 11.2%, and loses most of its expense deductions. The risk depends on how you actually work, not just the contract.

This page is for contractors who bill through their own corporation, often through a staffing agency, on a 35, 37.5 or 40 hour contract. The structure is legal and common. The question is whether CRA would see the work as a job billed through a corporation.

### H2: What is a personal services business?

Section 125(7) of the Income Tax Act defines a personal services business (PSB). It is a business of providing services where two things are true:

1. The individual who performs the services for the corporation, called the incorporated employee, or a person related to that individual, is a specified shareholder of the corporation.
2. The incorporated employee would reasonably be regarded as an officer or employee of the person or partnership receiving the services if the corporation did not exist.

The second point is the heart of it. CRA takes the corporation out of the picture and asks one question: would you simply be that client's employee?

#### H3: Who is the incorporated employee?

It is the person doing the work. If your corporation bills a bank for your time as a data engineer, you are the incorporated employee. The label names the person whose working relationship gets tested. It does not mean you are an employee.

#### H3: What is a specified shareholder?

A specified shareholder owns 10% or more of any class of the corporation's shares, directly or indirectly. Most contractors own all the shares of their corporation, so this part is usually met. Putting the shares in a spouse's name does not get around it, because the rule also covers persons related to you.

### H2: What are the two exceptions?

A business that meets the definition is still not a PSB if either exception applies:

- **More than five full-time employees.** The corporation employs more than five full-time employees in the business throughout the year. That means at least six, full-time, for the whole year. Whether owners or family members count depends on their real roles, so this needs review on your facts.
- **Services to an associated corporation.** The amount for the services is received from a corporation associated with yours in the year.

A one-person contractor corporation billing an agency or an end client usually fits neither exception. That is why the employee question matters so much.

### H2: What happens if CRA treats your corporation as a PSB?

Four things change at once:

- No small business deduction.
- No 13% federal general rate reduction.
- An additional 5% federal tax under section 123.5.
- Deductions limited by section 18(1)(p).

Federally, that is 38% less the 10% abatement, which leaves 28%, plus the extra 5%, for 33%. Add Ontario's 11.5% general rate and PSB income is taxed at a combined 44.5%.

#### H3: Which expenses can a PSB still deduct?

Section 18(1)(p) allows only:

- salary, wages or other remuneration paid in the year to the incorporated employee;
- the cost of benefits or allowances provided to the incorporated employee;
- certain amounts spent selling property or negotiating contracts, if an employee could deduct them; and
- legal expenses to collect amounts owed for the services.

Rent, software, equipment, travel, phone, insurance, accounting fees and similar costs are denied.

#### H3: How big is the gap between 11.2% and 44.5%?

Ontario cut its small business rate on July 1, 2026. The combined small business rate was 12.2% on income earned before that date and is 11.2% from that date. A December 31, 2026 year-end gets a day-weighted blended rate of 11.70% for 2026. I use the current 11.2% below.

Take a contractor billing $95 an hour for 37.5 hours a week over 48 weeks. That is $171,000 of revenue. Assume $9,000 of expenses, none of them an allowed type, no salary, and a full year at 11.2%.

| Item | Not a PSB | PSB |
|---|---|---|
| Combined Ontario corporate rate | 11.2% small business rate | 44.5% |
| Small business deduction | Yes, on up to $500,000 of active business income | No |
| 13% federal general rate reduction | Not needed, the small business rate applies | No |
| Additional 5% federal tax (section 123.5) | No | Yes |
| $9,000 of operating expenses | Deductible | Denied under 18(1)(p) |
| Income taxed | $162,000 | $171,000 |
| Corporate tax | $18,144 | $76,095 |
| Dividends paid from this income | Usually non-eligible, top 2026 Ontario rate 47.74% | Can be designated eligible, top 2026 Ontario rate 39.34% |

That is $57,951 more corporate tax in one year on the same work. Salary helps but does not fix it. If the corporation paid you a $120,000 salary, corporate tax would be $4,704 if it is not a PSB and $22,695 if it is, still $17,991 more. That leaves out employer CPP and the personal tax on the salary. Our [PSB tax cost calculator](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-tax-cost-calculator/) runs these numbers on your own figures.

A reassessment can also reach back to earlier years, when the rate was 12.2%. For 2025, with $159,000 of revenue and $9,000 of expenses, tax filed at $18,300 becomes $70,755 as a PSB. That is $52,455 more for one year, before arrears interest.

#### H3: What about dividends and GRIP?

PSB income is not eligible for the small business deduction, so it is taxed at the full rate and adds to the general rate income pool (GRIP). Dividends paid from it can be designated eligible dividends. That softens the hit but does not close the gap.

At top 2026 marginal rates, $100,000 of PSB income costs $44,500 in corporate tax. The remaining $55,500, paid as an eligible dividend at 39.34%, costs $21,833.70. Total tax is $66,333.70, or 66.33%. The same $100,000 paid to you as salary would cost $53,530 at 53.53%. The PSB route costs $12,803.70 more.

### H2: How does CRA decide whether you are really an employee?

CRA and the courts use the approach from Wiebe Door Services Ltd. v. M.N.R. and 671122 Ontario Ltd. v. Sagaz Industries Canada Inc. The central question is: whose business is it?

The main factors are:

- **Control:** who decides what work you do, and how, when and where you do it.
- **Tools and equipment:** who supplies the laptop, software, access and workspace.
- **Chance of profit and risk of loss:** whether you can earn more by working well, and whether you can lose money.
- **Integration:** whether your work is part of the client's business or part of your own.

Hiring helpers, your own investment and how you manage the work also count. No single factor decides the result.

CRA guide RC4110, Employee or Self-Employed?, sets out how CRA looks at a working relationship. Form CPT1 is used to request a CPP/EI ruling on a worker's status. A CPT1 ruling deals with CPP and EI. It is not a PSB ruling for income tax, though CRA looks at similar facts. We review whether a CPT1 request is useful in your facts.

#### H3: Does a 35, 37.5 or 40 hour contract make you an employee?

Not on its own. Fixed hours of 35, 37.5 or 40 a week, set schedules, timesheets approved by the end client, onsite or VPN-only rules and a client-supplied laptop are all control indicators. They add weight on the employee side. But hours alone never decide PSB status. CRA tests the whole relationship, year by year. I explain this in more detail on our page about [40 hour contract PSB risk](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/40-hour-contract-psb-risk/).

#### H3: How does a staffing agency change the picture?

Most contractors work in a three-party setup: your corporation, the staffing agency and the end client. CRA looks at whether you would reasonably be an employee of the party you really provide the services to. That can be the end client, not the agency. So the agency contract matters, and so do the end client's onboarding rules, security policies and day-to-day management.

### H2: Which contractors face the most personal services business risk?

The risk is highest where the setup looks most like a job: one main client, a long engagement, fixed hours, client equipment and a client manager. That pattern is common for IT and finance contractors placed with banks, insurers, governments and large enterprises. If that is you, please see our pages for an [accountant for IT contractors](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/accountant-for-it-contractors/) and an [accountant for finance contractors](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/accountant-for-finance-contractors/).

### H2: How do we help reduce personal services business risk?

No one can promise that CRA will accept your position. What we can do is help you reduce the risk, document the facts and support your position if CRA asks questions.

- **Review.** We review your contract and working facts as part of tax planning: control, equipment, profit and loss, integration, the agency structure and the hours clause. See how our [PSB contract review](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/contract-review-psb-indicators/) reads common clauses. We are accountants, not lawyers, so contract wording is for your lawyer.
- **Document.** We help you keep clear records of how you actually work: your own equipment, your investment, other clients and how you invoice. Records must reflect real facts.
- **Plan.** We set up payroll (salary, T4 slips and remittances), plan your salary and dividend mix, and handle your T2 corporate tax return, bookkeeping and GST/HST filings.
- **Support.** If CRA reviews your corporation, we act as its authorized representative. We respond to CRA requests and questionnaires, organize your documents, communicate with the auditor, and review and respond to proposal letters.

We work on a fixed flat-fee model and serve incorporated businesses only. When you are ready, [book a consultation](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/consultation/) and we will look at your facts.

## FAQs

#### Q: Is every incorporated contractor a personal services business?

No. A corporation carries on a PSB only if the person doing the work, or a related person, owns 10% or more of any class of its shares, and the person doing the work would reasonably be regarded as the client's employee if the corporation did not exist. Many contractors have facts on both sides, which is why each relationship needs review.

#### Q: How much more tax does a PSB pay in Ontario?

The combined rate on PSB income is 44.5%, compared with the current small business rate of 11.2%. On $150,000 of income kept in the corporation, that is $66,750 instead of $16,800, a difference of $49,950 in one year.

#### Q: Can a PSB still deduct its expenses?

Only a few. Section 18(1)(p) allows salary and benefits paid to the incorporated employee, certain costs of selling property or negotiating contracts that an employee could deduct, and legal costs to collect amounts owed for the services. Rent, software, equipment, travel, phone, insurance and accounting fees are denied.

#### Q: Does a 40 hour contract make my corporation a PSB?

Not by itself. Fixed hours, set schedules and client-approved timesheets point toward control, but hours alone never decide PSB status. CRA looks at the whole relationship, year by year.

#### Q: Will a CPT1 ruling settle my PSB status?

No. A CPT1 ruling deals with CPP and EI status. It is not a PSB ruling for income tax, although CRA looks at similar facts. We review whether a CPT1 request is useful in your facts.

#### Q: Can you make sure my corporation is never treated as a PSB?

No one can promise that. We help you reduce the risk by reviewing your contract and working facts as part of tax planning, documenting how you really work, and supporting your position if CRA asks questions.

#### Q: Do you work with contractors who are not incorporated?

No. For these services we work only with incorporated Canadian businesses. If you have not incorporated yet, we can help with incorporation first.

## Page copy

H1: Accountant for IT Contractors With an Ontario Corporation

An accountant for IT contractors looks after the corporation you bill through: T2 returns, bookkeeping, payroll, GST/HST and tax planning. In Ontario, the bigger issue is personal services business (PSB) risk. Income taxed at the 11.2% small business rate can be taxed at 44.5% if CRA treats your corporation as a PSB, and most expenses are denied.

### H2: Who do we work with?

We work with IT contractors who bill through their own Ontario corporation. That includes developers, DevOps engineers, data engineers, cloud architects, cybersecurity specialists, QA testers, business analysts and project managers.

Usually, the corporation signs with a staffing agency. The agency places the contractor with a bank, an insurer, a government ministry or a large enterprise. The contract usually runs on 35, 37.5 or 40 hours per week, billed by the hour and renewed when the project continues.

We work with incorporated Canadian businesses only, not individuals or sole proprietors. If you have not incorporated yet, we can help with that first.

### H2: What does an accountant for IT contractors do for your corporation?

The core work is what every corporation needs, done with an eye on how IT engagements are set up. We charge a fixed flat fee, so you know the cost before we start.

#### H3: Corporate tax return (T2)

We prepare and file your T2 each year. We claim the small business deduction where it is available, track capital cost allowance on your equipment, and look at the year's working facts before we file, not after.

#### H3: Bookkeeping

We keep your books current: agency payments, HST collected, expenses and shareholder loan activity. If CRA asks how your business runs, your records come first.

#### H3: Payroll for your salary

If you pay yourself a salary, we set up the payroll account, calculate and file the remittances, and prepare your T4 slip at year-end.

#### H3: GST/HST filings

Most IT corporations are registered for GST/HST and charge HST to the agency. We prepare and file the returns and reconcile them to your books.

#### H3: Tax planning, including salary and dividends

We plan how money leaves the corporation, how much stays in, and what that means for your personal tax. We review your contract and working facts as part of tax planning, because PSB risk changes what the right plan looks like.

#### H3: CRA audit support

If CRA writes to your corporation, we act as its authorized representative. We respond to CRA requests and questionnaires, organize your documents, communicate with the auditor, and review and respond to proposal letters.

### H2: Why is PSB risk higher for IT contractors?

Section 125(7) of the Income Tax Act sets out when a corporation carries on a personal services business. Two things must be true. First, the person who does the work (the "incorporated employee"), or a person related to them, is a specified shareholder. That means owning 10% or more of any class of shares, directly or indirectly. Second, that person would reasonably be regarded as an officer or employee of the client if the corporation did not exist.

A one-person IT corporation almost always meets the first part. The second part is where the facts matter.

In a three-party arrangement, CRA asks whether you would reasonably be an employee of the party you really provide the services to. That is often the end client, not the agency. If you are [contracting through a staffing agency](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/staffing-agency-contractor-tax/), both the agency contract and the end client's onboarding rules count.

There are two exceptions. The first is a corporation that employs more than five full-time employees in the business throughout the year, which means at least six. Whether owners or family members count depends on their real roles, so that needs review. The second is income received from a corporation associated with yours.

For the full rules and what they cost, please read our page on [personal services business risk](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-risk-incorporated-contractors/).

#### H3: What PSB indicators are typical in IT engagements?

CRA's guide RC4110 "Employee or Self-Employed?" sets out how CRA looks at a working relationship. The main factors come from the Wiebe Door and Sagaz decisions: control, ownership of tools, chance of profit and risk of loss, and integration. In IT work, the same facts come up often:

- Fixed hours, often 37.5 or 40 per week, with timesheets approved by the end client.
- Daily standups and sprint ceremonies run by the client's scrum master or manager.
- A client laptop, the client's VPN and a client email address.
- Work assigned through the client's ticketing system, and client access badges.
- On-call rotations set by the client's team lead.

None of these decides the result alone. Hours alone never decide PSB status, as we explain on our page about [40 hour contracts](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/40-hour-contract-psb-risk/). CRA tests the whole relationship, and it looks at each year on its own facts.

#### H3: How may CRA read common IT engagement facts?

| Common IT engagement fact | How CRA may read it | What we look at |
|---|---|---|
| Fixed 37.5 or 40 hours per week | Control: the client decides when you work | Whether hours are a billing cap or a set schedule |
| Client approves your timesheet | Control: the client supervises your time | Whether approval is a billing check or day-to-day supervision |
| Daily standups and sprint ceremonies run by the client | Control and integration: you work inside the client's team | Whether you deliver a defined scope or take tasks like any team member |
| Client laptop and VPN | Tools: the client supplies what you need | Whether the rule applies to every vendor, and which tools your corporation pays for |
| Tickets assigned in the client's system | Control: the client decides what you work on next | Whether the work is a defined deliverable or an open stream of tasks |
| Client access badge and email address | Integration: you look like staff | Whether you are set up as a vendor or as part of the team |
| On-call rotation set by the client | Control: the client sets your availability | Whether on-call work is agreed and priced as part of the contract |
| Paid by the hour with no fixed deliverable | Chance of profit and risk of loss: income tracks hours, like a wage | Whether your corporation carries real cost or risk if the work runs over |
| One client for the whole year | Integration: the work looks like a job | Other clients and work your corporation finds on its own |

### H2: Which expenses does an IT corporation normally deduct?

An IT corporation that is not a PSB deducts the normal costs of earning its income, such as:

- Laptop, monitors and other equipment, through capital cost allowance
- Software, cloud accounts and development tools
- A reasonable share of home office costs
- Phone and internet used for the business
- Professional development, such as certification courses and exams
- Accounting fees

#### H3: How does section 18(1)(p) change this for a PSB?

If your corporation is a PSB, section 18(1)(p) limits its deductions to a short list:

- Salary, wages or other remuneration paid in the year to the incorporated employee
- The cost of benefits or allowances provided to the incorporated employee
- Certain amounts spent selling property or negotiating contracts that an employee could deduct
- Legal expenses to collect amounts owed for the services

Equipment, software, home office, phone, training and accounting fees are all denied.

Here is one full taxation year at the current 11.2% rate. Revenue is $95 an hour x 37.5 hours x 48 weeks = $171,000. Expenses are $9,000. No salary is paid.

- Not a PSB: ($171,000 - $9,000) x 11.2% = $18,144
- PSB: expenses denied, so $171,000 x 44.5% = $76,095
- Extra tax: $57,951

The 11.2% rate applies to income earned from July 1, 2026. Income earned before that date was taxed at 12.2%, and a corporation with a December 31, 2026 year-end gets a blended 11.70%. To test your own numbers, please try our [PSB tax cost calculator](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-tax-cost-calculator/).

### H2: How do salary and dividends fit in?

Salary paid to you is one of the few deductions section 18(1)(p) still allows, so pay planning matters.

Take the same year, with a $120,000 salary paid to you:

- Not a PSB: ($171,000 - $9,000 - $120,000) = $42,000 x 11.2% = $4,704
- PSB: ($171,000 - $120,000) = $51,000 x 44.5% = $22,695
- Extra tax: $17,991

This leaves out employer CPP and the personal tax on the salary. Salary is not a way around the rules, and it has its own personal tax cost.

PSB income does not get the small business deduction, so it is taxed at the full rate and adds to the general rate income pool (GRIP). Dividends paid from it can be designated eligible dividends. Integration still leaves a gap. At top 2026 rates, $100,000 of PSB income costs $44,500 in corporate tax, and the $55,500 eligible dividend costs $21,833.70 in personal tax, for a total of $66,333.70. The same $100,000 paid as salary costs $53,530. The PSB route costs $12,803.70 more.

We cover pay mix in more detail on our page about [salary vs dividend for incorporated contractors](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/salary-vs-dividend-incorporated-contractor/).

### H2: How do we work with IT contractor corporations?

We keep the process plain:

1. We review your contract, the agency agreement and how the work runs day to day, as part of tax planning.
2. We set up your books, payroll and HST.
3. We plan salary and dividends for the year with PSB risk in view.
4. We file your T2 and look at the facts again each year, because a new contract or renewal can change the picture.

We are accountants, not lawyers. Contract wording is for your lawyer. We help you document the facts, reduce risk where the facts allow it, and support your position if CRA asks questions. No one can promise how CRA will view a file.

I lead the firm as a CPA (Canada and USA). Our team includes a second designated CPA (CPA Ontario) and bookkeeping support staff. We have 1300+ five-star Google reviews and are a 2026 Xero Awarded Firm.

Please [book a consultation](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/consultation/) and bring your current contract, the agency agreement and your last T2 if you have one. You can also call 647-212-9559 or email info@gondaliyacpa.ca. We are open 9AM to 8:30PM, Monday to Sunday, at 55 Queen St E, Suite 1205, Toronto.

## FAQs

#### Q: Do you work with IT contractors who are not incorporated?

No. For these services we work with incorporated Canadian businesses only. We do not take individuals or sole proprietors as clients. If you plan to incorporate, we can help set up the corporation first.

#### Q: Does a 37.5 hour contract make my corporation a PSB?

Not by itself. Fixed hours are a control indicator, but hours alone never decide PSB status. CRA looks at the whole relationship, including tools, chance of profit and risk of loss, and integration, and it looks at each year on its own facts.

#### Q: Can my corporation still deduct my laptop if it is a PSB?

No. Section 18(1)(p) limits a PSB's deductions to salary, wages and benefits for the incorporated employee, certain costs of selling property or negotiating contracts that an employee could deduct, and legal costs to collect amounts owed. Equipment, software, home office, phone and training costs are denied.

#### Q: Does billing through a staffing agency protect me from the PSB rules?

No. In a three-party arrangement, CRA asks whether you would reasonably be an employee of the party you really provide the services to, which is often the end client. The agency contract and the end client's onboarding rules both matter.

#### Q: What tax rate applies to my IT corporation in 2026?

If it is not a PSB, the combined Ontario small business rate on the first $500,000 of active business income is 11.2% from July 1, 2026, and was 12.2% before that date. A December 31, 2026 year-end gets a blended 11.70%. If it is a PSB, the combined rate is 44.5%.

#### Q: Will you review my contract?

We review your contract and working facts as part of tax planning, looking for PSB indicators. We are accountants, not lawyers, so we do not draft or change contract wording. That is for your lawyer.

#### Q: What happens if CRA asks questions about my corporation?

We act as your corporation's authorized representative. We respond to CRA requests and questionnaires, organize your documents, communicate with the auditor, and review and respond to proposal letters. Please contact us as soon as a letter arrives.

## Page copy

H1: Accountant for Finance Contractors Who Bill Through Their Own Corporation

An accountant for finance contractors looks after the corporation you bill through: the T2 return, bookkeeping, payroll, GST/HST and tax planning. As part of tax planning, we also review your contract and working facts for personal services business (PSB) risk. In Ontario, PSB income is taxed at 44.5% instead of the current 11.2% small business rate.

### H2: Who do we work with?

I work with finance professionals who bill through their own Ontario corporation. Most are FP&A analysts and managers, accountants, controllers, and risk, audit and compliance specialists. Many contract through a staffing agency to a bank, an insurer, a government or a large enterprise. The contract usually sets a fixed week of 37.5 or 40 hours.

You close books, build forecasts, test controls or review regulatory reports for a living. Our job is to keep your corporation compliant, plan how you pay yourself, and be straight with you about PSB risk.

We work with incorporated Canadian businesses only. If you are not yet incorporated, we can set up the corporation first.

### H2: What does an accountant for finance contractors do for you?

Everything below is work we already do for incorporated clients.

#### H3: Corporate tax returns and bookkeeping

We prepare and file your corporation's T2 return each year. We keep the books current, so invoices, agency payments, expenses and shareholder transactions are recorded properly. If CRA ever asks how your business runs, the records are the first thing it sees.

#### H3: Payroll and salary vs dividend planning

We set up salary if you want it, prepare T4 slips and handle remittances. Each year, we plan the mix of salary and dividends. Salary paid to you stays deductible even if the corporation is a PSB, so the pay mix also affects your exposure. You can read more on [salary vs dividend planning for contractors](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/salary-vs-dividend-incorporated-contractor/).

#### H3: GST/HST filings

Once your corporation is registered, it charges HST on its invoices to the agency or client. We prepare and file the GST/HST returns.

#### H3: Tax planning, including a review of your working facts

We review your contract and working facts as part of tax planning. We read the agency contract, the statement of work and the end client's onboarding rules. We ask how the work actually happens. Then we tell you which facts point toward employment, which point the other way, and what to document. The detail is on our [PSB contract review](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/contract-review-psb-indicators/) page.

We are accountants, not lawyers. If a clause needs new wording, that is for your lawyer.

#### H3: CRA audit support and representation

If CRA reviews your corporation, you can authorize us as its representative. We then respond to CRA requests and questionnaires, organize your documents, deal with the auditor directly, and review and respond to proposal letters. See [CRA PSB review support](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/cra-psb-review-support/) for what that process looks like.

### H2: Why do finance roles at banks and insurers carry PSB risk?

Under section 125(7) of the Income Tax Act, your corporation carries on a PSB when two things are true. First, you or a person related to you is a specified shareholder, which means owning 10% or more of any class of shares, directly or indirectly. Second, you would reasonably be regarded as an officer or employee of the client receiving the services if the corporation did not exist.

Banks, insurers and governments run on finance, risk, audit and compliance. These are core functions, not side projects. The tests from Wiebe Door Services Ltd. v. M.N.R. and 671122 Ontario Ltd. v. Sagaz Industries Canada Inc. ask whose business it is. When your work is part of how the bank closes its books or meets its reporting duties, that points toward integration with the bank's business.

Regulated employers also run tight controls. They decide who gets access, who approves what and when work is due. Many of those rules exist for regulatory reasons and apply to every outside firm. They still show control, and CRA weighs them with everything else.

The agency in the middle does not settle it. CRA looks at whether you would reasonably be an employee of the party your services are really provided to, which is usually the end client. I cover the full rules, including the two exceptions, on our page about [PSB risk for incorporated contractors](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-risk-incorporated-contractors/). Agency setups are covered on our [staffing agency contractor tax](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/staffing-agency-contractor-tax/) page.

### H2: Which PSB indicators show up in finance engagements?

These are the facts I see most often in finance engagements.

#### H3: Background checks, onboarding and the code of conduct

Regulated employers screen everyone who gets access, including outside firms. A background check on its own is a weak signal. Staff-style onboarding is stronger: employee orientation, the employee handbook, and agreeing to the client's code of conduct and policies on the same terms as staff.

#### H3: Badges, system entitlements and the org chart

An access badge, a client email address and entitlements in the general ledger or reporting tools are normal for this work. Together, they can make you look like staff. Appearing on the client's org chart, with a reporting line to a client manager, points to integration and supervision.

#### H3: Sign-off authority and reviewing client staff

Many controller and senior accountant engagements carry approval or sign-off authority over journal entries, reconciliations or control tests. Some include reviewing or supervising the client's own staff. Both put you inside the client's management and control structure.

#### H3: Month-end calendars and fixed hours

The client sets the close calendar. It decides what you work on and when. A fixed 37.5 or 40 hour week, timesheets approved by a client manager and set onsite days add to the control picture. Hours alone never decide PSB status. The whole relationship is tested, year by year.

#### H3: How CRA may read finance engagement facts

| Finance engagement fact | How CRA may read it | What we note on file |
|---|---|---|
| Client background check and onboarding | Weak on its own if every outside firm goes through it; stronger if onboarding mirrors new staff | Whether the same process applies to all vendors |
| Client code of conduct and policies | Control, if you are bound and managed like staff | Whether it is a regulatory condition for every vendor |
| Access badge, client email and system entitlements | Control and integration, because you look and work like a team member | The security reason, and which tools your corporation provides |
| Name on the client org chart | Integration into the client's structure and reporting lines | Who you report to, and for what |
| Approval or sign-off authority | Strong integration, because you act inside the client's control framework | What you sign, under whose authority, and whether it is in your scope |
| Supervising or reviewing client staff | Strong control and integration, because it is a management role in the client's business | Whether the review is a deliverable or an ongoing staff duty |
| Month-end calendar set by the client | Control over when and what you work on | Whether deadlines tie to deliverables in the statement of work |
| Fixed 37.5 or 40 hour week | Control over how much you work, never decisive alone | Whether the hours are a billing cap or a required schedule |
| Engagement covers a staff seat, such as a leave | Integration, because you do an employee's job | The scope, the end date and any project deliverables |

One row does not decide anything. CRA weighs the whole relationship, and so do we.

### H2: What does PSB status cost an Ontario corporation?

The tax gap is large. Take $150,000 of income kept in an Ontario corporation. At the current 11.2% small business rate, corporate tax is $16,800. As a PSB at 44.5%, it is $66,750. That is $49,950 more for each year the treatment applies.

The 44.5% is 33% federal, which includes the additional 5% tax under section 123.5, plus the 11.5% Ontario general rate. The 11.2% rate applies to income earned from July 1, 2026. Income earned before that date was taxed at 12.2%, so a December 31, 2026 year-end gets a blended 11.70%, or $17,543.84 on the same $150,000.

A PSB also loses most deductions. Section 18(1)(p) allows only salary and other pay to you, the cost of your benefits or allowances, certain costs of selling property or negotiating contracts that an employee could deduct, and legal costs to collect fees owed for your services. Rent, software, equipment, travel, phone, insurance and accounting fees are denied.

### H2: What can you do to support your position?

Nothing here promises a result. These steps help you show the facts as they are.

- Keep the agency contract, the statement of work, every renewal and the client's onboarding rules.
- Note the reason for each security or policy requirement.
- Where you can, scope work by deliverables, such as a model, a process or a remediation project, rather than an open-ended seat.
- Pay for your own equipment, software, training and professional liability insurance where the client allows it.
- Keep records of other clients, proposals and marketing.
- Describe the work delivered on your invoices, not only the hours.
- Review the facts each year, especially after a renewal, a new manager or a change in duties.

Two CRA documents come up often. Guide RC4110, "Employee or Self-Employed?", explains how CRA looks at a working relationship. Form CPT1 is the request for a CPP and EI ruling on a worker's status. That ruling covers CPP and EI only and is not a PSB ruling for income tax, even though CRA looks at similar facts. We review whether a request is useful in your facts.

If you are an incorporated finance contractor and want a clear view of your corporation's tax position and PSB risk, please [book a consultation](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/consultation/). Please bring your agency contract, your statement of work and the client's onboarding rules. You can also call us at 647-212-9559 or email info@gondaliyacpa.ca.

## FAQs

#### Q: What does an accountant for finance contractors do?

We look after the corporation you bill through: the T2 return, bookkeeping, payroll, GST/HST filings and tax planning, including salary and dividend planning. As part of tax planning, we review your contract and working facts for PSB risk.

#### Q: Does working through a staffing agency protect me from the PSB rules?

Not on its own. In a three-party arrangement, CRA looks at whether you would reasonably be an employee of the party your services are really provided to, which is usually the bank, insurer or government. The agency contract and the client's onboarding rules both matter.

#### Q: Does sign-off authority at the client increase PSB risk?

It can. Approving journal entries, reconciliations or control tests for the client, or reviewing its staff, puts you inside the client's control structure. CRA may read that as integration and control. We note what you sign and whether it is part of a defined scope.

#### Q: Is a fixed 37.5 or 40 hour week enough to make my corporation a PSB?

No. Fixed hours are a control indicator, but hours alone never decide PSB status. CRA looks at the whole relationship, including control, tools, chance of profit and risk of loss, and integration, year by year.

#### Q: What is the tax rate if my corporation is a PSB in Ontario?

The combined rate is 44.5%, compared with the 11.2% small business rate that applies from July 1, 2026. On $150,000 of corporate income, that is $66,750 instead of $16,800. Most deductions are also denied under section 18(1)(p).

#### Q: Can you rewrite my contract to lower PSB risk?

We are accountants, not lawyers, so contract wording is for your lawyer. We review your contract and working facts as part of tax planning and tell you which facts to document.

#### Q: Do you work with finance contractors who are not incorporated?

We work with incorporated Canadian businesses only. If you are not yet incorporated, we can set up your corporation, and then look after its T2 return, bookkeeping, payroll, GST/HST and tax planning.

#### Q: What happens if CRA reviews my corporation for PSB status?

We step in as your corporation's authorized representative. We answer CRA's requests and questionnaires, put your documents in order, communicate with the auditor, and review and respond to any proposal letter.

## Page copy

H1: 40 Hour Contract PSB Risk: What Fixed Hours Mean for Your Corporation

Here is the short answer on 40 hour contract PSB risk. A 35, 37.5 or 40 hour contract does not by itself make your Ontario corporation a personal services business (PSB). Fixed hours are one sign of control. CRA tests the whole relationship, year by year. PSB income is taxed at 44.5% instead of the current 11.2% small business rate.

### H2: Why does a fixed-hours clause matter for PSB risk?

Most contractors I work with bill a bank, insurer, government or large enterprise through their own Ontario corporation, often via a staffing agency, on 35, 37.5 or 40 hours a week. The question I hear most: does that number put my corporation at risk?

The number does not decide it. It matters because of how the Income Tax Act defines a PSB.

Under section 125(7), your corporation carries on a PSB in a year when two things are true and no exception applies. First, you (the person who does the work) or a person related to you is a specified shareholder. That means owning 10% or more of any class of shares, directly or indirectly. Second, you would reasonably be regarded as an officer or employee of the client if your corporation did not exist.

The second part is where hours come in. CRA answers it with the tests the courts use to tell an employee from a contractor, and control is one of them. I explain the full set of rules, including the two exceptions, on our page about [personal services business risk](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-risk-incorporated-contractors/).

### H2: What does a 35, 37.5 or 40 hour clause signal about control?

A fixed number of hours is a control indicator, but it can also be a billing term: how much time the client is buying. What sits around it decides how much weight it carries.

#### H3: Fixed hours and set schedules

A clause that caps billable hours at 37.5 a week is mainly a budget limit. A clause that says you must work 40 hours, at set start and finish times, on days the client picks, looks more like a staff schedule. The second version points to the client controlling when and how much you work.

#### H3: Timesheet approval by the end client

Almost every agency contract needs a timesheet. If the agency uses it only to bill the client, it is an invoicing step. If an end client manager approves your hours, approves your time off and reviews your work the way they review staff, it signals supervision.

#### H3: Onsite days, VPN-only access and client laptops

Banks, insurers and governments often require onsite days, VPN-only access and a client-supplied laptop. These are control indicators: the client decides where you work and with which tools. Many are security rules for every outside firm, and I note that, because it can read differently from a manager directing your day.

#### H3: Hours clauses compared

Here is how I look at the hours-related clauses I see most often.

| Hours-related clause | What it signals about control | What to document |
|---|---|---|
| Fixed 35, 37.5 or 40 hours a week | The client sets how much you work, like a staff position | Whether it is a billing cap or a required schedule, and who sets when hours are worked |
| Set start and finish times or core hours | The client sets when you work | The flexibility you actually use, and whether the times tie to team meetings or to deliverables |
| Timesheet approval by an end client manager | The client checks your time the way it checks staff time | Who approves, and whether approval is only for billing or also judges your performance |
| Extra hours need client approval | The client controls the amount of work | How extra hours are requested and billed, and who decides they are needed |
| Onsite days or VPN-only access | The client controls where and how you work | The security or regulatory reason, and whether it applies to all outside firms |
| Client-supplied laptop and accounts | The client owns the tools | The security reason, plus the equipment, software and licences your corporation pays for |
| Time off needs client approval | Close to staff leave approval | Whether you give notice for planning, or ask for permission |

One row does not decide anything.

### H2: Do hours alone decide PSB status?

No. Hours alone never decide PSB status. The courts set out the approach in Wiebe Door Services Ltd. v. M.N.R. and 671122 Ontario Ltd. v. Sagaz Industries Canada Inc. The central question is whose business it is. Are you in business on your own account, or working inside the client's business?

The tests look at four main factors:

- Control over the work and how it is done.
- Ownership of tools and equipment.
- Chance of profit and risk of loss.
- Integration, meaning whether the work is part of the client's business or your own business.

Hiring helpers, investment and management also count. Calling yourself an "independent contractor" in the contract does not settle it.

The test also runs year by year. Each taxation year of your corporation stands on its own facts. A renewal that moves you onsite five days a week, or a new manager who sets your daily tasks, can change the picture for that year.

### H2: Which facts can weigh the other way?

A 40 hour week can sit inside a relationship that still looks like a business. These facts can weigh against employee status. None of them settles the question on its own, and none of them promises a result.

- **Scope defined by deliverables.** A statement of work with clear outputs, milestones and acceptance terms, rather than an open-ended role.
- **Your own tools.** Equipment, software, licences, training and a workspace your corporation pays for and uses for the work.
- **Other clients.** Work for more than one client in the year, or a real effort to market your corporation to others.
- **A right to send a substitute.** A real right to send another qualified person, subject to the client's security checks, that works in practice and not only on paper.
- **Real financial risk.** Unpaid rework, fixed-price pieces of work, your own professional liability insurance, unpaid gaps between contracts, and costs you carry whether or not you bill.

I weigh these alongside the hours. A deliverables-based scope with a 40 hour cap can read very differently from an open-ended, staff-style role at 35 hours.

### H2: What is the tax difference if your corporation is a PSB?

One number shows the cost. On $150,000 of income kept in an Ontario corporation, tax at the current 11.2% small business rate is $16,800. At the 44.5% PSB rate, it is $66,750. The difference, $49,950, applies to every year CRA treats as PSB income.

Why 44.5%? Federal tax on PSB income is 33%, because there is no small business deduction, no 13% general rate reduction and an additional 5% tax under section 123.5. Ontario adds its 11.5% general rate. On the other side, 11.2% is the rate for income earned from July 1, 2026. Before that date it was 12.2%, so a December 31, 2026 year-end pays a blended 11.70% for 2026, which is $17,543.84 on the same $150,000.

Deductions shrink too. Under section 18(1)(p), a PSB can deduct only salary and other pay to you, the cost of benefits or allowances provided to you, certain costs of selling property or negotiating contracts that an employee could deduct, and legal costs to collect amounts owed for your services. Rent, software, equipment, travel, phone, insurance and accounting fees are denied. You can test your own figures with our [PSB tax cost calculator](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-tax-cost-calculator/).

### H2: What should you keep on file each year?

Records do not change the facts, but they let you show them. Each year, I suggest keeping:

- The agency contract, the statement of work, and every extension or renewal.
- The end client's onboarding rules, security rules and equipment policy.
- A short note on why the hours are fixed, for example a budget cap or a release schedule.
- Invoices that describe deliverables, not only hours.
- Receipts for equipment, software, training and insurance your corporation pays for.
- Contracts, proposals or marketing for other clients.

In an agency setup, CRA looks at whether you would reasonably be an employee of the party your services are really provided to, usually the end client. So the agency contract and the end client's onboarding rules both matter.

### H2: How do we review 40 hour contract PSB risk as part of tax planning?

We review your contract and working facts as part of tax planning. In practice:

1. We read the agency contract, the statement of work and the end client's onboarding rules.
2. We ask how the work happens day to day: who assigns tasks, who approves time, where you work and what you own.
3. We map those facts against the control, tools, profit and loss, and integration factors.
4. We look at your pay mix. Salary paid to you stays deductible even in a PSB, so salary and dividend planning matters.
5. We tell you which facts to document and where your file is weakest.

For background, CRA's guide RC4110, "Employee or Self-Employed?", explains how CRA looks at a working relationship. Form CPT1 asks CRA for a CPP and EI ruling on a worker's status. That ruling does not decide PSB status for income tax, although CRA looks at similar facts, so we review whether a request is useful in your facts.

We are accountants, not lawyers, so new contract wording is a job for your lawyer. To see how specific clauses are read, please visit our page on [PSB contract review](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/contract-review-psb-indicators/). You can also see how we work with IT contractors on our [accountant for IT contractors](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/accountant-for-it-contractors/) page, and with FP&A, controllership, risk and audit contractors on our [accountant for finance contractors](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/accountant-for-finance-contractors/) page.

Our team includes a second designated CPA (CPA Ontario) and bookkeeping support staff. We serve incorporated Canadian businesses only, on a fixed flat-fee model.

[TRUSTINDEX WIDGET]

If your contract says 35, 37.5 or 40 hours and you want to know how it reads, please [book a consultation](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/consultation/). Please bring your agency contract, your statement of work and the end client's onboarding rules. You can also call us at 647-212-9559, 9AM to 8:30PM, Monday to Sunday.

## FAQs

#### Q: Does a 40 hour contract make my corporation a personal services business?

Not by itself. A 40 hour week is one control fact. CRA looks at the whole relationship, including control, tools, chance of profit and risk of loss, and integration, and asks whose business it is.

#### Q: Is a 37.5 hour contract lower risk than a 40 hour contract?

Not on its own. The difference in hours is small. What matters more is whether the hours are a billing cap or a required schedule, and what the rest of the relationship looks like.

#### Q: Does timesheet approval by the end client add to PSB risk?

It can. If an end client manager approves your time and supervises your work like staff, it points to control. If the timesheet is only used by the agency for billing, it carries less weight. We note who approves and why.

#### Q: I must use the client's laptop and VPN. Is that a problem?

It is a control indicator, because the client decides your tools and access. Banks, insurers and governments often require this from every outside firm for security reasons. We document the reason and the tools your corporation does pay for.

#### Q: If CRA finds my corporation is a PSB, what rate applies?

PSB income is taxed at a combined 44.5% in Ontario. The current small business rate, from July 1, 2026, is 11.2%. On $150,000 kept in the corporation, that means $66,750 of tax instead of $16,800. Section 18(1)(p) also denies most expenses.

#### Q: Is PSB status decided once for the whole contract?

No. It is tested year by year, for each taxation year of your corporation. A renewal, a new manager or a move to full-time onsite work can change the facts for that year.

#### Q: Can you change the hours clause in my contract?

No. We are accountants, not lawyers, so any change to contract wording is for your lawyer. As part of tax planning, we review the hours clause and how you actually work, and tell you which facts to document.

#### Q: Should I file Form CPT1 to get a ruling?

A CPT1 ruling deals with CPP and EI status, not PSB status for income tax, though CRA looks at similar facts. We review whether it is useful in your facts before you decide.

## Page copy

H1: PSB Contract Review: How We Read Your Contract for PSB Indicators

A PSB contract review is how we read your agency agreement, statement of work and end-client onboarding papers for signs that CRA could treat your Ontario corporation as a personal services business. We then compare the paper to how you really work. The rate gap is wide: 11.2% on small business income versus 44.5% on PSB income.

### H2: What is a PSB contract review?

I start with the contract, because CRA can ask for it in a PSB review. A PSB contract review is not a separate service we sell. We review your contract and working facts as part of the tax planning we already do for your corporation.

The question behind it comes from section 125(7) of the Income Tax Act. Your corporation earns PSB income if you, the person doing the work, or a person related to you, own 10% or more of any class of its shares, directly or indirectly, and you would reasonably be regarded as an officer or employee of the client if your corporation did not exist. If your corporation has no other employees and bills an agency it is not associated with, neither exception in the definition applies. So the answer turns on the working relationship. Our page on [personal services business risk](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-risk-incorporated-contractors/) explains the rules in full.

The cost is why the review matters. In Ontario, income eligible for the small business deduction is taxed at a combined 11.2% from July 1, 2026. PSB income is taxed at 44.5%. On $150,000 kept in the corporation, that is $16,800 versus $66,750, a difference of $49,950. For a December 31, 2026 year-end, the small business rate is a blended 11.70%, because income earned before July 1, 2026 was taxed at 12.2%. PSB status also limits deductions under section 18(1)(p) to salary, benefits and allowances for you, certain costs of selling property or negotiating contracts that an employee could deduct, and legal costs to collect fees owed.

### H2: Which documents do we read?

Many incorporated contractors work in a three-party setup: their corporation, a staffing agency and an end client. CRA looks at whether you would reasonably be an employee of the party you really provide the services to. That can be the end client, even though the agency pays your invoices. So we read every layer of paper. Our page on [agency contracts](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/staffing-agency-contractor-tax/) explains the three-party setup in more detail.

#### H3: The agency agreement

This is the contract between your corporation and the agency. It sets the rate, the term, termination rights, insurance and indemnity terms, and often limits on working for the end client directly. Many agency agreements also pass the end client's rules down to you.

#### H3: The statement of work

The statement of work, or work order, describes the assignment: the role, the dates, the hours, the location, the manager you report to and sometimes the deliverables. It often says more about control than the master agreement does.

#### H3: End-client onboarding documents

Banks, insurers and government clients often send their own package: a code of conduct, IT security and acceptable use rules, a badge request, required training and sometimes a background check. You may sign some of these in your own name. They show how the client actually treats you.

### H2: What do we look for in each clause?

I read each clause for what it can indicate about the factors from Wiebe Door Services Ltd. v. M.N.R. and 671122 Ontario Ltd. v. Sagaz Industries Canada Inc.: control, ownership of tools and equipment, chance of profit and risk of loss, and integration. The central question is whose business it is. No single clause decides that.

| Clause | What it can indicate | What facts we ask about |
|---|---|---|
| Parties | Who the services are really provided to, and whether your corporation is the contracting party | Who signs each document, who you deal with day to day |
| Term and renewals | An open-ended role renewed again and again can point to integration | How long you have been there, how many renewals, whether the scope changed |
| Rate basis | Hourly pay with no downside suggests little chance of profit or risk of loss | Who set the rate, whether you carry rework, unpaid time or bad debt |
| Hours and schedule | Fixed hours and set core hours can point to control | Who sets your schedule in practice, how time off is handled |
| Location and remote access | Required onsite days or VPN-only access can point to control | Where you actually work, whether the rule applies to every vendor |
| Equipment | A client laptop and client software point to the client's tools | What your corporation owns and pays for, including software licences |
| Supervision and reporting line | A client manager who directs how you work points to control and integration | Whether the manager sets methods or only signs off results |
| Timesheet approval | End-client approval can point to control over your time | Whether approval only verifies billing or is used to manage you |
| Termination | One-way termination at will, with no fee, can point to control | Whether rights are mutual, whether work in progress is paid |
| Substitution and helpers | A personal services only clause points away from your own business | Whether you could send a substitute or hire help, and whether you have |
| Exclusivity and non-solicit | A non-compete limits your chance of profit from other clients | Other clients this year, how you market your corporation |
| Insurance and indemnity | Carrying your own liability insurance and risk for errors can weigh toward a business | Whether you hold the policy, who pays, any cost of fixing work |
| Client policies and training | Staff-style training, reviews and policies can point to control and integration | Whether the rules apply to all vendors or only to staff, any client title or email |

#### H3: Hours, schedule and timesheets

IT and finance contracts usually fix 35, 37.5 or 40 hours a week, and the end client approves the timesheet. These are control indicators, but hours alone never decide PSB status. We look at who really sets your schedule. Our page on [40 hour contract PSB risk](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/40-hour-contract-psb-risk/) covers the hours clause in detail.

#### H3: Substitution, exclusivity and termination

A clause that says only you can do the work matters, because personal service is a feature of employment. A business can usually send someone else or hire help. A non-compete that stops you serving other clients matters more than a narrow non-solicit. Termination terms are a weaker sign on their own, but a one-way right to end the contract at will, with no fee, fits an employment pattern more than a deal between two businesses.

#### H3: Insurance, indemnity and client policies

If your corporation must carry its own liability insurance and bear the cost of its own errors, that can weigh toward a business, but only if you actually hold the policy. Client policies that apply to every vendor for security or regulatory reasons carry less weight than rules that treat you like staff.

### H2: Why is the paper only half of the review?

CRA does not stop at the contract. It tests what actually happens, day to day and year by year. A clause that lets you send a substitute carries little weight if the client would never accept one. A clause that says you supply your own equipment means little if you only ever work on a client laptop. The reverse is also true: a contract can carry staff-style wording that does not match how you work.

The CRA guide RC4110, Employee or Self-Employed?, describes this approach. CRA looks at what the parties intended, then checks that against how the relationship really works. So after reading the paper, I ask about the working facts:

- Who decides what you work on each day, and how you do it?
- Who sets your hours, and how is time off handled?
- What does your corporation own and pay for to do the work?
- Have you had other clients, or marketed your corporation, this year?
- Have you ever fixed work at your own cost, or gone unpaid?
- Are you on the client's org chart with a client email and title?

Where the paper and the facts point in different directions, the facts usually carry more weight.

### H2: What do we do with what we find?

We go through the results with you as part of your tax planning. Depending on your facts, that can include:

- Listing the clauses that point toward employment and the facts that weigh the other way.
- Suggesting records that support your position, such as invoices to other clients, receipts for your own equipment and software licences, and your insurance policy.
- Planning your salary and dividend mix with PSB risk in mind, since salary paid to you stays deductible even if CRA treats the income as PSB income.
- Noting clauses you may want to raise with your lawyer and the agency at renewal.

We are accountants, not lawyers. We do not draft or rewrite contract wording, and we do not give legal opinions. Any change in wording is for your lawyer and the agency to agree. Our role is to show you the tax side of each clause, so you can have that conversation with the facts in hand.

PSB status is tested year by year, so when your contract renews or changes, please send us the new version. If CRA does contact you, we already know your contract and facts. Our [CRA PSB review](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/cra-psb-review-support/) page explains how our CRA audit support and representation works in that case.

### H2: Who do we do this for?

We work with incorporated IT contractors, such as developers, DevOps, data, cloud, cybersecurity, QA, BA and PM roles, and finance contractors in FP&A, accounting, controllership, risk, audit and compliance. Many bill through an agency to banks, insurers, governments and large enterprises. We work only with incorporated Canadian businesses, not individuals or sole proprietors. If you are in the GTA, see our page for an [incorporated contractor accountant in Toronto](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/incorporated-contractor-accountant-toronto/).

[TRUSTINDEX WIDGET]

When you book, please have your agency agreement, statement of work and onboarding package ready. [Book a consultation](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/consultation/) and we will go through them as part of your tax planning. You can also call 647-212-9559 or email info@gondaliyacpa.ca. We are open 9AM to 8:30PM, Monday to Sunday.

## FAQs

#### Q: Is a PSB contract review a separate service?

No. We review your contract and working facts as part of the tax planning work we do for your corporation. It is not sold or priced on its own.

#### Q: Can a contract review tell me for certain whether my corporation is a PSB?

No. A review shows which clauses and facts point toward employment and which weigh the other way. CRA weighs the whole relationship, year by year, and how it works in practice. A review helps you reduce risk and document your position. It cannot settle the question.

#### Q: Will you rewrite my contract to reduce PSB risk?

No. We are accountants, not lawyers, and we do not draft contract wording. We explain the tax side of each clause. Any change in wording is for your lawyer and the agency to agree.

#### Q: Does the hours clause in my contract make my corporation a PSB?

Not on its own. Fixed 35, 37.5 or 40 hour weeks, set schedules and timesheet approval by the end client are control indicators, but hours alone never decide PSB status. CRA looks at all the factors together.

#### Q: My contract is with the agency. Do the end client's documents matter?

Yes. In a three-party setup, CRA looks at whether you would reasonably be an employee of the party you really provide the services to. The agency agreement and the end client's onboarding rules both matter.

#### Q: Which documents should I bring?

Your agency agreement, every statement of work and renewal, the end client's onboarding package, and a few recent invoices. If your corporation has other clients, its own equipment or its own insurance, bring proof of those too.

#### Q: Should I file a CPT1 to get a ruling on my status?

Form CPT1 is used to request a CPP and EI ruling on a worker's status. It is not a PSB ruling for income tax, although CRA looks at similar facts. If you are thinking about one, we review whether it is useful in your facts.

#### Q: How often should my contract be reviewed?

PSB status is tested year by year, so the review should follow your contract. When it renews, changes scope or moves you to a new end client, we look at it again as part of your tax planning.
