## Page copy

H1: Staffing Agency Contractor Tax for Incorporated Contractors in Ontario

Staffing agency contractor tax in Ontario works like this: your corporation bills the agency, charges 13% HST, and pays tax at the 11.2% small business rate on its active business income. The agency in the middle does not settle PSB status. CRA looks at who really receives your services, usually the end client. A PSB finding means 44.5%.

Many contractors who bill through an agency never sign anything with the bank, insurer or government department where they sit every day. That middle layer changes the paperwork, the invoicing and the HST. It does not change the question CRA asks about personal services business (PSB) status.

### H2: How does staffing agency contractor tax work in a three-party structure?

There are three parties and usually two contracts.

- **Your corporation.** It provides the services. You are its shareholder, its director and the person who does the work.
- **The staffing agency.** It holds the supplier agreement with the end client and signs a separate contract with your corporation, often with a statement of work for each assignment.
- **The end client.** The bank, insurer, government body or large enterprise where the work happens. It usually has no contract with your corporation at all.

Money flows one way: end client to agency, agency to your corporation, and your corporation to you through salary or dividends. Direction of the work often flows the other way. The end client's managers assign tasks, set priorities and approve your hours. That matters later.

#### H3: What paperwork will the agency ask for?

Before the first timesheet, most agencies ask your corporation for:

1. **Proof of incorporation.** Your certificate and articles of incorporation.
2. **Business number.** The business number CRA assigned to your corporation.
3. **HST number.** Your GST/HST account number. Many agencies will not pay HST without it.
4. **Insurance.** A certificate of insurance, usually commercial general liability and often professional liability (errors and omissions).
5. **Banking details.** A void cheque or direct deposit form in the corporation's name, not yours.

Check that the agency contract names your corporation, not you, as the contracting party. Contract wording questions go to your lawyer, and insurance questions go to your broker.

### H2: How do invoicing and payment work with an agency?

The usual cycle looks like this:

1. You enter your hours in the agency's or the end client's timesheet system.
2. A manager at the end client approves them.
3. Your corporation invoices the agency, or the agency's portal builds the invoice from the approved hours.
4. The agency pays your corporation your hourly rate plus HST, on the payment terms in your contract.
5. Your corporation pays you salary or dividends and remits the HST to CRA.

Your invoice should show your corporation's legal name, HST number, period, hours, rate and HST charged. Here is a two-week invoice at 37.5 hours a week and $95 an hour:

| Invoice line | Amount |
|---|---|
| 75 hours x $95 | $7,125.00 |
| HST at 13% | $926.25 |
| Invoice total | $8,051.25 |

Over a year of 48 weeks, that is $171,000 of fees and $22,230 of HST, or $193,230 deposited. The $22,230 is not your money. It belongs to CRA, less input tax credits on HST your corporation paid on business costs. I suggest moving it to a separate account when it lands.

The agency does not deduct income tax, CPP or EI from payments to your corporation. That is a big difference from T4 employment.

### H2: What are the GST/HST basics for an Ontario contractor corporation?

- **Rate.** For services supplied in Ontario, HST is 13%.
- **Small supplier threshold.** A corporation is a small supplier while its taxable supplies, including those of associated corporations, are $30,000 or less over four consecutive calendar quarters. Once it goes over, it must register. Going over $30,000 in a single calendar quarter also ends small supplier status, with a faster start date for charging HST. We confirm the exact date with you.
- **What that means for you.** At $95 an hour and 37.5 hours a week, your corporation bills $3,562.50 a week and passes $30,000 in its ninth week, at $32,062.50. Most agencies want your HST number before the first invoice anyway, so plan to register before you start. We handle the GST/HST registration for your corporation.
- **Input tax credits.** HST your corporation pays on business purchases, such as a laptop or software, can usually be claimed back. Keep the receipts.
- **Filing.** The corporation files a GST/HST return for each reporting period.

A PSB finding is an income tax issue. It does not switch off HST on your fees. Your corporation still charges and remits HST either way.

### H2: Why does CRA look past the agency to the end client?

Under section 125(7) of the Income Tax Act, your corporation carries on a PSB when two things are true. First, you, the person doing the work (the "incorporated employee"), or a person related to you, own 10% or more of any class of its shares, directly or indirectly. Second, you would reasonably be regarded as an officer or employee of the person receiving the services if your corporation did not exist.

On paper, the agency receives your services. CRA looks at the party the services are really provided to, and in most agency placements that is the end client. So two sets of documents matter: the agency contract, and the end client's onboarding rules.

The tests come from Wiebe Door Services Ltd. v. M.N.R. and 671122 Ontario Ltd. v. Sagaz Industries Canada Inc. The central question is whose business it is. CRA weighs control, ownership of tools and equipment, chance of profit and risk of loss, and integration into the client's business. Common agency facts that point toward control include:

- fixed 35, 37.5 or 40 hour weeks with set schedules
- timesheets approved by an end client manager
- onsite or VPN-only access
- a client-supplied laptop, badge and email address

None of these decides the answer alone. CRA tests the whole relationship, year by year. There are two exceptions: the corporation employs more than five full-time employees throughout the year, or the payments come from a corporation associated with yours. Neither usually fits a one-person corporation billing an unrelated agency, and whether owners or family members count needs a review of their real roles. For the full picture, please read our page on [PSB risk for incorporated contractors](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-risk-incorporated-contractors/).

#### H3: What does a PSB finding cost?

The combined Ontario small business rate is 11.2% for income earned from July 1, 2026 (9% federal plus 2.2% Ontario). Income earned before July 1, 2026 was taxed at 12.2%, so a December 31, 2026 year-end gets a blended 11.70%. PSB income is taxed at 44.5%: 33% federal, which includes the additional 5% under section 123.5, plus the 11.5% Ontario general rate. Section 18(1)(p) also denies most expenses, such as software, equipment, phone, insurance and accounting fees.

Here is a full year at 11.2%, with $171,000 of revenue, $9,000 of expenses and no salary:

- Not a PSB: ($171,000 minus $9,000) x 11.2% = $18,144
- PSB: expenses denied, so $171,000 x 44.5% = $76,095
- Extra tax: $57,951 for one year

### H2: How does agency contracting compare with T4 employment or a direct contract?

| Point | Incorporated contractor through an agency | T4 employee of the agency | Direct contract with the end client |
|---|---|---|---|
| Who signs with whom | Your corporation signs with the agency, and the agency signs with the end client | You sign an employment contract with the agency | Your corporation signs with the end client |
| Who controls the daily work | Usually the end client's managers, within the agency contract | Usually the end client's managers, with the agency as legal employer | The end client, within your contract terms |
| How you are paid | Agency pays your corporation fees plus 13% HST | Agency pays you wages through payroll | End client pays your corporation fees plus 13% HST |
| Tax treatment | Corporate tax at 11.2% if not a PSB, then personal tax on salary or dividends | Agency withholds income tax, CPP and EI, with personal rates up to 53.53% at the top 2026 Ontario rate | Same corporate treatment as the agency route |
| Expenses | Deductible if not a PSB, mostly denied under 18(1)(p) if a PSB | Very limited employment expense deductions | Same as the agency route |
| PSB exposure | Yes, tested against the party really receiving the services | None, because there is no corporation | Yes, tested directly against the end client |

The agency layer does not lower PSB risk on its own. A direct contract does not either. The facts of how you work decide. If you are weighing these options for a new role, please bring the offer to a consultation.

### H2: How do we help contractors who work through agencies?

We work only with incorporated Canadian businesses, on a fixed flat-fee model. For agency contractors, that usually means:

- **Incorporation.** Setting up the corporation so the agency paperwork is ready.
- **Bookkeeping and GST/HST filings.** Recording agency deposits, separating HST, claiming input tax credits and filing returns.
- **Payroll.** Setting up your salary, T4 slips and remittances.
- **Corporate tax returns (T2).** Filed for each taxation year.
- **Tax planning.** This includes [salary vs dividend for incorporated contractors](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/salary-vs-dividend-incorporated-contractor/). We review your contract and working facts as part of tax planning, and our page on [PSB contract review](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/contract-review-psb-indicators/) explains how we read clauses for PSB indicators. We are accountants, not lawyers, so contract wording is for your lawyer.
- **CRA audit support and representation.** If CRA sends a questionnaire or review letter, we act as the corporation's authorized representative, organize documents, communicate with the auditor, and review and respond to proposal letters.

IT contractors can read more on our page for an [accountant for IT contractors](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/accountant-for-it-contractors/). Finance contractors, such as FP&A, controllership or audit professionals, can read our page for an [accountant for finance contractors](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/accountant-for-finance-contractors/).

Our firm has 1300+ five-star Google reviews and is a "2026 Xero Awarded Firm". The team includes a second designated CPA (CPA Ontario) and bookkeeping support staff.

[TRUSTINDEX WIDGET]

If you are about to sign with an agency, or you already bill one through your corporation, please [book a consultation](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/consultation/). Please have your agency contract and statement of work ready. You can also call 647-212-9559 or email info@gondaliyacpa.ca. We are open 9AM to 8:30PM, Monday to Sunday.

## FAQs

#### Q: Do I need to be incorporated to contract through a staffing agency?

Not always. Many agencies offer a T4 payroll option as well as a contract with your corporation. If you choose the corporate route, the agency will ask for proof of incorporation, your business number and your HST number. Our services are for incorporated businesses, and we can also set up the corporation for you.

#### Q: Does working through an agency protect my corporation from PSB status?

No. On paper the agency receives your services, but CRA looks at the party the services are really provided to, which is usually the end client. The agency contract and the end client's onboarding rules both matter. CRA tests the whole relationship, year by year.

#### Q: Does my corporation have to charge HST to the agency?

Once your corporation is registered, yes. The rate in Ontario is 13%. A corporation must register once its taxable supplies go over $30,000 in a single calendar quarter or over four consecutive calendar quarters. A full-time agency contract passes that quickly, and most agencies ask for your HST number before the first invoice.

#### Q: Will the agency deduct tax from payments to my corporation?

No. The agency pays your corporation its fees plus HST, with no income tax, CPP or EI taken off. Your corporation is responsible for its own corporate tax, its HST returns and the payroll for any salary it pays you.

#### Q: What happens to my business expenses if CRA finds a PSB?

Section 18(1)(p) limits deductions to salary, wages and other pay to the incorporated employee, benefits and allowances provided to that person, certain costs of selling property or negotiating contracts that an employee could deduct, and legal costs to collect fees owed. Rent, software, equipment, travel, phone, insurance, accounting fees and similar costs are denied.

#### Q: Can you review my agency contract for PSB indicators?

We review your contract and working facts as part of tax planning, including how the agency contract and the end client's onboarding rules read for control, tools and integration. We are accountants, not lawyers. Contract wording changes are for your lawyer.

#### Q: What corporate tax rate applies to an agency contractor corporation in 2026?

If the corporation is not a PSB, the combined Ontario small business rate is 11.2% from July 1, 2026. Income earned before that date was taxed at 12.2%, so a December 31, 2026 year-end gets a blended 11.70%. If the corporation is a PSB, the combined rate is 44.5%.

## Page copy

H1: PSB Tax Cost Calculator for Ontario Contractor Corporations

This PSB tax cost calculator shows how much more corporate tax your Ontario corporation could pay if CRA treats it as a personal services business. It compares the current 11.2% small business rate with the 44.5% PSB rate on the same income, then shows how paying yourself a salary changes the gap.

[CALCULATOR WIDGET]

### H2: How do I use the PSB tax cost calculator?

I built this calculator for incorporated IT and finance contractors who bill through their own Ontario corporation, often through a staffing agency.

Enter three amounts and your year-end date. The results update as you type. Scenario 1 uses your income and expenses with no salary. Scenario 2 adds the salary you entered. Each shows taxable income, corporate tax and the effective rate in a "Not a PSB" column and a "PSB" column. The last row shows the extra corporate tax if CRA treats the corporation as a PSB.

The defaults match a common contract: $95 an hour, 37.5 hours a week, 48 weeks a year. That is $171,000 of billings.

#### H3: What does each input mean?

**Corporate income for the year before salary.** Your corporation's contract revenue for the taxation year, before salary and other expenses. Use the amount billed to the agency or client, without GST/HST.

**Business expenses other than salary.** The costs your corporation deducts today: home office rent, software, a laptop and other equipment, phone, insurance, accounting fees and similar costs.

**Salary paid to you by your corporation.** Gross salary for the year, paid through payroll with a T4 slip. Enter 0 if you take dividends only or leave the money in the corporation.

**Taxation year-end date.** The last day of your corporation's taxation year, as shown on your T2 return. The calculator assumes a full 12-month year.

### H2: Which tax rates does the calculator use?

For a corporation that is not a PSB, the calculator applies the combined Ontario small business rate to the first $500,000 of taxable income. That rate is the 9% federal small business rate plus the Ontario small business rate. The 2026 Ontario Budget cut Ontario's rate to 2.2% from 3.2%, starting July 1, 2026. So the combined rate is now 11.2%. Income earned before July 1, 2026 was taxed at 12.2%.

Taxable income above $500,000 is taxed at the 26.5% general rate, which is 15% federal plus 11.5% Ontario. The calculator assumes the full $500,000 limit, which associated corporations share.

For a PSB, the calculator applies 44.5% to all taxable income. There is no lower rate on the first dollar.

#### H3: How is the July 1, 2026 rate cut prorated?

The Ontario cut is prorated by days when a taxation year straddles July 1, 2026. The calculator counts the days in your 12-month taxation year before July 1, 2026 and the days on or after it. Then it blends the two rates.

A December 31, 2026 year-end has 181 days at 12.2% and 184 days at 11.2%. The blended rate is 11.69589%, shown as 11.70%. On $150,000 of taxable income, that is $17,543.84 of tax.

A June 30, 2026 year-end falls fully before the cut, so it gets 12.20%. A December 31, 2027 year-end gets 11.20%. With the default numbers and a December 31, 2026 year-end, the Not a PSB tax in Scenario 1 is $18,947.34 rather than $18,144, because of the blend.

Leap years count as 366 days. The calculator assumes current rates continue for later years.

#### H3: Where does 44.5% come from?

The federal rate starts at 38%. The 10% provincial abatement brings it to 28%. A PSB does not get the 13% general rate reduction or the small business deduction. It also pays an additional 5% tax on PSB income under section 123.5 of the Income Tax Act. That makes 33% federal. Ontario adds its 11.5% general rate, for a combined 44.5%.

The July 1, 2026 cut does not touch the PSB rate. So the gap between the two rates is now 33.3 percentage points, compared with 32.3 before the cut.

### H2: Why are expenses removed in the PSB column?

Paragraph 18(1)(p) of the Income Tax Act limits what a PSB can deduct. The allowed items are:

- salary, wages or other remuneration paid in the year to the incorporated employee;
- the cost of benefits or allowances provided to the incorporated employee;
- certain amounts spent selling property or negotiating contracts that an employee could deduct; and
- legal expenses to collect amounts owing for services.

Rent, software, equipment, travel, phone, insurance, accounting fees and similar costs are denied. So the PSB column adds your expenses back. With the defaults, taxable income rises to $171,000 from $162,000, and all of it is taxed at 44.5%.

The calculator treats every dollar in the expenses field as denied. If some costs fall within the allowed items, your real PSB figure would be lower.

### H2: How does paying a salary change the PSB tax cost?

Salary paid to you is deductible even for a PSB. It is the first allowed item under 18(1)(p). So every dollar of salary takes a dollar out of the income exposed to 44.5%. That is why the gap shrinks in Scenario 2.

But the salary does not disappear. It is taxed in your hands at your personal marginal rate, which reaches 53.53% in Ontario for 2026 on taxable income over $258,482. Salary also brings payroll work: CPP, T4 slips and source deduction remittances. The calculator shows corporate tax only, so it cannot tell you whether a salary is the right choice overall. Our page on [salary vs dividend for incorporated contractors](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/salary-vs-dividend-incorporated-contractor/) covers the pay mix in more detail.

Salary also does not change whether your corporation is a PSB. It only changes how much corporate income is left to be taxed at 44.5%.

#### H3: What do the numbers look like with and without salary?

Both columns use $171,000 of revenue, $9,000 of expenses and a full taxation year at 11.2%, such as a December 31, 2027 year-end.

| Item | No salary | $120,000 salary |
|---|---|---|
| Revenue | $171,000 | $171,000 |
| Business expenses | $9,000 | $9,000 |
| Salary paid to the contractor | $0 | $120,000 |
| Taxable income, not a PSB | $162,000 | $42,000 |
| Corporate tax at 11.2%, not a PSB | $18,144 | $4,704 |
| Taxable income, PSB (expenses denied) | $171,000 | $51,000 |
| Corporate tax at 44.5%, PSB | $76,095 | $22,695 |
| Extra corporate tax if PSB | $57,951 | $17,991 |

With the salary, the corporate gap falls by $39,960. That is not a $39,960 saving. The $120,000 salary is now taxed in your hands, and this table leaves out employer CPP and personal tax.

### H2: What does this estimate leave out?

Please read the results as a first look, not a tax return. The calculator does not include:

- personal tax on salary or dividends;
- CPP and EI;
- the passive income rules, which can reduce the small business limit when a corporation earns investment income;
- a small business limit shared with associated corporations;
- short taxation years, such as a first year after incorporation or a change of year-end;
- the allowed 18(1)(p) items other than salary;
- arrears interest if CRA reassesses past years.

It also leaves out what happens after money leaves the corporation. PSB income is taxed at the full rate, so it adds to the general rate income pool (GRIP). Dividends paid from it can be designated eligible dividends. Integration still leaves a gap. On $100,000 of PSB income at top 2026 rates, corporate tax of $44,500 plus personal tax of $21,833.70 on a $55,500 eligible dividend comes to $66,333.70. The same $100,000 paid as salary costs $53,530 in personal tax. The PSB route costs $12,803.70 more.

CRA can look at more than one year, and each year stands on its own facts. Our page on [CRA PSB review](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/cra-psb-review-support/) explains what to expect and how we support you as the corporation's representative.

#### H3: Does the calculator tell me if my corporation is a PSB?

No. It assumes the answer both ways and shows the cost. Whether your corporation is a PSB depends on facts.

Under subsection 125(7), a PSB exists when the person doing the work, or a person related to them, owns 10% or more of any class of shares, directly or indirectly, and the worker would reasonably be regarded as an officer or employee of the client if the corporation did not exist. There are two exceptions: the corporation employs more than five full-time employees in the business throughout the year, or the amount is received from an associated corporation. Whether owners or family members count toward the five needs review.

CRA looks at the whole relationship using the Wiebe Door and Sagaz tests: control, ownership of tools and equipment, chance of profit and risk of loss, and whether the work is part of the client's business or your own. Fixed 35, 37.5 or 40 hour schedules, timesheet approval and client laptops point toward control, but hours alone never decide it. Our page on [40 hour contract PSB risk](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/40-hour-contract-psb-risk/) explains how hours are read. Our guide to [personal services business risk](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-risk-incorporated-contractors/) covers the full test.

### H2: How can we help you read your results?

If the extra tax figure worries you, look at your facts rather than guess. We review your contract and working facts as part of tax planning. We look at the agency agreement, the end client's onboarding rules, how you are paid and how you work. Then we talk through ways to reduce risk, document your position, and fit salary and dividends to your numbers.

We are accountants, not lawyers. Contract wording is for your lawyer.

We also prepare your T2 return, run payroll with T4 slips and remittances, file GST/HST and keep your books, on a fixed flat fee. We work only with incorporated Canadian businesses. If you are in the GTA, our page for an [incorporated contractor accountant in Toronto](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/incorporated-contractor-accountant-toronto/) explains how we work with contractors. We have 1300+ five-star Google reviews and are a 2026 Xero Awarded Firm.

[TRUSTINDEX WIDGET]

Please run your own numbers above, then [book a consultation](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/consultation/) so we can review your contract, your year-end and your pay mix together. You can also call us at 647-212-9559 or email info@gondaliyacpa.ca. We are open 9AM to 8:30PM, Monday to Sunday.

## Calculator code (Elementor HTML widget)

```html
<style>
#gcpa-psbc{box-sizing:border-box;max-width:780px;margin:0 auto;padding:20px;background:#ffffff;color:#1f2933;border:1px solid #c5ced8;border-radius:8px;font-family:inherit;font-size:16px;line-height:1.5;text-align:left;overflow-wrap:break-word}
#gcpa-psbc *,#gcpa-psbc *::before,#gcpa-psbc *::after{box-sizing:border-box}
#gcpa-psbc p{margin:0 0 12px}
#gcpa-psbc .gcpa-psbc-title{margin:0 0 4px;font-size:20px;font-weight:700;line-height:1.3;color:#12324f}
#gcpa-psbc .gcpa-psbc-intro{color:#3e4c59;font-size:15px}
#gcpa-psbc .gcpa-psbc-grid{display:grid;grid-template-columns:1fr 1fr;gap:16px}
#gcpa-psbc .gcpa-psbc-field{display:flex;flex-direction:column;min-width:0}
#gcpa-psbc label{display:block;margin:0 0 4px;padding:0;font-size:15px;font-weight:600;line-height:1.35;color:#1f2933}
#gcpa-psbc input{display:block;width:100%;max-width:100%;min-height:44px;margin:0;padding:8px 10px;font-family:inherit;font-size:16px;line-height:1.4;color:#1f2933;background:#ffffff;border:1px solid #7b8794;border-radius:6px;box-shadow:none}
#gcpa-psbc input:focus{outline:2px solid #12324f;outline-offset:1px;border-color:#12324f}

#gcpa-psbc .gcpa-psbc-actions{margin:16px 0 0}
#gcpa-psbc .gcpa-psbc-btn{display:inline-block;min-height:44px;margin:0;padding:10px 22px;font-family:inherit;font-size:16px;font-weight:600;line-height:1.2;color:#ffffff;background:#12324f;border:1px solid #12324f;border-radius:6px;cursor:pointer;text-transform:none;letter-spacing:normal;box-shadow:none}
#gcpa-psbc .gcpa-psbc-btn:hover,#gcpa-psbc .gcpa-psbc-btn:focus{color:#ffffff;background:#1d4a73;border-color:#1d4a73}
#gcpa-psbc .gcpa-psbc-btn:focus-visible{outline:2px solid #12324f;outline-offset:2px}
#gcpa-psbc .gcpa-psbc-error{margin:16px 0 0;padding:10px 12px;border:1px solid #b42318;border-radius:6px;background:#fef3f2;color:#7a271a;font-size:15px}
#gcpa-psbc .gcpa-psbc-error p{margin:0 0 4px}

#gcpa-psbc .gcpa-psbc-rate{margin:0 0 16px;padding:10px 12px;border:1px solid #c5ced8;border-radius:6px;background:#f5f7fa;font-size:15px}
#gcpa-psbc .gcpa-psbc-rate strong{font-size:17px;color:#12324f}
#gcpa-psbc .gcpa-psbc-tablewrap{width:100%;margin:0 0 18px;overflow-x:auto;-webkit-overflow-scrolling:touch}
#gcpa-psbc table{width:100%;margin:0;border-collapse:collapse;border-spacing:0;border:1px solid #7b8794;background:#ffffff;font-size:15px}
#gcpa-psbc caption{caption-side:top;margin:0;padding:0 0 6px;text-align:left;font-size:16px;font-weight:700;color:#12324f}
#gcpa-psbc th,#gcpa-psbc td{padding:8px 10px;border:1px solid #7b8794;text-align:left;vertical-align:top;background:#ffffff;color:#1f2933;font-size:15px;line-height:1.4}
#gcpa-psbc thead th{background:#e4e9ef;font-weight:700}
#gcpa-psbc tbody th{font-weight:600}
#gcpa-psbc thead th.gcpa-psbc-num,#gcpa-psbc td.gcpa-psbc-num{text-align:right}
#gcpa-psbc td.gcpa-psbc-num{white-space:nowrap;font-variant-numeric:tabular-nums}
#gcpa-psbc tr.gcpa-psbc-extra th,#gcpa-psbc tr.gcpa-psbc-extra td{background:#fff4e5;font-weight:700}
#gcpa-psbc .gcpa-psbc-note{margin:-8px 0 18px;font-size:14px;color:#3e4c59}
#gcpa-psbc .gcpa-psbc-notes{font-size:14px;color:#3e4c59}
#gcpa-psbc .gcpa-psbc-notes p{margin:0 0 8px}
#gcpa-psbc .gcpa-psbc-disclaimer{margin:16px 0 0;padding:12px 14px;border:1px solid #b7791f;border-radius:6px;background:#fffaeb;color:#1f2933;font-size:14px}
#gcpa-psbc .gcpa-psbc-disclaimer p{margin:0}
#gcpa-psbc .gcpa-psbc-disclaimer a{color:#12324f;text-decoration:underline;word-break:break-all}
#gcpa-psbc [hidden]{display:none !important}
@media (max-width:600px){
#gcpa-psbc{padding:14px}
#gcpa-psbc .gcpa-psbc-grid{grid-template-columns:1fr;gap:14px}
#gcpa-psbc th,#gcpa-psbc td{padding:6px 7px;font-size:14px}
#gcpa-psbc .gcpa-psbc-btn{width:100%}
}
</style>
<div id="gcpa-psbc" class="gcpa-psbc">
  <p class="gcpa-psbc-title">PSB tax cost calculator (Ontario)</p>
  <p class="gcpa-psbc-intro">Compare corporate tax at the Ontario small business rate with the 44.5% personal services business (PSB) rate on the same income, with and without salary.</p>
  <div class="gcpa-psbc-grid">
    <div class="gcpa-psbc-field">
      <label for="gcpa-psbc-income">Corporate income for the year before salary (CAD)</label>
      <input id="gcpa-psbc-income" name="gcpa-psbc-income" type="text" inputmode="decimal" autocomplete="off" value="171000" aria-describedby="gcpa-psbc-income-help">
      <span class="gcpa-psbc-help" id="gcpa-psbc-income-help">Contract revenue for the taxation year, before salary and other expenses. Leave out GST/HST.</span>
    </div>
    <div class="gcpa-psbc-field">
      <label for="gcpa-psbc-expenses">Business expenses other than salary (CAD)</label>
      <input id="gcpa-psbc-expenses" name="gcpa-psbc-expenses" type="text" inputmode="decimal" autocomplete="off" value="9000" aria-describedby="gcpa-psbc-expenses-help">
      <span class="gcpa-psbc-help" id="gcpa-psbc-expenses-help">Rent, software, equipment, phone, insurance, accounting fees and similar costs.</span>
    </div>
    <div class="gcpa-psbc-field">
      <label for="gcpa-psbc-salary">Salary paid to you by your corporation (CAD)</label>
      <input id="gcpa-psbc-salary" name="gcpa-psbc-salary" type="text" inputmode="decimal" autocomplete="off" value="0" aria-describedby="gcpa-psbc-salary-help">
      <span class="gcpa-psbc-help" id="gcpa-psbc-salary-help">Gross salary for the year. Enter 0 if you take dividends only or leave the money in the corporation.</span>
    </div>
    <div class="gcpa-psbc-field">
      <label for="gcpa-psbc-yearend">Taxation year-end date</label>
      <input id="gcpa-psbc-yearend" name="gcpa-psbc-yearend" type="date" value="2026-12-31" min="2021-01-01" max="2027-12-31" aria-describedby="gcpa-psbc-yearend-help">
      <span class="gcpa-psbc-help" id="gcpa-psbc-yearend-help">Last day of your corporation's taxation year. A 12-month year is assumed.</span>
    </div>
  </div>
  <div class="gcpa-psbc-actions">
    <button type="button" class="gcpa-psbc-btn" id="gcpa-psbc-calc">Calculate</button>
  </div>
  <div class="gcpa-psbc-error" id="gcpa-psbc-error" role="alert" hidden></div>
  <div class="gcpa-psbc-results" id="gcpa-psbc-results" aria-live="polite">
    <p class="gcpa-psbc-rate">Small business rate applied for this year-end: <strong data-out="sbrate"></strong><br><span data-out="period"></span></p>
    <div class="gcpa-psbc-tablewrap">
      <table>
        <caption>Scenario 1: Same income, no salary</caption>
        <thead>
          <tr><th scope="col">Item</th><th scope="col" class="gcpa-psbc-num">Not a PSB</th><th scope="col" class="gcpa-psbc-num">PSB</th></tr>
        </thead>
        <tbody>
          <tr><th scope="row">Taxable income</th><td class="gcpa-psbc-num" data-out="s1-np-taxable"></td><td class="gcpa-psbc-num" data-out="s1-p-taxable"></td></tr>
          <tr><th scope="row">Corporate tax</th><td class="gcpa-psbc-num" data-out="s1-np-tax"></td><td class="gcpa-psbc-num" data-out="s1-p-tax"></td></tr>
          <tr><th scope="row">Effective rate on taxable income</th><td class="gcpa-psbc-num" data-out="s1-np-rate"></td><td class="gcpa-psbc-num" data-out="s1-p-rate"></td></tr>
          <tr class="gcpa-psbc-extra"><th scope="row">Extra corporate tax if CRA treats the corporation as a PSB</th><td class="gcpa-psbc-num" colspan="2" data-out="s1-extra"></td></tr>
        </tbody>
      </table>
    </div>
    <div class="gcpa-psbc-tablewrap">
      <table>
        <caption>Scenario 2: With the salary you entered</caption>
        <thead>
          <tr><th scope="col">Item</th><th scope="col" class="gcpa-psbc-num">Not a PSB</th><th scope="col" class="gcpa-psbc-num">PSB</th></tr>
        </thead>
        <tbody>
          <tr><th scope="row">Taxable income</th><td class="gcpa-psbc-num" data-out="s2-np-taxable"></td><td class="gcpa-psbc-num" data-out="s2-p-taxable"></td></tr>
          <tr><th scope="row">Corporate tax</th><td class="gcpa-psbc-num" data-out="s2-np-tax"></td><td class="gcpa-psbc-num" data-out="s2-p-tax"></td></tr>
          <tr><th scope="row">Effective rate on taxable income</th><td class="gcpa-psbc-num" data-out="s2-np-rate"></td><td class="gcpa-psbc-num" data-out="s2-p-rate"></td></tr>
          <tr class="gcpa-psbc-extra"><th scope="row">Extra corporate tax if CRA treats the corporation as a PSB</th><td class="gcpa-psbc-num" colspan="2" data-out="s2-extra"></td></tr>
        </tbody>
      </table>
    </div>
    <p class="gcpa-psbc-note" data-out="s2-note">You entered no salary, so Scenario 2 matches Scenario 1. Enter a salary to see how it changes the result.</p>
    <div class="gcpa-psbc-notes">
      <p>PSB column: business expenses other than salary are treated as denied under paragraph 18(1)(p) of the Income Tax Act. Salary paid to you is still deducted. If some costs are benefits or allowances for you or other allowed items, the real PSB figure would be lower.</p>
      <p>Not a PSB column: the small business rate applies to the first $500,000 of taxable income and 26.5% applies to any amount above that.</p>
      <p>Assumptions: an Ontario corporation, a 12-month taxation year, the full $500,000 small business limit (no associated corporations), a 9% federal small business rate, an Ontario small business rate of 3.2% before July 1, 2026 and 2.2% from July 1, 2026, and no change in current rates for later years.</p>
    </div>
  </div>
  <div class="gcpa-psbc-disclaimer">
    <p><strong>Please note:</strong> Results are estimates only and are not tax or legal advice. They do not tell you whether your corporation is a PSB. They cover corporate tax only and leave out personal tax on salary or dividends, CPP, EI and the passive income rules. Please book a consultation at <a href="https://gondaliyacpa.ca/consultation/">https://gondaliyacpa.ca/consultation/</a> for a review of your facts.</p>
  </div>
</div>
<script>
(function () {
  'use strict';

  var RATES = {
    federalSmall: 0.09,
    ontarioSmallBefore: 0.032,
    ontarioSmallAfter: 0.022,
    general: 0.265,
    psb: 0.445,
    smallBusinessLimit: 500000
  };
  var DAY_MS = 86400000;
  var CUT_UTC = Date.UTC(2026, 6, 1);
  var MIN_YEAR_END_UTC = Date.UTC(2021, 0, 1);
  var MAX_YEAR_END_UTC = Date.UTC(2027, 11, 31);
  var MONTHS = ['January', 'February', 'March', 'April', 'May', 'June', 'July',
    'August', 'September', 'October', 'November', 'December'];

  function parseIsoDate(value) {
    var m = /^(\d{4})-(\d{2})-(\d{2})$/.exec(String(value == null ? '' : value).trim());
    if (!m) { return null; }
    var y = Number(m[1]);
    var mo = Number(m[2]);
    var d = Number(m[3]);
    var utc = Date.UTC(y, mo - 1, d);
    var check = new Date(utc);
    if (check.getUTCFullYear() !== y || check.getUTCMonth() !== mo - 1 || check.getUTCDate() !== d) {
      return null;
    }
    return { y: y, m: mo, d: d, utc: utc };
  }

  function daysInMonth(year, month1to12) {
    return new Date(Date.UTC(year, month1to12, 0)).getUTCDate();
  }

  /* Taxation year = year-end minus one year plus one day, through the year-end. */
  function taxationYear(yearEnd) {
    var e = parseIsoDate(yearEnd);
    if (!e) { return null; }
    var prevYear = e.y - 1;
    var prevDay = Math.min(e.d, daysInMonth(prevYear, e.m));
    var startUTC = Date.UTC(prevYear, e.m - 1, prevDay) + DAY_MS;
    var endExclusiveUTC = e.utc + DAY_MS;
    var totalDays = Math.round((endExclusiveUTC - startUTC) / DAY_MS);
    var daysBefore = Math.round((CUT_UTC - startUTC) / DAY_MS);
    daysBefore = Math.max(0, Math.min(totalDays, daysBefore));
    var daysAfter = totalDays - daysBefore;
    var smallRate = RATES.federalSmall +
      (daysBefore * RATES.ontarioSmallBefore + daysAfter * RATES.ontarioSmallAfter) / totalDays;
    return {
      startUTC: startUTC,
      endUTC: e.utc,
      totalDays: totalDays,
      daysBefore: daysBefore,
      daysAfter: daysAfter,
      smallRate: smallRate
    };
  }

  function round2(x) {
    return Math.round(x * 100) / 100;
  }

  function taxNotPSB(taxable, smallRate) {
    var small = Math.min(taxable, RATES.smallBusinessLimit);
    var above = Math.max(0, taxable - RATES.smallBusinessLimit);
    return small * smallRate + above * RATES.general;
  }

  function scenario(income, expenses, salary, smallRate) {
    var npTaxable = Math.max(0, income - expenses - salary);
    var npTax = round2(taxNotPSB(npTaxable, smallRate));
    var pTaxable = Math.max(0, income - salary);
    var pTax = round2(pTaxable * RATES.psb);
    return {
      notPSB: { taxable: npTaxable, tax: npTax, effectiveRate: npTaxable > 0 ? npTax / npTaxable : 0 },
      psb: { taxable: pTaxable, tax: pTax, effectiveRate: pTaxable > 0 ? pTax / pTaxable : 0 },
      extra: round2(pTax - npTax)
    };
  }

  /* Pure calculation. input: { income, expenses, salary (numbers), yearEnd ('YYYY-MM-DD') } */
  function calculate(input) {
    var errors = [];
    var labels = { income: 'Corporate income', expenses: 'Business expenses', salary: 'Salary' };
    var keys = ['income', 'expenses', 'salary'];
    for (var i = 0; i < keys.length; i++) {
      var v = input[keys[i]];
      if (typeof v !== 'number' || !isFinite(v)) {
        errors.push({ field: keys[i], message: labels[keys[i]] + ' must be a number. Please enter 0 if there is none.' });
      } else if (v < 0) {
        errors.push({ field: keys[i], message: labels[keys[i]] + ' cannot be a negative number.' });
      }
    }
    if (!errors.length && input.salary > input.income) {
      errors.push({ field: 'salary', message: 'Salary cannot be more than corporate income.' });
    }
    var ty = taxationYear(input.yearEnd);
    if (!ty) {
      errors.push({ field: 'yearend', message: 'Please enter a valid taxation year-end date.' });
    } else if (ty.endUTC < MIN_YEAR_END_UTC || ty.endUTC > MAX_YEAR_END_UTC) {
      errors.push({ field: 'yearend', message: 'Please choose a year-end on or after January 1, 2021 and no later than December 31, 2027. Rates after 2027 are not yet announced.' });
    }
    if (errors.length) { return { ok: false, errors: errors }; }
    return {
      ok: true,
      taxYear: ty,
      smallRate: ty.smallRate,
      scenario1: scenario(input.income, input.expenses, 0, ty.smallRate),
      scenario2: scenario(input.income, input.expenses, input.salary, ty.smallRate)
    };
  }

  function parseAmount(value) {
    var s = String(value == null ? '' : value).replace(/[\s,$]/g, '');
    if (!/^-?(\d+\.?\d*|\.\d+)$/.test(s)) { return NaN; }
    return Number(s);
  }

  function money(x) {
    return x.toLocaleString('en-CA', { style: 'currency', currency: 'CAD' });
  }

  function pct(r) {
    return (r * 100).toFixed(2) + '%';
  }

  function formatDate(utc) {
    var d = new Date(utc);
    return MONTHS[d.getUTCMonth()] + ' ' + d.getUTCDate() + ', ' + d.getUTCFullYear();
  }

  function periodText(ty) {
    var before = ((RATES.federalSmall + RATES.ontarioSmallBefore) * 100).toFixed(1) + '%';
    var after = ((RATES.federalSmall + RATES.ontarioSmallAfter) * 100).toFixed(1) + '%';
    var parts = [];
    if (ty.daysBefore > 0) { parts.push(ty.daysBefore + ' days at ' + before); }
    if (ty.daysAfter > 0) { parts.push(ty.daysAfter + ' days at ' + after); }
    return 'Taxation year ' + formatDate(ty.startUTC) + ' to ' + formatDate(ty.endUTC) +
      ' (' + ty.totalDays + ' days): ' + parts.join(' and ') + '.';
  }

  function init() {
    var k;
    var root = document.getElementById('gcpa-psbc');
    if (!root || root.getAttribute('data-gcpa-ready') === '1') { return; }
    root.setAttribute('data-gcpa-ready', '1');

    var fields = {
      income: document.getElementById('gcpa-psbc-income'),
      expenses: document.getElementById('gcpa-psbc-expenses'),
      salary: document.getElementById('gcpa-psbc-salary'),
      yearend: document.getElementById('gcpa-psbc-yearend')
    };
    var errorBox = document.getElementById('gcpa-psbc-error');
    var results = document.getElementById('gcpa-psbc-results');
    var button = document.getElementById('gcpa-psbc-calc');
    if (!fields.income || !fields.expenses || !fields.salary || !fields.yearend || !errorBox || !results) { return; }

    var outs = {};
    var nodes = root.querySelectorAll('[data-out]');
    for (var i = 0; i < nodes.length; i++) {
      outs[nodes[i].getAttribute('data-out')] = nodes[i];
    }
    function setText(key, text) {
      if (outs[key]) { outs[key].textContent = text; }
    }
    function fill(prefix, s) {
      setText(prefix + '-np-taxable', money(s.notPSB.taxable));
      setText(prefix + '-p-taxable', money(s.psb.taxable));
      setText(prefix + '-np-tax', money(s.notPSB.tax));
      setText(prefix + '-p-tax', money(s.psb.tax));
      setText(prefix + '-np-rate', pct(s.notPSB.effectiveRate));
      setText(prefix + '-p-rate', pct(s.psb.effectiveRate));
      setText(prefix + '-extra', money(s.extra));
    }

    function run() {
      var input = {
        income: parseAmount(fields.income.value),
        expenses: parseAmount(fields.expenses.value),
        salary: parseAmount(fields.salary.value),
        yearEnd: fields.yearend.value
      };
      var r = calculate(input);
      for (k in fields) {
        if (Object.prototype.hasOwnProperty.call(fields, k)) { fields[k].removeAttribute('aria-invalid'); }
      }
      if (!r.ok) {
        while (errorBox.firstChild) { errorBox.removeChild(errorBox.firstChild); }
        for (var j = 0; j < r.errors.length; j++) {
          var p = document.createElement('p');
          p.textContent = r.errors[j].message;
          errorBox.appendChild(p);
          if (fields[r.errors[j].field]) { fields[r.errors[j].field].setAttribute('aria-invalid', 'true'); }
        }
        errorBox.hidden = false;
        results.hidden = true;
        return;
      }
      errorBox.hidden = true;
      results.hidden = false;
      setText('sbrate', pct(r.smallRate));
      setText('period', periodText(r.taxYear));
      fill('s1', r.scenario1);
      fill('s2', r.scenario2);
      if (outs['s2-note']) { outs['s2-note'].hidden = input.salary !== 0; }
    }

    if (button) { button.addEventListener('click', run); }
    for (k in fields) {
      if (Object.prototype.hasOwnProperty.call(fields, k)) {
        fields[k].addEventListener('input', run);
        fields[k].addEventListener('change', run);
      }
    }
    run();
  }

  if (typeof window === 'undefined' && typeof module === 'object' && module && module.exports) {
    module.exports = { calculate: calculate, taxationYear: taxationYear, parseAmount: parseAmount, RATES: RATES };
  }

  if (typeof document !== 'undefined' && document.getElementById) {
    if (document.readyState === 'loading') {
      document.addEventListener('DOMContentLoaded', init);
    } else {
      init();
    }
  }
})();
</script>
```

## FAQs

#### Q: What rates does the PSB tax cost calculator use?

For a corporation that is not a PSB, it uses 9% federal plus the Ontario small business rate (3.2% before July 1, 2026 and 2.2% from July 1, 2026) on the first $500,000, and 26.5% above that. For a PSB, it uses 44.5% on all taxable income.

#### Q: Why does a December 31, 2026 year-end show 11.70%?

That taxation year has 181 days before July 1, 2026 at 12.2% and 184 days from July 1, 2026 at 11.2%. The day-weighted rate is 11.69589%, which the calculator shows as 11.70%.

#### Q: Why does the PSB column ignore my business expenses?

Paragraph 18(1)(p) of the Income Tax Act denies most PSB expenses, such as rent, software, equipment, phone, insurance and accounting fees. Salary, wages, benefits and allowances paid to the incorporated employee stay deductible, along with a few narrow items.

#### Q: Does paying myself a salary remove PSB risk?

No. Salary is deductible even for a PSB, so it reduces the corporate income taxed at 44.5%. It does not change whether your corporation is a PSB, and the salary is taxed personally at your marginal rate.

#### Q: Does the calculator include personal tax, CPP or EI?

No. It shows corporate tax only. It leaves out personal tax on salary or dividends, CPP, EI and the passive income rules. Please book a consultation if you want the full picture for your facts.

#### Q: Can I use the calculator if my corporation is not in Ontario?

It uses Ontario rates only. Other provinces have different small business and general rates, so the results would not match a corporation taxed in another province.

#### Q: Does the calculator save the numbers I enter?

No. The calculator runs in your browser. It does not store your inputs or send them to us.

## Page copy

H1: Salary vs Dividend for Incorporated Contractors in Ontario

Salary vs dividend for incorporated contractors in Ontario starts with one question: is your corporation a personal services business (PSB)? If it is not, income is taxed at 11.2% and dividends are usually non-eligible. If it is, income is taxed at 44.5%, but salary paid to you is still deductible.

### H2: Why does PSB status change salary vs dividend for incorporated contractors?

Most pay advice for contractor corporations assumes the corporation runs an active business that gets the small business deduction. But if you bill through a staffing agency on a 35, 37.5 or 40 hour contract with one bank, insurer or government client, there is a second question in the background.

That question is whether CRA could treat your corporation as a PSB. If it did, income left in the corporation would be taxed at 44.5% instead of 11.2%, most expenses are denied, and the way dividends are taxed changes. I explain the test itself on our page about [personal services business risk](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-risk-incorporated-contractors/). This page is about what PSB status means for how you pay yourself.

For every contractor corporation, I look at pay in two scenarios. The first is the position you file, where the corporation is not a PSB. The second is the downside, where CRA reassesses it as one. A good pay mix holds up reasonably well in both.

### H2: How should you pay yourself if your corporation is not a PSB?

#### H3: Income is taxed at 11.2% and dividends are usually non-eligible

If your corporation is not a PSB, its active business income up to the $500,000 small business limit is taxed at a combined Ontario rate of 11.2%. That rate applies from July 1, 2026. Income earned before July 1, 2026 was taxed at 12.2%, so a corporation with a December 31, 2026 year-end gets a blended rate of 11.70% for 2026.

Because that income gets the small business deduction, dividends paid from it are generally non-eligible dividends. The top 2026 Ontario marginal rate on non-eligible dividends is 47.74%.

Salary works differently. The corporation deducts it, so it is not taxed in the corporation at all. You pay personal tax on it instead, at up to 53.53%, the top 2026 Ontario marginal rate on salary.

#### H3: At the top rates, salary and dividends land close together

Here is $100,000 of corporate income, not PSB, at top 2026 marginal rates. It assumes your other income already puts you in the top bracket, and it leaves out CPP.

- Dividend route: corporate tax at 11.2% is $11,200. The $88,800 left is paid as a non-eligible dividend and taxed at 47.74%, which is $42,393.12. Total tax: $53,593.12.
- Salary route: $100,000 of salary taxed at 53.53% is $53,530, with no corporate tax.

The difference is $63.12. At the top bracket, the choice is not really about the rate. It is about RRSP room, CPP, how much cash you need personally, and whether you want to leave money in the corporation, taxed at 11.2% now and personally later.

#### H3: Non-eligible dividends cost more from January 1, 2027

On January 1, 2027, Ontario's non-eligible dividend tax credit falls to 1.9863%, down from 2.9863%. The top rate on non-eligible dividends rises to 48.89%, up from 47.74%. On the same $88,800 dividend, top-rate personal tax goes from $42,393.12 to $43,414.32, an increase of $1,021.20. If you were already planning a dividend near year-end, we review whether December 2026 or January 2027 suits you better.

### H2: How should you pay yourself if CRA treats your corporation as a PSB?

#### H3: Salary is still deductible under 18(1)(p)

A PSB loses most of its deductions. Section 18(1)(p) of the Income Tax Act limits them to a short list. The main item is salary, wages or other remuneration paid in the year to the incorporated employee, which is you. The cost of benefits or allowances provided to you, certain costs of selling property or negotiating contracts that an employee could deduct, and legal costs to collect fees owed are also on the list. Rent, software, equipment, travel, phone, insurance and accounting fees are denied.

So salary is the one big lever left. Every dollar of salary paid to you comes off the income taxed at 44.5%. Note the words "paid in the year". We plan payroll so salary is actually paid, with source deductions remitted, within the year.

Salary does not change whether your corporation is a PSB. That depends on the whole working relationship. Salary only changes how much income is exposed if CRA says it is one.

#### H3: Example C: what a $120,000 salary does to the PSB exposure

Take a contractor corporation that bills $171,000 in a year ($95 an hour, 37.5 hours a week, 48 weeks) and has $9,000 of expenses. Assume a full year at 11.2%.

| Scenario | Not PSB | PSB | Extra tax if PSB |
|---|---|---|---|
| No salary (Example B) | $162,000 x 11.2% = $18,144 | $171,000 x 44.5% = $76,095 | $57,951 |
| $120,000 salary (Example C) | $42,000 x 11.2% = $4,704 | $51,000 x 44.5% = $22,695 | $17,991 |

In the PSB column, the $9,000 of expenses is denied, so taxable income is $171,000 less the $120,000 salary, which is $51,000. The salary cuts corporate tax under PSB by $53,400 ($120,000 x 44.5%) and cuts the extra PSB exposure by $39,960. Employer CPP is left out, and personal tax on the salary is not shown because it depends on your other income.

Even a salary equal to all the income left after expenses does not take the PSB exposure to zero. If the corporation paid you the full $162,000, the $9,000 of denied expenses would still be taxed at 44.5%, which is $4,005. To test your own billings and salary, please use our [PSB tax cost calculator](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-tax-cost-calculator/).

#### H3: Dividends from PSB income can be eligible, but the gap stays

PSB income does not get the small business deduction. It is taxed at the full rate and adds to the general rate income pool (GRIP). That means dividends paid from it can be designated as eligible dividends, with a top 2026 Ontario marginal rate of 39.34%.

That looks better than 47.74%, but the corporate tax has already done the damage. Here is Example D at top 2026 marginal rates:

- PSB income of $100,000. Corporate tax at 44.5% is $44,500.
- The $55,500 left is paid as an eligible dividend, taxed at 39.34%, which is $21,833.70.
- Total tax is $66,333.70, or 66.33%.
- The same $100,000 paid to you as salary at 53.53% costs $53,530.

The PSB route costs $12,803.70 more on every $100,000. The dividend tax credit is built around the general corporate rate, not the 44.5% PSB rate, so the extra corporate tax never comes back. In a PSB, salary is usually the cheaper way to take money out.

### H2: What do RRSP room and CPP add to the salary side?

Salary is earned income for RRSP purposes. Each year, your new RRSP room is 18% of the prior year's earned income, up to the annual dollar limit, less any pension adjustment. A $120,000 salary creates $21,600 of room for the next year. Dividends are not earned income, so they create none.

Salary is also pensionable. The corporation withholds your share of CPP from each pay, pays the employer share, and remits both to CRA. On higher salaries, the second tier, CPP2, applies too. Some see CPP as a cost, others value the pension. We include it when we compare options.

Dividends need no payroll account and no CPP. They are simpler to pay. But they build no RRSP room, and in a PSB they come out of income that was first taxed at 44.5%.

### H2: Salary vs eligible dividend vs non-eligible dividend: how do they compare?

| Feature | Salary | Eligible dividend | Non-eligible dividend |
|---|---|---|---|
| Deductible to the corporation? | Yes. In a PSB, salary paid in the year to the incorporated employee stays deductible under 18(1)(p) | No. Paid from after-tax income | No. Paid from after-tax income |
| PSB relevance | Reduces the income exposed to 44.5% | Can be designated when paid from PSB income, because PSB income adds to GRIP | The usual type when the corporation is not a PSB and income is taxed at 11.2% |
| Creates RRSP room? | Yes, 18% of earned income up to the annual limit | No | No |
| CPP contributions? | Yes, employee and employer shares | No | No |
| Top 2026 Ontario marginal rate | 53.53% | 39.34% | 47.74% (48.89% from January 1, 2027) |

These are top marginal rates. They apply to income in the top bracket, so the rate on your own income may be lower.

### H2: How do we set the pay mix for a contractor corporation?

Each year, we work through the same steps:

1. We review your contract and working facts as part of tax planning, to see where your PSB risk sits this year. Hours alone never decide it. We are accountants, not lawyers, so any change to contract wording is for your lawyer.
2. We look at your personal cash needs and other income, and check the tax on split income (TOSI) rules before any dividend to family.
3. We model salary, dividends and retained income in both scenarios: not PSB and PSB.
4. If salary is part of the plan, we set up payroll, run it on a schedule, remit source deductions and file the T4 slips.
5. We file the T2 corporate return and keep the pay records that support the position you took.

If you write code, run cloud platforms or work in data, QA or security, our page for an [accountant for IT contractors](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/accountant-for-it-contractors/) explains how we handle the year-round work. FP&A, controller, audit and risk contractors can see our [accountant for finance contractors](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/accountant-for-finance-contractors/) page. For a general model of salary and dividends outside the PSB question, please try our [salary vs dividend calculator](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/salary-vs-dividend-calculator/).

[TRUSTINDEX WIDGET]

### H2: Would you like us to review your pay mix?

If you bill through your own Ontario corporation and you are not sure your pay mix holds up in both scenarios, please book a consultation. We work with incorporated businesses only, on a fixed flat fee. Bring your contract, your last T2 and your payroll or dividend records, and we will go through the numbers with you. [Book a consultation](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/consultation/).

## FAQs

#### Q: Is salary deductible if my corporation is a personal services business?

Yes. Section 18(1)(p) still allows salary, wages or other remuneration paid in the year to the incorporated employee, plus the cost of benefits or allowances provided to that person. Most other costs, such as rent, software, equipment, travel and phone, are denied. That is why salary matters so much in a PSB.

#### Q: Are dividends from my contractor corporation eligible or non-eligible?

If the corporation is not a PSB and its income is taxed at 11.2% with the small business deduction, dividends from that income are generally non-eligible. If the corporation is a PSB, its income is taxed at the full rate and adds to GRIP, so dividends paid from it can be designated eligible.

#### Q: Does paying myself a salary reduce my PSB risk?

No. Salary does not change whether your corporation is a PSB. That depends on the whole working relationship, tested year by year. What salary does is reduce the income left in the corporation that would be taxed at 44.5% if CRA reassessed it as a PSB.

#### Q: How much RRSP room does salary from my corporation create?

New RRSP room is 18% of the prior year's earned income, up to the annual dollar limit, less any pension adjustment. A $120,000 salary creates $21,600 of room for the next year. Dividends are not earned income, so they create no room.

#### Q: Do I have to pay CPP on salary from my own corporation?

Yes. Salary is pensionable, so the corporation withholds the employee share of CPP, pays the employer share and remits both to CRA. Dividends are not subject to CPP. We include CPP when we compare salary and dividends for you.

#### Q: What changes for non-eligible dividends on January 1, 2027?

Ontario cuts its non-eligible dividend tax credit from 2.9863% to 1.9863%. The top Ontario marginal rate on non-eligible dividends rises from 47.74% in 2026 to 48.89%. If you were planning a dividend near year-end, we review whether paying it in December 2026 or January 2027 suits you better.

#### Q: Can you run payroll for my contractor corporation?

Yes. We set up salary, remit source deductions and file T4 slips for incorporated businesses. We work with corporations only, not individuals or sole proprietors, on a fixed flat fee. Please book a consultation and we will look at your pay mix with you.

## Page copy

H1: CRA PSB Review: What to Expect, Stage by Stage

A CRA PSB review checks whether your Ontario corporation's contract income is personal services business income. It usually starts with a letter or call and a questionnaire, then document requests, possibly interviews, a proposal letter and a notice of reassessment. The stake is 44.5% tax instead of 11.2%. We can act as your corporation's representative.

### H2: What is a CRA PSB review?

A CRA PSB review is an audit of your corporation's tax return that turns on one question: was the income from a personal services business (PSB)?

Most of the contractors I work with are IT and finance professionals. They bill through their own Ontario corporation, often through a staffing agency, for a bank, an insurer, a government or a large enterprise. That setup is legal and common. It is also the kind of arrangement the PSB rules were written for.

Section 125(7) of the Income Tax Act sets two conditions for a PSB in a year. You, the person doing the work, or someone related to you must be a specified shareholder, which means owning 10% or more of any class of shares, directly or indirectly. And you must reasonably be regarded as an officer or employee of the client if your corporation were not there. Two exceptions can apply: the corporation employs more than five full-time employees in the business throughout the year, or the income is received from an associated corporation. Whether either one fits needs review on the facts.

A one-person contractor corporation usually meets the first part, because you own the shares. So the review is really about the second part. To answer it, CRA uses the tests from Wiebe Door Services Ltd. v. M.N.R. and 671122 Ontario Ltd. v. Sagaz Industries Canada Inc. The central question is whose business it is. CRA looks at control, who owns the tools and equipment, your chance of profit and risk of loss, and whether your work is part of the client's business or your own. It also looks at hiring helpers, investment and management. The whole relationship is tested, year by year.

I explain the definition, the tests and the exceptions in more detail on our page about [personal services business risk](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-risk-incorporated-contractors/).

### H2: What is at stake if CRA decides your corporation is a PSB?

From July 1, 2026, the combined Ontario small business rate is 11.2%. PSB income does not get the small business deduction or the 13% federal general rate reduction, and it pays an additional 5% federal tax under section 123.5. The combined Ontario PSB rate is 44.5%.

A review looks at returns you have already filed, so earlier rates matter too. Income earned before July 1, 2026 was taxed at 12.2%. A taxation year ending December 31, 2026 gets a blended 11.70%.

Section 18(1)(p) also denies most expenses for a PSB. Salary and benefits paid to you stay deductible. Costs such as rent, software, equipment, travel, phone, insurance and accounting fees do not.

Here is one example for a 2025 year. The corporation billed $159,000 and had $9,000 of expenses. As filed, it paid $18,300, which is 12.2% of $150,000. Reassessed as a PSB, it pays $70,755, which is 44.5% of the full $159,000. That is $52,455 more for one year, before arrears interest. You can test your own numbers with our [PSB tax cost calculator](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-tax-cost-calculator/).

### H2: What are the stages of a CRA PSB review?

Every file is different, but a CRA PSB review usually moves through five stages. Here is what each one looks like in plain words.

#### H3: First contact: a letter or a call

The review usually starts with a letter, or a call from an auditor followed by a letter. It names your corporation and the years under review. It often includes a questionnaire about the working relationship: who sets your hours, who approves your time, whose laptop you use, whether you could send someone else to do the work, and whether you have other clients.

If someone calls saying they are from CRA, please do not share details until you have confirmed the call is genuine. Ask for the auditor's name and phone number, and send them to us.

Do not rush the questionnaire. Each answer is a fact CRA will rely on. Short, accurate answers backed by documents work better than long explanations.

#### H3: Requests for documents

CRA then asks for records. For a contractor corporation, the usual list is the agency agreement, statements of work, invoices, timesheets, onboarding emails from the end client, details of the equipment you use and who paid for it, and a list of other clients. The auditor may also ask for the general ledger and bank statements, as in any corporate audit.

The documents often say more than the questionnaire. An onboarding email that sets your daily stand-up, your reporting line and your required office days can say more about control than a clause that calls you an independent contractor.

#### H3: Interviews

The auditor may ask to interview you. The questions usually follow the same tests: what a normal week looks like, who supervises you, who decides how the work is done, who pays for tools, and what happens if work is late or wrong. CRA may also contact the agency or the end client for their view of the relationship. We set up the interview with the auditor in advance, go through the facts with you beforehand, and attend it with you as the corporation's representative.

#### H3: The proposal letter

If the auditor believes the income was from a PSB, CRA sends a proposal letter. It sets out the changes CRA plans to make, the years, the amounts and the reasons. It also states a deadline to respond. This is the stage to point out facts or documents the auditor has not considered, and to check the numbers. Please note the deadline the day the letter arrives.

#### H3: The notice of reassessment

If CRA does not change its view after the response, it issues a notice of reassessment for each year it changes. The notice shows the revised tax, the balance owing and arrears interest. A notice of objection is generally due within 90 days of the date on the notice of reassessment. That is a later stage in the CRA process.

For a Canadian-controlled private corporation, CRA can generally reassess a year within three years of the date of the original notice of assessment for that year. Some situations extend that period.

### H2: What does CRA ask for at each stage, and what do we prepare with you?

| Stage | What CRA usually asks for | What we prepare with you |
|---|---|---|
| First contact | A contact person for the corporation and answers to a working-relationship questionnaire | Our authorization as the corporation's representative, and draft answers built from your facts for you to confirm |
| Request for documents | Agency agreement, statements of work, invoices, timesheets, onboarding emails, equipment details and a list of other clients | An organized, indexed document package, with invoices matched to the books and any missing items noted |
| Interview | A walk through a normal week: supervision, schedule, tools, risk and other work | A review of the facts and documents with you before the interview, contact with the auditor about scope and timing, and attendance with you at the interview |
| Proposal letter | A written response by the deadline stated in the letter | A line-by-line review of the proposed changes and a written response sent before the deadline |
| Notice of reassessment | Payment of the balance owing, including arrears interest | A check of the notice against the proposal letter, and a plain explanation of each amount and date |

### H2: How do we support your corporation during a CRA PSB review?

Our support in a PSB review is the same CRA audit support and representation work we do for incorporated businesses. In practice, that means:

- You authorize us as the corporation's representative with CRA, so the auditor can deal with us directly.
- We organize the documents into a clear, indexed package.
- We respond to CRA requests and questionnaires, using facts you have confirmed.
- We communicate with the auditor, so you are not handling the calls and letters alone.
- We review the proposal letter, check every number in it, and respond in writing before the deadline.

We do not change facts. Our job is to make sure CRA sees the real facts clearly, with the documents that support them. I cannot promise how CRA will decide, and no honest adviser can.

We are accountants, not lawyers. Contract wording is for your lawyer. If you want to see how contract clauses are read for PSB indicators, our page on [PSB contract review](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/contract-review-psb-indicators/) explains it. For how we handle CRA audits more generally, please see our page on [CRA audit support for small businesses](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/cra-audit-support-for-small-businesses/).

We work with incorporated Canadian businesses only. In a PSB review, we act for the corporation.

### H2: What should you do first if CRA contacts you?

1. Read the letter and note the years under review and any deadline.
2. Confirm the contact is genuine before you share anything.
3. Do not guess or send partial answers. Gather your agency agreement, statements of work, invoices, timesheets and onboarding emails first.
4. Keep your records as they are. Do not rewrite or backdate anything.
5. Contact us early, so there is time to organize the documents before the first deadline.

We work with contractor corporations from our office at 55 Queen St E in Toronto. Our page for an [incorporated contractor accountant in Toronto](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/incorporated-contractor-accountant-toronto/) explains how we work with contractor corporations through the year, not only when CRA writes.

[TRUSTINDEX WIDGET]

If CRA has contacted your corporation about PSB status, please [book a consultation](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/consultation/) and bring the CRA letter, your agency agreement and your statements of work. You can also call us at 647-212-9559 or email info@gondaliyacpa.ca, 9AM to 8:30PM, Monday to Sunday.

## FAQs

#### Q: How does a CRA PSB review usually start?

Usually with a letter, or a call followed by a letter, naming your corporation and the years under review. It often includes a questionnaire about your working relationship: hours, supervision, tools, other clients and who bears the risk.

#### Q: What documents does CRA usually ask for in a PSB review?

The agency agreement, statements of work, invoices, timesheets, onboarding emails from the end client, details of your equipment and a list of other clients. The auditor may also ask for the general ledger and bank statements.

#### Q: How long do I have to respond to a CRA proposal letter?

The proposal letter states its own deadline, so please read it the day it arrives. If you need more time, we can ask the auditor before the deadline passes. CRA decides whether to allow it.

#### Q: What is the deadline after a notice of reassessment?

A notice of objection is generally due within 90 days of the date on the notice of reassessment. Arrears interest applies to the balance owing. Please do not wait until the end of that period to look at your options.

#### Q: How many years can CRA review?

For a Canadian-controlled private corporation, CRA can generally reassess a year within three years of the date of the original notice of assessment. Some situations extend that period. Each year is tested on its own facts.

#### Q: Is a CRA PSB review the same as a CPP/EI ruling?

No. Form CPT1 is used to request a CPP/EI ruling on a worker's status. That ruling deals with CPP and EI, not PSB status for income tax, though CRA looks at similar facts. We review whether it is useful in your facts.

#### Q: Can you promise CRA will accept our position?

No. Nobody can promise an outcome. We make sure CRA sees the real facts, organized and supported by documents, and we respond to each request and to the proposal letter on time.

#### Q: Do you represent individuals in a CRA PSB review?

No. We work with incorporated Canadian businesses only, and we do not take individuals or sole proprietors as clients for these services. In a PSB review, we act as the corporation's representative with CRA.
