## Page copy

H1: Incorporated Contractor Accountant in Toronto and the GTA

Gondaliya CPA is an incorporated contractor accountant in Toronto for IT and finance contractors with an Ontario corporation. We file the T2, keep the books, run payroll, file GST/HST and plan your tax, which includes reviewing PSB risk. Ontario taxes small business income at a combined 11.2% from July 1, 2026, and PSB income at 44.5%.

### H2: Who do we work with in Toronto and the GTA?

We work with IT and finance professionals who bill through their own Ontario corporation. On the IT side, that means developers, DevOps and cloud engineers, data specialists, cybersecurity consultants, QA testers, business analysts and project managers. On the finance side, it means FP&A analysts, accountants, controllers, and risk, audit and compliance specialists.

Many of these contractors work for banks, insurers, government and large enterprises through a staffing agency. Contracts are often set at 35, 37.5 or 40 hours a week, and they renew in stages. The work is steady, but the tax setup needs care every year.

If you work in technology, my page for an [accountant for IT contractors](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/accountant-for-it-contractors/) covers your setup in more detail. If you work in finance, my page for an [accountant for finance contractors](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/accountant-for-finance-contractors/) does the same.

Our office is at 55 Queen St E, Suite 1205, in downtown Toronto, close to the financial district. We work with incorporated contractors across the GTA, including:

- Downtown Toronto and the financial district
- North York, Etobicoke and Scarborough
- Mississauga and Brampton
- Markham, Vaughan and Richmond Hill
- Oakville

You can meet us in person at the office or online. Our hours are 9AM to 8:30PM, Monday to Sunday, so you can book a time outside your contract hours.

### H2: What does an incorporated contractor accountant in Toronto handle each year?

An Ontario contractor corporation has the same yearly filings as any other small corporation. It also carries one extra layer: PSB risk. Here is what you need each year and what we handle for each.

| What you need each year | Why it matters | What we handle |
|---|---|---|
| T2 corporate tax return | Your corporation files a T2 every year, even in a quiet year. It is where the small business deduction is claimed, or not. | We prepare and file the T2 and the year-end financial statements behind it. |
| Bookkeeping | Every number on your T2 and GST/HST returns comes from the books. CRA usually asks for them in a review. | We record income and expenses, reconcile the bank account and keep the records organized for year-end. |
| Payroll for salary | Salary paid to you is deductible to the corporation. It stays deductible even if CRA treats the corporation as a PSB. | We set up payroll, handle the remittances and issue your T4 slip. |
| GST/HST filings | Once registered, your corporation charges GST/HST on its invoices and must report and remit it on time. | We prepare and file your GST/HST returns and match them to the books. |
| Tax planning | The salary and dividend mix, the income you keep in the corporation and your PSB indicators all change your tax bill. | We plan your salary and dividend mix, and we review your contract and working facts as part of tax planning. |
| CRA support | CRA can send a letter, a questionnaire or a review request at any time. | We act as your corporation's authorized representative, respond to requests, organize documents and communicate with the auditor. |

We work on a fixed flat-fee model. We also work with incorporated businesses only. If you are still a sole proprietor and plan to incorporate, we can help with the incorporation first.

### H2: How does PSB risk show up for Toronto contractors?

A personal services business, or PSB, is defined in section 125(7) of the Income Tax Act. It applies when the person doing the work, called the incorporated employee, or someone related to them, owns 10% or more of any class of the corporation's shares, directly or indirectly. It also requires that the incorporated employee would reasonably be regarded as an officer or employee of the client if the corporation did not exist. I explain the full rule on my page about [personal services business risk](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-risk-incorporated-contractors/).

There are two exceptions. The first is a corporation that employs more than five full-time employees in the business throughout the year. That means at least six full-time employees, all year, and whether owners or family members count depends on their real roles, so it needs review. The second is income received from an associated corporation. For a one-person contractor corporation, neither one usually applies.

#### H3: The agency and the end client

Many Toronto contracts have three parties: your corporation, a staffing agency and the end client. CRA looks at whether you would reasonably be an employee of the party the services are really provided to. The agency contract and the end client's onboarding rules both matter. My page on [staffing agency contractor tax](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/staffing-agency-contractor-tax/) covers this setup.

#### H3: Hours, timesheets and equipment

Contracts at 35, 37.5 or 40 hours a week are common here. So are set schedules, timesheets approved by the end client, onsite days or VPN-only access, and client-supplied laptops. CRA reads these as control indicators.

Hours alone never decide PSB status. CRA tests the whole relationship, year by year. The tests come from the Wiebe Door and Sagaz cases, and the central question is whose business it is. The factors are control, ownership of tools and equipment, chance of profit and risk of loss, and whether the work is part of the client's business or your own.

#### H3: What PSB status would cost

The 44.5% PSB rate is 33% federal plus 11.5% Ontario. A PSB gets no small business deduction and no 13% federal general rate reduction, and it pays an extra 5% federal tax under section 123.5. The gap is large. Take $150,000 of income kept in the corporation:

- At the Ontario small business rate of 11.2%, the tax is $16,800.
- At the PSB rate of 44.5%, the tax is $66,750.
- The extra tax is $49,950 for one year.

The 11.2% rate applies from July 1, 2026. Income earned before that date was taxed at 12.2%. A corporation with a December 31, 2026 year-end gets a blended rate of 11.70%, which is $17,543.84 on the same $150,000.

PSB status also limits deductions under section 18(1)(p). Only a short list stays deductible: salary and other pay to you, benefits or allowances provided to you, certain costs of selling property or negotiating contracts that an employee could deduct, and legal costs to collect fees owed for your services. Rent, software, equipment, travel, phone, insurance and accounting fees are denied.

No one can promise how CRA will see your file. What we can do is review your facts each year, reduce risk where the facts allow it, and document your position.

### H2: How does a first consultation work?

#### H3: Before we meet

Please send what you have before the meeting. It helps to have:

- Your agency contract and any statement of work
- The end client's onboarding documents, if you received any
- Your last T2 and financial statements, if your corporation has filed before
- Payroll records and T4 slips, if you pay yourself a salary
- Your GST/HST account details
- Any letter or questionnaire from CRA

#### H3: In the meeting

You can meet me or my team in person at 55 Queen St E, Suite 1205, or online. We go through how your corporation earns income, how you are paid and how the work is done day to day. I tell you plainly what I see, including any PSB indicators in your facts.

We are accountants, not lawyers. If a clause in your contract needs new wording, that is for your lawyer.

#### H3: After the meeting

If you decide to work with us, we set up your file and confirm what we handle each year. You then have one firm for the T2, the books, payroll, GST/HST, tax planning and CRA support. When you are ready, you can [book a consultation](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/consultation/) online.

### H2: What if CRA has already contacted you?

Some contractors come to us after a CRA letter or a PSB questionnaire. We act as your corporation's authorized representative. We respond to CRA requests and questionnaires, organize your documents, communicate with the auditor, and review and respond to proposal letters.

My page on [CRA PSB review support](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/cra-psb-review-support/) explains what a review looks like. Please do not answer a PSB questionnaire in a hurry. Facts written down in a rush are hard to correct later.

### H2: Why do Toronto contractors choose a small CPA firm?

With a small firm, you deal with the same people every year. I am a CPA in Canada and the USA, with 6 years of cross-border experience and 100+ cross-border files. Our team includes a second designated CPA (CPA Ontario) and bookkeeping support staff.

We have 1300+ five-star Google reviews, and we are a 2026 Xero Awarded Firm.

Our hours, 9AM to 8:30PM, Monday to Sunday, suit contractors who work full days on a client site or a client VPN. You do not need to give up a billable day to meet us.

[TRUSTINDEX WIDGET]

If you are an incorporated contractor in Toronto or anywhere in the GTA, please [book a consultation](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/consultation/) or call 647-212-9559. Bring your contract and your last T2, and we will go through your file with you.

## FAQs

#### Q: Do you only work with contractors in downtown Toronto?

No. Our office is in downtown Toronto, but we work with incorporated contractors across the GTA, including North York, Etobicoke, Scarborough, Mississauga, Brampton, Markham, Vaughan, Richmond Hill and Oakville. Meetings can be in person or online.

#### Q: Can I meet you outside my contract hours?

Yes. Our hours are 9AM to 8:30PM, Monday to Sunday. You can book an evening or weekend time so you do not lose billable hours.

#### Q: Do you work with Toronto contractors who are not incorporated?

No. We work with incorporated Canadian businesses only. If you plan to incorporate, we can help with the incorporation and then look after the corporation from its first year.

#### Q: Will you review my agency contract for PSB risk?

Yes, as part of tax planning. We review your contract and your working facts together, because CRA looks at the whole relationship. We are accountants, not lawyers, so any change to contract wording is for your lawyer.

#### Q: How much more tax would my corporation pay as a PSB?

On $150,000 of corporate income, the tax at the 11.2% small business rate is $16,800. At the 44.5% PSB rate it is $66,750, which is $49,950 more. Deductions are also limited under section 18(1)(p).

#### Q: Do fixed contract hours make my corporation a PSB?

Not on its own. Fixed hours, timesheet approval and client-supplied equipment are control indicators, but hours alone never decide PSB status. CRA tests the whole relationship, year by year.

#### Q: What should I bring to a first consultation?

Please bring your agency contract and any statement of work, the end client's onboarding documents, your last T2 and financial statements, payroll records if you take a salary, your GST/HST details, and any CRA letters.

#### Q: Can you help if CRA sends a PSB questionnaire?

Yes. We act as your corporation's authorized representative, respond to the questionnaire and any CRA requests, organize your documents, communicate with the auditor, and review and respond to proposal letters.

## Post copy

H1: What Is a Personal Services Business in Canada? A CPA Explains for IT Contractors

What is a personal services business (PSB)? It is a business a corporation carries on by providing an individual's services, where that individual, or a related person, owns 10% or more of any class of its shares, and the individual would reasonably be the client's employee without the corporation. In Ontario, PSB income is taxed at 44.5%, not 11.2%.

**Key takeaways**

| # | Takeaway |
|---|---|
| 1 | A PSB is defined in section 125(7) of the Income Tax Act. It is a tax classification of the business your corporation carries on, not a separate kind of corporation. |
| 2 | The core test is easy to state: take your corporation away and ask whether you would reasonably be your client's employee. |
| 3 | The shareholder part is met by most IT contractors, because you or a related person usually owns 10% or more of the shares. |
| 4 | There are two exceptions: more than five full-time employees all year, or payment from an associated corporation. A one-person IT corporation rarely fits either. |
| 5 | The cost is large. On $171,000 of revenue with $9,000 of expenses and no salary, a PSB pays $57,951 more corporate tax in one year at 11.2% vs 44.5%. |

### H2: Why does the personal services business rule exist?

Canada gives small active businesses a low tax rate through the small business deduction. Without a special rule, an employee could set up a corporation, bill the employer through it, and pay corporate tax at the small business rate instead of personal tax on a salary.

The PSB rules are aimed at that situation. They do not turn your corporation's income into salary. The income stays business income of the corporation. What changes is the tax result: the small business deduction is gone, most expenses are denied, and an extra federal tax applies.

This matters to IT contractors because the usual setup can look a lot like a job. One long contract, through a staffing agency, with a bank, insurer, government or large enterprise, on a 35, 37.5 or 40 hour week. That is a legal and common way to work. It is also the pattern the definition was written to test.

### H2: What is a personal services business under section 125(7)?

In plain words, a personal services business is a business of providing services where both of these are true:

1. The individual who performs the services for the corporation, called the incorporated employee, or a person related to that individual, is a specified shareholder of the corporation.
2. The incorporated employee would reasonably be regarded as an officer or employee of the person or partnership receiving the services, if the corporation did not exist.

Even when both are true, the business is not a PSB if either exception applies. The first is that the corporation employs more than five full-time employees in the business throughout the year. The second is that the amount for the services is received from a corporation associated with it in the year.

Now let me take each piece in turn, using IT examples.

#### H3: Piece 1: Is it a business of providing services?

Your corporation sells your time and skill. A developer billing by the hour, a DevOps engineer running build pipelines, a data engineer building data flows, a cloud architect, a penetration tester, a QA analyst, a business analyst and a project manager are all providing services. Selling software licences or hardware would be a different kind of business. Almost every hourly IT contract meets this first piece.

#### H3: Piece 2: Who performs the services?

The incorporated employee is the person who actually does the work for the corporation. If the agency placement names you, and you are the one writing the code or running the tests, that is you.

The term is only a label. It does not mean CRA has decided you are an employee. It tells you whose working relationship gets tested in the next pieces.

#### H3: Piece 3: Are you a specified shareholder?

A specified shareholder owns 10% or more of the shares of any class of the corporation, directly or indirectly. Shares held by people you do not deal with at arm's length, such as your spouse, can count toward that 10%. The definition also covers the case where a person related to you, rather than you, is the specified shareholder.

So if you own 100% of your corporation, this piece is met. If you and your spouse each own 50%, it is met. If your spouse owns all the shares and you own none, it is still met, because a person related to you is a specified shareholder. For most one-person IT corporations, this piece is simply met.

#### H3: Piece 4: Would you reasonably be the client's employee?

This is the piece that decides most files. CRA imagines your corporation is gone and asks one question. If you did this same work directly for the client, would you be its employee, or would you be in business for yourself?

To answer it, CRA and the courts look at the whole working relationship. They use the approach from Wiebe Door Services Ltd. v. M.N.R. and 671122 Ontario Ltd. v. Sagaz Industries Canada Inc. The central question is whose business it is. I explain each factor in my post on the [Wiebe Door and Sagaz tests](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/wiebe-door-sagaz-tests-contractors/). Here is how the factors tend to show up in IT work:

- **Control.** Does the client's manager or scrum master assign your tickets, set your sprint work and approve your timesheets? Or do you agree on a deliverable and decide how and when to do it?
- **Tools and equipment.** Do you work on a client-issued laptop, inside the client's VPN, with the client's software licences? Or on your own equipment, with your own tools and licences?
- **Chance of profit and risk of loss.** Are you paid for every hour no matter what? Or can you earn more by working efficiently on a fixed-price piece of work, and lose money if you must fix defects on your own time?
- **Integration.** Are you part of the client's team, with a client email address, a badge and a place on its org chart? Or is the work part of your own business, which also serves other clients?

Hiring helpers, your own investment and how you manage the work count too. No one factor settles the answer.

CRA explains its approach in guide RC4110, Employee or Self-Employed? Form CPT1 is a request for a CPP/EI ruling on a worker's status. That ruling covers CPP and EI only. It does not decide PSB status for income tax, though CRA looks at similar facts.

#### H3: Piece 5: Does either exception apply?

The first exception needs more than five full-time employees in the business throughout the year. That means at least six people, full-time, for the whole year. A one-person IT corporation does not meet it. Whether you, your spouse or other family members count depends on your real roles, so this needs careful review in any file that comes close.

The second exception applies when the corporation is paid for the services by a corporation associated with it in the year. When you bill a staffing agency or an unrelated bank, the payer is normally not associated with your corporation, so this exception does not usually help.

### H2: How does each part of the definition apply to an IT contractor?

Here is the whole definition in one place, with what each element means for a typical IT contractor.

| Element of the definition | What the law looks at | What it means for an IT contractor |
|---|---|---|
| Business of providing services | The corporation earns income by supplying services | Hourly development, DevOps, data, cloud, cybersecurity, QA, BA or PM work meets this |
| Incorporated employee | The individual who performs the services for the corporation | You, the person named on the placement and doing the work |
| Specified shareholder | 10% or more of any class of shares, directly or indirectly, held by you or a related person | Met by most one-person IT corporations, including where a spouse holds the shares |
| Would reasonably be an employee | Control, tools, profit and loss, and integration, viewed as if the corporation did not exist | A client laptop, VPN-only access, assigned tickets and client-approved timesheets point one way. Your own tools, fixed deliverables and other clients point the other |
| Exception: more than five full-time employees | At least six full-time employees in the business throughout the year | Rarely available to a one-person IT corporation |
| Exception: associated corporation | Payment for the services comes from an associated corporation | Not usually relevant when you bill an agency or an unrelated end client |

#### H3: What about 35, 37.5 or 40 hour contracts?

Most IT contracts set fixed hours. Fixed hours of 35, 37.5 or 40 a week, set schedules, timesheet approval by the end client, onsite or VPN-only requirements and client-supplied laptops are control indicators. They add weight on the employee side of piece 4. But hours alone never decide PSB status. CRA tests the whole relationship, year by year.

#### H3: Does the staffing agency change who the client is?

Many IT contractors work in a three-party setup: your corporation, a staffing agency and the end client. CRA looks at whether you would reasonably be an employee of the party you really provide the services to. That may be the end client, whose manager directs your work, rather than the agency that pays your invoice. So the agency contract matters, and so do the end client's onboarding rules.

### H2: What does PSB status cost an IT contractor in Ontario?

The definition matters because of the cost. First, the rates. The current combined federal and Ontario small business rate is 11.2%, from July 1, 2026. Income earned before that date was taxed at 12.2%, and a corporation with a December 31, 2026 year-end gets a blended 11.70% for 2026. PSB income is taxed at a combined 44.5% in Ontario. I break those rates down in my post on the [PSB tax rate in Ontario](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-tax-rate-ontario-2026/).

Now a worked example. A developer bills $95 an hour for 37.5 hours a week over 48 weeks, so the corporation's revenue is $171,000. It has $9,000 of expenses, such as software, phone, internet, insurance and accounting fees. It pays no salary, and the example assumes a full taxation year at the 11.2% rate.

- **Not a PSB:** ($171,000 minus $9,000) x 11.2% = $18,144 of corporate tax.
- **PSB:** the $9,000 of expenses is denied under section 18(1)(p), so $171,000 x 44.5% = $76,095 of corporate tax.
- **Difference:** $57,951 more corporate tax in one year.

Same work, same client, same hours. The only change is how the relationship is classified.

Section 18(1)(p) limits a PSB's deductions to salary, wages and other remuneration paid to the incorporated employee, benefits or allowances provided to that person, certain costs of selling property or negotiating contracts that an employee could deduct, and legal costs to collect amounts owed for the services. Software, equipment, phone and similar costs are denied. Paying yourself a salary narrows the gap, because that salary stays deductible, but it does not close it. You can test your own figures with our [PSB tax cost calculator](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-tax-cost-calculator/).

There is one partial offset. PSB income does not get the small business deduction, so it adds to the general rate income pool (GRIP), and dividends paid from it can be designated eligible dividends. At top 2026 rates, $100,000 of PSB income costs $44,500 in corporate tax, plus $21,833.70 of personal tax on the $55,500 eligible dividend. That is $66,333.70 in total. The same $100,000 paid as salary would cost $53,530.

### H2: What does the definition not say?

A few beliefs come up often with IT contractors. None of them is in the definition.

- **"I am incorporated, so I am not an employee."** The definition assumes you are incorporated. It asks what you would be without the corporation.
- **"My contract says independent contractor."** The wording of the contract is considered, but CRA looks at how you actually work.
- **"The agency is my client, so only the agency contract counts."** CRA looks at the party you really provide services to, and that may be the end client.
- **"I had two clients this year, so I am fine."** Other clients help show you run your own business, but CRA still looks at how each engagement actually works.
- **"Status is decided once."** It is tested year by year. Facts can change when a contract renews or the end client changes its rules.

### H2: How can a CPA help with the definition question?

I cannot tell you from a blog post whether your corporation carries on a PSB. That depends on your contract and how you actually work, year by year. What we can do is look at it properly.

We review your contract and working facts as part of tax planning. We go through each piece of the definition, the control indicators, the agency structure and the hours clause. We are accountants, not lawyers, so contract wording is for your lawyer. We also prepare your T2 corporate tax return, keep your bookkeeping, run payroll, file GST/HST, and plan your salary and dividend mix. If CRA asks questions, we act as your corporation's authorized representative.

No one can promise that CRA will accept your position. We help you reduce the risk, document the facts and support your position. For the wider picture, please read our guide to [personal services business risk](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-risk-incorporated-contractors/). For ongoing help with your corporation, see our page for an [accountant for IT contractors](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/accountant-for-it-contractors/).

If you bill through your own corporation, please [book a consultation](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/consultation/) and we will look at your facts. We serve incorporated businesses only, on a fixed flat-fee model. You can also call 647-212-9559 or email info@gondaliyacpa.ca, 9AM to 8:30PM, Monday to Sunday.

## FAQs

#### Q: Is a personal services business the same as being an employee?

No. A PSB is a classification of your corporation's business for income tax. Your corporation still earns the income and files a T2 return. It loses the small business deduction, pays a combined 44.5% in Ontario, and can deduct only the limited items allowed by section 18(1)(p).

#### Q: Does owning less than 10% of the shares take me out of the definition?

Only if neither you nor anyone related to you is a specified shareholder. Shares can be held directly or indirectly, and shares held by a spouse or another related person can count. In a one-person IT corporation, this part of the definition is almost always met.

#### Q: Can an IT contractor with more than one client still carry on a PSB?

Yes. Other clients help show that you run your own business, but they do not settle the question. CRA looks at how each engagement actually works, year by year.

#### Q: What tax rate applies to PSB income in Ontario?

A combined 44.5%, made up of 33% federal tax and Ontario's 11.5% general rate. The current small business rate it replaces is 11.2%, which applies from July 1, 2026.

#### Q: Does the more-than-five-employees exception help a typical IT contractor?

Rarely. It needs more than five full-time employees in the business throughout the year, which means at least six. Whether owners or family members count depends on their real roles, so it needs review.

#### Q: Can a CPT1 ruling tell me whether my corporation is a PSB?

No. A CPT1 ruling deals with CPP and EI status for a worker. It is not a PSB ruling for income tax, although CRA looks at similar facts. We review whether a CPT1 request is useful in your facts.

## LinkedIn post (Blog2Social)

What is a personal services business? It is one of the first questions incorporated IT contractors ask me.

Section 125(7) of the Income Tax Act has two main parts. First, you or a related person owns 10% or more of any class of shares. Second, take your corporation away: would you reasonably be your client's employee?

If both are true and no exception applies, the corporation pays a combined 44.5% in Ontario instead of the current 11.2%, and most expenses are denied. On $171,000 of revenue with $9,000 of expenses, that is $57,951 more corporate tax in one year.

I wrote a plain walk-through of each piece of the definition, with IT examples. Please read it before your next contract renewal.

#ITContractors #CanadianTax #IncorporatedContractors

https://gondaliyacpa.ca/what-is-a-personal-services-business-canada/

## Post copy

H1: Does a 40-Hour Contract Make You an Incorporated Employee?

No. A 40-hour contract does not by itself make you an incorporated employee. Fixed hours are one control fact among many. Under section 125(7), CRA asks whether you would reasonably be the client's employee if your Ontario corporation did not exist. If so, its income is usually PSB income, taxed at 44.5% instead of 11.2%.

**Key takeaways**

| # | Takeaway |
|---|---|
| 1 | A 40-hour week is one control fact. It does not by itself make you an incorporated employee. |
| 2 | Section 125(7) asks whether you would reasonably be regarded as the client's officer or employee if your corporation did not exist. |
| 3 | The rule applies only when you, or a person related to you, own 10% or more of any class of shares. |
| 4 | In my example, $182,400 of billings with $9,000 of expenses costs $61,747.20 more in corporate tax for one year if the corporation is a PSB. |
| 5 | The test runs year by year. Clear records of scope, tools, other clients and financial risk support your position. |

### H2: Why do so many contractors ask this question?

Many contractors I work with have a contract that says 40 hours a week. Some say 37.5 or 35. They bill through their own Ontario corporation, often through a staffing agency, to a bank, an insurer, a government or a large enterprise.

Sooner or later, someone tells them that a 40-hour week makes them an "incorporated employee", and that CRA will tax their corporation as a personal services business (PSB).

That is half right. The term is real. It comes straight from the Income Tax Act. But the hours do not decide it. To see why, it helps to know what the term actually means.

### H2: What is an incorporated employee under section 125(7)?

Section 125(7) defines a personal services business. Inside that definition is the term "incorporated employee". It means the individual who performs the services on behalf of the corporation. For most contractor corporations, that person is you.

In everyday use, people say "incorporated employee" for someone who fails the PSB test: a person who would be the client's employee if the corporation were taken away. That is the sense of the question in the title.

Your corporation carries on a PSB in a year when two links are present, unless an exception applies. If either link is missing, the corporation is not a PSB.

#### H3: The specified shareholder link

The first link is ownership. You, or a person related to you, must be a specified shareholder of the corporation. That means owning 10% or more of any class of shares, directly or indirectly.

A person related to you includes your spouse. So putting the shares in your spouse's name does not break this link.

For almost every one-person contractor corporation, this link is present. You own the shares, and you do the work.

#### H3: The "if the corporation did not exist" question

The second link is the one that matters. Would you reasonably be regarded as an officer or employee of the person receiving your services if your corporation did not exist?

This is a thought experiment. Take the corporation out of the picture. Look at the working relationship that is left. If it looks like employment, you are an incorporated employee in that everyday sense, and the corporation's income from those services is PSB income unless an exception applies.

To answer it, CRA uses the tests the courts set out in Wiebe Door Services Ltd. v. M.N.R. and 671122 Ontario Ltd. v. Sagaz Industries Canada Inc. The central question is whose business it is. The main factors are:

- Control: who directs the work, and how it is done.
- Tools: who owns the equipment you use.
- Chance of profit and risk of loss.
- Integration, meaning whether your work is part of the client's business or part of your own business.

Hiring helpers, your own investment and how you manage the work also count. A contract that calls you an "independent contractor" does not settle it. What happens day to day counts more than the label. I explain the full set of rules, including what happens to deductions, on our page about [personal services business risk](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-risk-incorporated-contractors/).

### H2: Where do 40 hours fit in the incorporated employee test?

Hours sit under control. A 40-hour week tells me how much time the client is buying. It does not tell me who is in charge of the work.

What I look at is what sits around the hours:

- Are the 40 hours a billing cap, or a required schedule with set start and finish times?
- Does an end client manager approve your timesheet and your time off, the way they would for staff?
- Must you work onsite, or only through the client's VPN, on a client-supplied laptop?
- Does the client need to approve any extra hours?

Each "yes" adds weight to control. None of them answers the question alone. A 40-hour week with a clear statement of work, your own tools and real financial risk can still look like a business. A 35-hour week inside an open-ended role, managed like staff, can look like employment.

Many of these rules, like VPN-only access and client laptops, exist for security at banks, insurers and governments. They often apply to every outside firm. I still treat them as control indicators, but I note the reason.

The test also runs year by year. Each taxation year of your corporation is judged on its own facts. A renewal that moves you onsite full-time, or a new manager who assigns your daily tasks, can change the answer for that year.

I go through each hours clause, and what to document for it, on our page about [40 hour contract PSB risk](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/40-hour-contract-psb-risk/). I compare 35, 37.5 and 40 hour terms in a separate post on [contract hours and the control test](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/contract-hours-cra-control-test/).

### H2: Who is the "client" when you work through an agency?

Most of my IT and finance contractors work in a three-party setup: your corporation, a staffing agency and the end client. The agency signs your contract and pays your invoices. The end client manages the work.

In this setup, CRA looks at whether you would reasonably be an employee of the party your services are really provided to. That is usually the end client. So the agency contract matters, but so do the end client's onboarding rules, its security rules and the way its managers treat you day to day.

A clean agency contract does not help much if the end client runs you like staff. And a strict security policy at a bank does not, on its own, make you staff.

### H2: How does an employee-style 40-hour engagement compare with a business-style one?

Here are two engagements with the same 40-hour week. The hours are identical. The rest is not.

| Factor | Employee-style 40-hour engagement | Business-style 40-hour engagement |
|---|---|---|
| Scope | Open-ended role on a client team, tasks assigned daily | Statement of work with defined deliverables, milestones and acceptance terms |
| Schedule | Set start and finish times, set by the client | 40 hour cap, and you decide when the work is done around agreed meetings |
| Timesheets | End client manager approves hours and time off | Agency uses the timesheet for billing, and you give notice of time away for planning |
| Location and access | Onsite five days a week, client laptop, VPN only, as for staff | Client security rules apply to all vendors, and you work on your own equipment where allowed |
| Tools and costs | Client supplies everything, and the corporation pays for almost nothing | Corporation pays for its own equipment, software, licences, training and insurance |
| Other clients | One client for years, no effort to find others | Other clients in the year, or real efforts to find them |
| Substitution | Only you can do the work | A real right to send a qualified substitute, subject to security checks |
| Financial risk | Paid for every hour, no rework at your own cost | Unpaid rework, some fixed-price work, unpaid gaps between contracts |
| Integration | You manage client staff and sit on the org chart | You deliver a defined piece of work as an outside firm |

The left column points toward an incorporated employee. The right column gives you facts that can support your position. Neither column promises a result. Real files sit somewhere in between, and the same contract can move left or right from one year to the next.

### H2: What does it cost if you are treated as an incorporated employee?

Let me put numbers on it. Take a contractor billing $95 an hour, 40 hours a week, for 48 weeks.

$95 x 40 x 48 = $182,400 of billings.

Assume the corporation spends $9,000 on equipment, software, insurance and accounting. It pays no salary and keeps the income. Assume a full taxation year at the current 11.2% combined Ontario small business rate.

#### H3: Same billings, two results

- **Not a PSB:** ($182,400 - $9,000) = $173,400 x 11.2% = $19,420.80 of corporate tax.
- **PSB:** the $9,000 of expenses is denied under section 18(1)(p), so $182,400 x 44.5% = $81,168 of corporate tax.
- **Extra tax:** $81,168 - $19,420.80 = $61,747.20 for one year.

Why is the gap so large? A PSB loses most of its deductions, the small business deduction and the 13% federal general rate reduction. It also pays the additional 5% federal tax under section 123.5. That makes 33% federal plus the 11.5% Ontario general rate, or 44.5% in total.

A note on the rate. The 11.2% applies to income earned from July 1, 2026. Income earned before that date was taxed at 12.2%. If your corporation's year ends December 31, 2026, the blended rate is 11.70%. On the same $173,400, that works out to $20,280.67 using the exact day count.

#### H3: What changes if you pay yourself a salary?

Section 18(1)(p) still allows salary and other pay to the incorporated employee. So pay planning matters. Say the corporation pays you a salary of $120,000:

- **Not a PSB:** ($182,400 - $9,000 - $120,000) = $53,400 x 11.2% = $5,980.80.
- **PSB:** ($182,400 - $120,000) = $62,400 x 44.5% = $27,768.
- **Extra tax:** $21,787.20.

This leaves out employer CPP and does not show personal tax on the salary. Salary narrows the corporate gap. It does not close it.

#### H3: Does paying a dividend fix it?

Not fully. PSB income is not eligible for the small business deduction, so it is taxed at the full rate and adds to the general rate income pool (GRIP). Dividends paid from it can be designated eligible dividends. Integration still leaves a gap.

Take $100,000 of PSB income at top 2026 Ontario rates. Corporate tax is $44,500. The eligible dividend of $55,500 is taxed at 39.34%, or $21,833.70. Total tax is $66,333.70. The same $100,000 paid as salary at 53.53% costs $53,530. The PSB route costs $12,803.70 more.

### H2: Are there exceptions to the incorporated employee rule?

There are two. First, the corporation employs in the business, throughout the year, more than five full-time employees. That means at least six, full-time, all year. Whether owners or family members count depends on their real roles, so it needs review.

Second, the amount for the services is received from a corporation associated with yours in the year.

Most one-person contractor corporations that bill a staffing agency fit neither exception. So the "if the corporation did not exist" question is usually where the file is decided.

### H2: What can you do this year?

You cannot change the test. You can make sure the real facts are clear and on file. Each year, I suggest keeping:

- Your share register, so the ownership link is on record.
- Your agency contract and statement of work, with each extension or renewal.
- The end client's onboarding, security and equipment rules, with a note on which ones apply to every vendor.
- A short note on why your hours are fixed, such as a budget cap or a release date.
- Invoices that name deliverables as well as hours.
- Receipts for the equipment, software, training and insurance your corporation pays for, and proof of any other clients.

We review your contract and working facts as part of tax planning. We read the agency contract, the statement of work and the onboarding rules. We ask how the work happens day to day. We map those facts against control, tools, profit and loss, and integration. Then we look at your pay mix, because salary stays deductible even in a PSB. You can see how clauses are read on our [PSB contract review](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/contract-review-psb-indicators/) page, and in my post on [contract clauses that raise PSB risk](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/contract-clauses-psb-risk/).

CRA's guide RC4110, "Employee or Self-Employed?", sets out how CRA looks at a working relationship. Form CPT1 is used to request a CPP and EI ruling on a worker's status. A CPT1 ruling deals with CPP and EI. It is not a PSB ruling for income tax, though CRA looks at similar facts. We review whether it is useful in your facts.

We are accountants, not lawyers. If a clause needs new wording, that is for your lawyer.

If your contract says 40 hours and you want to know how your facts read, please [book a consultation](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/consultation/). Please have your agency contract, statement of work and onboarding package with you. You can also call us at 647-212-9559, 9AM to 8:30PM, Monday to Sunday.

## FAQs

#### Q: What is an incorporated employee?

It is the term section 125(7) uses for the individual who performs services on behalf of a corporation. If that person would reasonably be regarded as the client's employee without the corporation, and is a specified shareholder or related to one, the corporation carries on a PSB unless an exception applies.

#### Q: Does working 40 hours a week make me an incorporated employee?

Not by itself. Fixed hours are a control fact. CRA weighs control together with tools, chance of profit and risk of loss, and integration, and asks whose business it is.

#### Q: If my spouse owns the shares, can I still be an incorporated employee?

Yes. The ownership link is met when you or a person related to you, such as your spouse, owns 10% or more of any class of shares, directly or indirectly.

#### Q: When I work through an agency, who is the client for the test?

CRA looks at whether you would reasonably be an employee of the party your services are really provided to. That is usually the end client, so its onboarding rules and day-to-day management matter as much as the agency contract.

#### Q: How much more tax would a PSB pay on a 40 hour contract?

The combined PSB rate is 44.5%, compared with the 11.2% small business rate from July 1, 2026. On $182,400 of billings with $9,000 of expenses and no salary, the extra corporate tax is $61,747.20 for one year.

#### Q: Can my corporation still deduct my salary if it is a PSB?

Yes. Section 18(1)(p) allows salary and other pay to the incorporated employee, plus a few other items. Most other costs, such as rent, software, equipment, travel, phone, insurance and accounting fees, are denied.

#### Q: Is the incorporated employee question decided once for the whole contract?

No. Each taxation year of your corporation is tested on its own facts. A renewal, a new manager or a move to full-time onsite work can change the answer for that year.

## LinkedIn post (Blog2Social)

Does a 40-hour contract make you an incorporated employee?

Not by itself. Under section 125(7), the question is whether you would reasonably be regarded as the client's employee if your corporation did not exist. Fixed hours are one control fact. CRA also weighs tools, chance of profit and risk of loss, and whether the work is part of the client's business or your own.

Here is one example. On $182,400 of billings ($95 x 40 x 48) with $9,000 of expenses, an Ontario corporation pays $19,420.80 at 11.2%. As a PSB at 44.5%, with expenses denied, it pays $81,168.

In my new post, I explain the specified shareholder link, how agency setups are read, and what to keep on file each year.

## Post copy

H1: 35 vs 37.5 vs 40 Hours: What Your Contract Hours Signal to CRA Under the Control Test

In the contract hours control test, a 35, 37.5 or 40 hour week reads like a standard employee workweek, so CRA treats it as a control fact. Hours never decide PSB status on their own. CRA tests the whole relationship, year by year. In Ontario, the stakes are 11.2% corporate tax if not a PSB, and 44.5% if it is.

**Key takeaways**

| # | Takeaway |
|---|---|
| 1 | A fixed 35, 37.5 or 40 hour week matches common full-time employee schedules, so CRA reads it as a sign of control. |
| 2 | The number matters less than who decides when, where and how long you work. |
| 3 | Set schedules, core hours, overtime approval, timesheet approval and on-call rotations each add to or reduce the control picture. |
| 4 | Hours never decide personal services business (PSB) status alone. Control is one factor, weighed with tools, profit and loss, and integration. |
| 5 | More hours mean more revenue, so the PSB tax gap grows: $54,154.80 at 35 hours, $57,951 at 37.5 hours and $61,747.20 at 40 hours in my example. |

### H2: What is the contract hours control test, and where do hours fit?

A PSB exists under section 125(7) of the Income Tax Act when two things are true. First, the person doing the work for the corporation, or a person related to them, owns 10% or more of any class of its shares, directly or indirectly. Second, that person would reasonably be regarded as an officer or employee of the client if the corporation did not exist. The tax law calls that person the [incorporated employee](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/40-hour-contract-incorporated-employee/).

To answer the second question, CRA uses the tests from Wiebe Door Services Ltd. v. M.N.R. and 671122 Ontario Ltd. v. Sagaz Industries Canada Inc. The central question is whose business it is. The factors are control, ownership of tools and equipment, chance of profit and risk of loss, and integration into the client's business. Hiring helpers, investment and management also count. I explain each factor in my post on the [Wiebe Door test](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/wiebe-door-sagaz-tests-contractors/).

Hours sit inside the control factor. Control asks who decides what work gets done, how, when and where. A contract that fixes your weekly hours answers part of the "when" question before you start. That is why CRA pays attention to it. CRA guide RC4110 "Employee or Self-Employed?" sets out the same approach in plain terms.

This post stays on that narrow point: what each hours pattern tells CRA about control. For the wider question of what a 40 hour contract means for your corporation, please read our page on [40 hour contract PSB risk](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/40-hour-contract-psb-risk/).

### H2: Why do 35, 37.5 and 40 hour weeks read as control facts?

Because they are the shapes of a standard full-time job.

- **35 hours** is seven hours a day over five days.
- **37.5 hours** is seven and a half hours a day over five days. It is the shape of a 9AM start and a 5PM finish with an unpaid half-hour lunch.
- **40 hours** is eight hours a day over five days.

Each one mirrors a schedule many employers use for their own full-time staff. When a contractor's week matches the end client's own staff week, minute for minute, it suggests the contractor was slotted into the client's workforce. It does not look like a business selling a result.

Contrast that with how a business usually prices work. A firm quoting a project sets a price for a deliverable, or an estimate of hours it controls. It decides how many hours to spend. If it finishes early, it keeps the margin. If it runs late, it absorbs the cost. A fixed weekly hours term removes that choice.

#### H3: Is "up to 40 hours" different from "40 hours required"?

Yes, and the wording matters. A cap such as "not to exceed 40 hours a week without written approval" is often a budget limit. Clients control spend with every supplier, from software vendors to consulting firms. A requirement such as "the consultant will work 40 hours per week, Monday to Friday" reads as a direction about when you work.

In practice, CRA looks at what actually happens, not only the clause. If the contract says "up to 40 hours" but your manager expects you online from 9AM every weekday, the real facts carry more weight. Contract wording is for your lawyer. My role is to look at how the facts read for tax.

### H2: Which schedule terms add to the control picture?

The weekly number is only the start. These are the hours-related facts I look at when I review a contractor's file as part of tax planning.

#### H3: Set schedules

A set schedule tells you which days and which hours to work. Fixed start and end times, a required Monday to Friday pattern, and rules about when you can take time off all point toward control. A contractor who chooses their own working hours, within the delivery dates in the contract, is in a stronger position.

#### H3: Core hours

Many teams use core hours: a window each day when everyone must be available for meetings and questions. Some availability is normal for any supplier on a team project. A required daily window that matches the employee rule, enforced by the same manager, reads more like employment.

#### H3: Overtime approval

Employees often need a manager's approval to work extra hours, and many are paid a premium when they do. For a contractor, two facts matter. Does someone at the client tell you when to work extra hours? And are the extra hours paid at an overtime premium, like an employee, or billed at your normal rate under the contract? Approving extra spend is normal commercial practice. Directing your time looks like supervision.

#### H3: Timesheet approval

Almost every agency placement uses timesheets approved by a manager at the end client. That alone is not fatal. Any business billing by the hour needs the client to confirm the hours before it gets paid. The question is what the approval does. If it only confirms the billable hours, it reads as billing. If the manager uses it to review your daily attendance, your tasks and your performance, it reads as supervision.

#### H3: On-call and after-hours rotations

Production support, cloud and cybersecurity contractors often join on-call rotations. Being placed on the client's own rotation, with standby pay set by the client's policy, looks like what an employee would do. A support commitment written into your corporation's contract as a service, priced by your corporation, reads more like a business.

### H2: What does the hours pattern cost in tax at 35, 37.5 and 40 hours?

Here is the worked example. Same contractor, same $95 hourly rate, 48 billable weeks, three different weekly hours. My assumptions: $9,000 of business expenses, no salary paid to the contractor, and a full taxation year at the current 11.2% combined Ontario small business rate.

One note on rates. The 11.2% rate applies to income earned from July 1, 2026 (9% federal plus 2.2% Ontario). Income earned before that date was taxed at 12.2%, so a December 31, 2026 year-end gets a blended 11.70%. I use 11.2% here because it is the current rate. The PSB rate is 44.5%: 33% federal, including the additional 5% under section 123.5, plus the 11.5% Ontario general rate. If CRA finds a PSB, section 18(1)(p) denies the $9,000 of expenses. I assume they are ordinary costs such as software, equipment and phone, not salary or benefits for the incorporated employee.

**35 hours a week**

- Revenue: $95 x 35 x 48 = $159,600
- Not a PSB: ($159,600 minus $9,000) = $150,600 x 11.2% = $16,867.20
- PSB: $159,600 x 44.5% = $71,022
- Extra tax: $71,022 minus $16,867.20 = $54,154.80

**37.5 hours a week**

- Revenue: $95 x 37.5 x 48 = $171,000
- Not a PSB: ($171,000 minus $9,000) = $162,000 x 11.2% = $18,144
- PSB: $171,000 x 44.5% = $76,095
- Extra tax: $76,095 minus $18,144 = $57,951

**40 hours a week**

- Revenue: $95 x 40 x 48 = $182,400
- Not a PSB: ($182,400 minus $9,000) = $173,400 x 11.2% = $19,420.80
- PSB: $182,400 x 44.5% = $81,168
- Extra tax: $81,168 minus $19,420.80 = $61,747.20

| Weekly hours | Revenue | Tax at 11.2% (not PSB) | Tax at 44.5% (PSB) | Extra tax if PSB |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 35 | $159,600 | $16,867.20 | $71,022.00 | $54,154.80 |
| 37.5 | $171,000 | $18,144.00 | $76,095.00 | $57,951.00 |
| 40 | $182,400 | $19,420.80 | $81,168.00 | $61,747.20 |

Read this table carefully. The gap between 35 and 40 hours is $7,592.40 a year. That difference comes from revenue, not from a different PSB answer. A 35 hour week does not make you safer by $7,592.40. It only means there is less income exposed if the facts go against you.

That is the point of this post. Hours change the size of the exposure. The rest of the relationship decides whether the exposure becomes real. These figures are corporate tax only. They leave out personal tax on what you later take out of the corporation.

### H2: What might CRA read into each hours pattern, and what should you document?

| Hours pattern | What CRA may read into it | What to document |
|---|---|---|
| Fixed 35, 37.5 or 40 hours, Monday to Friday | The client sets when you work, like its own staff | Any choice you have over your daily hours, and work done on your own schedule |
| "Up to" a weekly cap | A budget limit, common with any supplier | Weeks where you chose to bill fewer hours, and why |
| Set start and end times | Direct control over your time | Written proof of any flexibility, such as emails agreeing to your schedule |
| Required core hours | Integration into the client's team routine | That availability is for project coordination, and how it is limited |
| Overtime approved by a client manager | Supervision of your time, if the manager directs it | That extra hours are billed at your contract rate, and who decided to work them |
| Timesheets approved by the end client | Attendance monitoring, if used to review your work | That approval only confirms billable hours for invoicing |
| On-call rotation set by the client | Treatment like a member of staff | That support is a service in your corporation's contract, priced by your corporation |
| Deliverable or milestone billing | Your corporation controls how much time a result takes | Statements of work, milestone sign-offs and any cost overruns you absorbed |

Documentation does not change the facts. It records them. If your real working pattern looks like employment, a file note will not fix that. If your pattern has real independence, a clean record supports your position when CRA asks.

### H2: Do hours ever decide PSB status on their own?

No. A contractor on a fixed 40 hour week who owns their tools, carries real financial risk, serves more than one client and controls how the work is done can have a strong position. A contractor on a loose 35 hour week who is directed daily, uses a client laptop and sits inside a client team can have a weak one. CRA weighs all of it, and it looks again each year.

Two other facts also sit outside the hours question. Your corporation is not a PSB if it employs more than five full-time employees in the business throughout the year, or if the amount it receives comes from a corporation associated with it. The first means at least six full-time employees all year, and whether owners or family members count depends on their real roles, so it needs a review. Few contractor corporations meet either exception, and a one-person corporation cannot meet the first.

For the full set of PSB risks beyond hours, please read our page on [PSB risk for incorporated contractors](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-risk-incorporated-contractors/).

### H2: What can you do about the hours facts in your contract?

Start with what is true. Then look at what you can honestly change at the next renewal or the next contract.

1. **Read the hours clause.** Is it a requirement or a cap? Does it name days and times?
2. **Compare it with how you really work.** If the contract and your week do not match, CRA will look at your week.
3. **Keep your own records.** Note the hours you chose, the work you did on your own time and the results you delivered.
4. **Talk to your lawyer about wording.** We are accountants, not lawyers. Contract wording is for your lawyer.
5. **Review the tax side with us.** We review your contract and working facts as part of tax planning, and we look at your pay mix at the same time.

Our firm works only with incorporated Canadian businesses, on a fixed flat-fee model. For contractors, that covers corporate tax returns (T2), bookkeeping, payroll, GST/HST filings, tax planning and CRA audit support and representation. If CRA sends your corporation a questionnaire about your working relationship, we act as its authorized representative, organize the documents and communicate with the auditor. If you work in development, DevOps, data, cloud, cybersecurity or QA, our page for an [accountant for IT contractors](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/accountant-for-it-contractors/) explains how we work with you.

If your contract says 35, 37.5 or 40 hours and you are not sure how it reads, please [book a consultation](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/consultation/). Please bring your contract, your statement of work and a typical week's timesheet. You can also call 647-212-9559 or email info@gondaliyacpa.ca.

## FAQs

#### Q: Does a 40 hour week decide the control test?

No. A fixed 40 hour week is a control indicator because it matches a standard employee schedule, but it does not decide the control test or PSB status alone. CRA weighs control with ownership of tools, chance of profit and risk of loss, and integration, year by year.

#### Q: Is a 35 hour contract safer than a 40 hour contract?

Not in itself. Both read as standard full-time schedules. A 35 hour week produces less revenue, so less income is exposed if CRA finds a PSB. At $95 an hour over 48 weeks, the extra tax is $54,154.80 at 35 hours and $61,747.20 at 40 hours in my example.

#### Q: Does timesheet approval by the end client decide the control question?

No. Any business billing by the hour needs the client to confirm hours before it is paid. What matters is what the approval is used for. Confirming billable hours reads as billing. Reviewing your attendance, tasks and performance reads as supervision.

#### Q: How does CRA view core hours and on-call rotations?

Core hours and on-call duties are control facts when the client sets them the same way it does for its own staff. They read more like a business service when they are written into your corporation's contract, priced by your corporation and limited to what the project needs.

#### Q: What tax rate applies if CRA decides my corporation is a PSB?

The combined Ontario PSB rate is 44.5%: 33% federal, including the additional 5% under section 123.5, plus the 11.5% Ontario general rate. If the corporation is not a PSB, the current combined small business rate is 11.2%. Section 18(1)(p) also denies most expenses for a PSB.

#### Q: Can you change my contract hours clause for me?

No. We are accountants, not lawyers, and contract wording is for your lawyer. We review your contract and working facts as part of tax planning, explain how the hours terms read for tax, and help you keep records that support your position.

## LinkedIn post (Blog2Social)

Many incorporated contractors sign for 35, 37.5 or 40 hours a week. To CRA, those numbers look like a standard employee workweek, so they count as a control fact.

They never decide personal services business status on their own. CRA weighs set schedules, core hours, overtime approval, timesheet approval and on-call rotations together with tools, risk and integration, year by year.

To put numbers on it: at $95 an hour over 48 weeks, a PSB finding adds $54,154.80 of corporate tax at 35 hours and $61,747.20 at 40 hours in my example, comparing 44.5% with the current 11.2% combined Ontario small business rate.

I wrote up what each hours pattern signals and what to document. Please read it here:
https://gondaliyacpa.ca/contract-hours-cra-control-test/

## Post copy

H1: PSB Tax Rate in Ontario for 2026: 44.5% vs 11.2% Explained With Numbers

The PSB tax rate in Ontario is 44.5% for 2026. A corporation that is not a PSB pays 11.2% on its first $500,000 of active business income earned from July 1, 2026. A December 31, 2026 year-end gets a blended 11.70%, because income earned before July 1, 2026 was taxed at 12.2%.

**Key takeaways**

| # | Takeaway |
|---|---|
| 1 | The combined Ontario PSB rate is 44.5%: 33% federal plus 11.5% Ontario. The July 1, 2026 cut did not change it. |
| 2 | The combined Ontario small business rate is 11.2% from July 1, 2026. It was 12.2% before that date. |
| 3 | A taxation year that straddles July 1, 2026 gets a day-weighted rate. For a December 31, 2026 year-end, that is 11.70%. |
| 4 | On $150,000 of corporate income, PSB treatment costs $49,950 more corporate tax than the 11.2% rate, before any denied expenses. |
| 5 | PSB income adds to GRIP, so eligible dividends can be paid from it. At top 2026 rates, the PSB route still costs $12,803.70 more per $100,000 than salary. |

### H2: What is the PSB tax rate in Ontario for 2026?

The combined federal and Ontario tax rate on personal services business income is 44.5% for 2026. That is 33% federal plus 11.5% Ontario.

I see a lot of incorporated contractors who know the small business rate well. Fewer know the PSB rate, because it only shows up when CRA decides your corporation is really standing in for an employee. If that happens, your corporation loses the low rate on its contract income and pays 44.5% instead. Most of its expenses are denied as well.

A personal services business exists under subsection 125(7) of the Income Tax Act when the person doing the work, or a person related to them, owns 10% or more of any class of shares, directly or indirectly, and that person would reasonably be regarded as an officer or employee of the client if the corporation did not exist. There are two exceptions: more than five full-time employees in the business throughout the year, or income received from an associated corporation. Whether owners or family members count toward the five needs review. Our post on [what is a personal services business](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/what-is-a-personal-services-business-canada/) walks through the definition for IT contractors.

This post is about the rate and the dollars. If you want to know where you stand on the facts, start with our guide to [personal services business risk](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-risk-incorporated-contractors/).

### H2: What changed on July 1, 2026?

The 2026 Ontario Budget cut Ontario's small business rate to 2.2%, down from 3.2%, starting July 1, 2026. The federal small business rate stays at 9%. So the combined Ontario small business rate is now 11.2%. Before July 1, 2026, it was 12.2%.

The small business limit did not change. It is still $500,000 of active business income a year, shared by associated corporations. Income above that limit is still taxed at the general rate of 26.5% (15% federal plus 11.5% Ontario).

The PSB rate did not change either. A PSB never had access to the small business rate, so a cut to that rate does nothing for it. The result is a wider gap. Before July 1, 2026, the gap between the PSB rate and the small business rate was 32.3 percentage points. From July 1, 2026, it is 33.3 points.

Here is the current comparison in one line: **11.2% vs 44.5%**.

#### H3: Why does a December 31, 2026 year-end get 11.70%?

The Ontario cut is prorated by days for taxation years that straddle July 1, 2026. A calendar-year corporation with a December 31, 2026 year-end has 365 days in its taxation year:

- January 1, 2026 through June 30, 2026: 181 days at 12.2%.
- July 1, 2026 through December 31, 2026: 184 days at 11.2%.

The day-weighted rate is 11.69589%, which rounds to 11.70%. On $150,000 of small business income, that is $17,543.84 of corporate tax.

#### H3: What rate applies to other year-ends?

It depends on how many days of the taxation year fall before July 1, 2026.

- A June 30, 2026 year-end falls fully before the cut. The whole year is taxed at 12.2%.
- A March 31, 2027 year-end runs from April 1, 2026. That gives 91 days at 12.2% and 274 days at 11.2%, a blended 11.45% (11.44932% exact).
- A June 30, 2027 year-end is the first 12-month year that falls fully after the cut. It gets the full 11.2%.

Our [PSB tax cost calculator](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-tax-cost-calculator/) does this proration for any 12-month year-end you enter, and shows the PSB figure next to it.

### H2: How is the 44.5% PSB rate built?

The 44.5% comes from four federal pieces and one Ontario piece. Here is how I explain it to clients.

1. **Start with the federal base rate of 38%.** Every corporation starts here.
2. **Take off the 10% provincial abatement.** This leaves room for the provinces to tax corporate income. The federal rate is now 28%.
3. **No 13% general rate reduction.** A regular corporation earning income above the small business limit gets this reduction, which brings it to 15% federal. PSB income is excluded from it. It also gets no small business deduction, which is what brings small business income down to 9%.
4. **Add the 5% additional tax under section 123.5.** This tax applies only to PSB income. The federal rate is now 33%.
5. **Add Ontario's 11.5% general rate.** Ontario's small business rate is not available on PSB income either.

So 28% plus 5% is 33% federal, and 33% plus 11.5% is 44.5%.

Compare that with the 26.5% general rate. A regular corporation over the small business limit pays 15% federal (28% less the 13% reduction) plus 11.5% Ontario. The PSB pays 18 percentage points more than that, because it loses the 13% reduction and adds the 5% tax.

### H2: How much tax is that in dollars?

Here is the same $150,000 of retained corporate income taxed at each rate. This assumes no salary and no expenses, so you can see the rate effect alone.

| Rate | When it applies | Corporate tax on $150,000 | Extra tax if PSB instead |
|---|---|---|---|
| 12.2% | Small business income earned before July 1, 2026 | $18,300 | $48,450 |
| 11.70% | Blended rate, December 31, 2026 year-end (11.69589% exact) | $17,543.84 | $49,206.16 |
| 11.2% | Small business income from July 1, 2026 | $16,800 | $49,950 |
| 26.5% | General rate on active business income above $500,000, not a PSB | $39,750 | $27,000 |
| 44.5% | PSB income | $66,750 | Not applicable |

At the current rates, PSB treatment costs $49,950 more on $150,000. That is the cost of the rate alone.

#### H3: What happens to expenses in a PSB?

The real gap is usually bigger, because paragraph 18(1)(p) denies most PSB expenses. A PSB can deduct salary, wages or other remuneration paid to the incorporated employee, the cost of benefits or allowances for that person, certain costs of selling property or negotiating contracts that an employee could deduct, and legal costs to collect amounts owed for services. Rent, software, equipment, travel, phone, insurance and accounting fees are denied.

Take a contractor billing $95 an hour for 37.5 hours a week over 48 weeks. That is $171,000 of revenue. Say the corporation has $9,000 of expenses and pays no salary, in a full year at 11.2%.

- Not a PSB: $162,000 x 11.2% = $18,144.
- PSB: expenses denied, so $171,000 x 44.5% = $76,095.
- Extra corporate tax: $57,951.

CRA can also reassess more than one year, and each year is tested on its own facts. Our post on [PSB reassessment](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-reassessment-denied-deductions/) explains how denied deductions and back years add up.

### H2: How do GRIP and eligible dividends work for PSB income?

Corporate tax is only half the story. The money still has to come out of the corporation to you.

When a corporation earns small business income at 11.2%, the dividends it pays from that income are usually non-eligible dividends. Those are taxed at up to 47.74% in Ontario for 2026.

PSB income is different. It is not eligible for the small business deduction, so it is taxed at the full rate. Income taxed at the full rate adds to the corporation's general rate income pool (GRIP). Dividends paid out of GRIP can be designated as eligible dividends, which are taxed at up to 39.34% in Ontario for 2026.

That lower personal rate is meant to make up for the higher corporate rate. For PSB income, it does not make up for all of it.

#### H3: Does integration close the gap?

No. Here is $100,000 of PSB income paid out to an owner at the top 2026 Ontario rates.

- Corporate tax at 44.5%: $44,500.
- Left to pay out: $55,500, as an eligible dividend.
- Personal tax on the dividend at 39.34%: $21,833.70.
- Total tax: $66,333.70, or 66.33% of the $100,000.

Now pay the same $100,000 as salary instead. Salary is deductible to the corporation, even for a PSB, so there is no corporate tax on it. The personal tax at 53.53% is $53,530.

The PSB route costs $12,803.70 more on every $100,000. The 5% additional tax and the lost 13% reduction are the main reasons.

This is why the salary and dividend mix matters so much when PSB risk is real. Salary is one of the few deductions 18(1)(p) still allows, so it takes income out of the 44.5% bracket. But salary brings payroll costs and is taxed in your hands. Our page on [salary vs dividend for incorporated contractors](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/salary-vs-dividend-incorporated-contractor/) covers how we think about that mix.

### H2: What changes for non-eligible dividends on January 1, 2027?

Starting January 1, 2027, Ontario cuts its non-eligible dividend tax credit to 1.9863%, down from 2.9863%. The top Ontario rate on non-eligible dividends rises to 48.89%, up from 47.74% in 2026.

The two Ontario changes work together. The corporation pays less tax on small business income from July 1, 2026. Then the owner pays more personal tax when that income comes out as a non-eligible dividend from January 1, 2027.

In dollars, at the top rate:

- A $50,000 non-eligible dividend paid in 2026: $23,870 of personal tax.
- The same dividend paid in 2027: $24,445 of personal tax.
- Difference: $575.

Eligible dividends are not affected by this change. So the 39.34% in the integration example above is not affected either.

If you take non-eligible dividends, the timing of your 2026 and 2027 payouts is worth a look before December 31, 2026. Whether paying earlier helps depends on your other income, your cash needs and your corporation's year-end. We work through that as part of tax planning.

### H2: What does this mean for an IT or finance contractor?

The rate tells you what is at stake. It does not tell you whether you are a PSB. That depends on the whole working relationship, looked at year by year.

CRA applies the Wiebe Door and Sagaz tests. It looks at control, ownership of tools and equipment, chance of profit and risk of loss, and whether your work is part of the client's business or your own. Fixed 35, 37.5 or 40 hour weeks, timesheet approval by the end client, VPN-only access and client laptops all point toward control. Hours alone never decide it. In an agency setup, CRA asks whether you would reasonably be an employee of the party you really provide services to. The agency contract and the end client's onboarding rules both matter.

Three things I suggest to every contractor who reads a post like this:

- **Know your number.** Run your own revenue, expenses, salary and year-end through the calculator.
- **Look at your facts once a year.** Contracts change. A renewal with a new end client can change the picture.
- **Keep your records.** Contracts, invoices, other clients, your own equipment and your own business costs all help support your position.

None of this makes PSB risk go away. It helps you reduce risk and document where you stand.

### H2: How can we help with PSB tax planning?

We work with incorporated IT and finance contractors across Ontario on a fixed flat fee. We prepare corporate tax returns (T2), keep the books, run payroll with T4 slips and remittances, and file GST/HST. We review your contract and working facts as part of tax planning, and we plan your salary and dividend mix with the 44.5% rate in mind. If CRA sends a PSB questionnaire, we support you as the corporation's authorized representative.

We are accountants, not lawyers. Contract wording is for your lawyer.

Please [book a consultation](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/consultation/) if you want us to look at your numbers and your contract together. You can also call us at 647-212-9559 or email info@gondaliyacpa.ca. We are open 9AM to 8:30PM, Monday to Sunday. Please bring your latest T2 return and your current contract so we can start with your real figures.

## FAQs

#### Q: What is the PSB tax rate in Ontario for 2026?

The combined federal and Ontario PSB rate is 44.5%. That is 33% federal (38% less the 10% abatement, with no 13% general rate reduction, plus the 5% additional tax under section 123.5) and 11.5% Ontario.

#### Q: Did the July 1, 2026 Ontario rate cut lower the PSB rate?

No. The cut lowered the Ontario small business rate to 2.2%, down from 3.2%, so the combined small business rate is now 11.2%. A PSB cannot use the small business rate, so its rate stays at 44.5%.

#### Q: Why is my December 31, 2026 year-end rate 11.70% and not 11.2%?

The cut is prorated by days. Your taxation year has 181 days before July 1, 2026 at 12.2% and 184 days from July 1, 2026 at 11.2%. The day-weighted rate is 11.69589%, shown as 11.70%.

#### Q: Can a PSB pay eligible dividends?

Yes. PSB income is not eligible for the small business deduction, so it is taxed at the full rate and adds to the general rate income pool (GRIP). Dividends paid from GRIP can be designated as eligible dividends.

#### Q: Does paying myself a salary reduce PSB tax?

Salary paid to you is still deductible for a PSB under 18(1)(p), so it reduces the income taxed at 44.5%. It does not change whether the corporation is a PSB, and the salary is taxed in your hands.

#### Q: What changes for non-eligible dividends on January 1, 2027?

Ontario cuts its non-eligible dividend tax credit to 1.9863%, down from 2.9863%. The top Ontario rate on non-eligible dividends rises to 48.89%, up from 47.74% in 2026. Eligible dividends are not affected.

## LinkedIn post (Blog2Social)

Ontario cut its small business rate on July 1, 2026. The combined rate is now 11.2%, down from 12.2%.

The PSB rate did not move. It is still 44.5%.

So the gap for incorporated contractors got wider. On $150,000 of corporate income, PSB treatment now costs $49,950 more in corporate tax. Add denied expenses under 18(1)(p) and the gap grows.

A December 31, 2026 year-end gets a blended 11.70%. And from January 1, 2027, the top Ontario rate on non-eligible dividends rises to 48.89%.

I wrote up how the 44.5% is built, how GRIP and eligible dividends fit in, and what integration still leaves on the table, with exact numbers.
