# Post 5: /wiebe-door-sagaz-tests-contractors/

## Brief

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Final SEO title | Wiebe Door Test: The Four Tests CRA Uses for Contractors (56 characters) |
| Slug | /wiebe-door-sagaz-tests-contractors/ |
| Meta description | The Wiebe Door test and Sagaz in plain words: control, tools, profit and loss, and integration, applied step by step to a hypothetical IT contractor. (149 characters) |
| Primary keyword | Wiebe Door test |
| Secondary keywords | Sagaz test, four-in-one test, employee or self-employed, chance of profit and risk of loss, integration test, RC4110 |
| Search intent | Informational: an incorporated IT or finance contractor in Ontario wants to understand the tests CRA uses to decide whether they are in business on their own account, and what those tests mean for personal services business risk. |
| Featured image headline | Line 1: The Wiebe Door Test / Line 2: The Four Tests CRA Uses |
| Author and review line | Written by Sharad Gondaliya, CPA (Canada and USA). Reviewed by Sharad Gondaliya, CPA, Founder and Managing Director, Gondaliya CPA Professional Corporation, CPA Ontario Firm ID 61330051. Last updated: October 16, 2026. |
| Publish date | 2026-10-16 |

## Post copy

H1: The Four Tests CRA Uses: Wiebe Door and Sagaz Applied to a Contractor

The Wiebe Door test is the set of factors CRA uses to decide whether a worker is an employee or in business on their own account. Sagaz frames the central question: whose business is it? CRA weighs control, tools, chance of profit and risk of loss, and integration. For an Ontario contractor corporation, that means 11.2% or 44.5% tax.

**Key takeaways**

| # | Takeaway |
|---|---|
| 1 | The central question is whose business it is. The four tests help answer it. They are not a scorecard. |
| 2 | Control, ownership of tools, chance of profit and risk of loss, and integration are weighed together, along with hiring helpers, investment and management. |
| 3 | An hourly contract with no downside looks like a wage. A fixed-price deliverable where overruns cost the corporation looks like a business, but only if the risk is real. |
| 4 | The same tests answer the PSB question under section 125(7): would you be an employee of the client if your corporation did not exist? |
| 5 | The answer decides whether the same income is taxed at 11.2% or 44.5%, and CRA looks at each year on its own facts. |

### H2: What is the Wiebe Door test, and what did Sagaz add?

Wiebe Door Services Ltd. v. M.N.R. is a Federal Court of Appeal decision. It is the source of the four factors most people use to decide whether a person is an employee or is in business for themselves:

1. Control
2. Ownership of tools
3. Chance of profit and risk of loss
4. Integration

People often call it the four-in-one test, because the factors are read together, not one at a time.

671122 Ontario Ltd. v. Sagaz Industries Canada Inc. is a Supreme Court of Canada decision. Sagaz was not a tax case, but its statement of the test is now used in tax and in CPP and EI status work. It put the central question in one line: is the person performing the services as a person in business on their own account?

Sagaz also made two points I come back to with every contractor. First, there is no single test that settles the answer. Second, the weight of each factor depends on the facts. Control is always relevant. Other factors include who provides the equipment, whether the worker hires helpers, how much financial risk the worker takes, how much the worker invests and manages, and whether the worker can profit from doing the work well.

CRA's guide RC4110 "Employee or Self-Employed?" sets out how CRA applies these factors. CRA also asks what the parties intended when they set up the relationship, then checks whether the day-to-day facts support that intention. A contract that calls you an independent contractor helps only if the facts match it.

### H2: Why do these tests matter for an incorporated contractor?

Most IT and finance contractors in Ontario do not work in their own name. They bill through their own corporation. That is where the personal services business (PSB) rules in section 125(7) of the Income Tax Act come in.

A corporation carries on a PSB when two things are true:

- The person who does the work (the "incorporated employee"), or a person related to them, is a specified shareholder. That means owning 10% or more of any class of shares, directly or indirectly.
- That person would reasonably be regarded as an officer or employee of the client if the corporation did not exist.

The second part is the Wiebe Door question, asked as if the corporation were not there. If the answer is "employee", the corporation is a PSB unless an exception applies. There are two. One is employing more than five full-time employees in the business throughout the year, which means at least six. The other is earning the income from a corporation associated with yours.

When you work through a staffing agency, CRA looks at whether you would reasonably be an employee of the party you really provide the services to. The agency contract and the end client's onboarding rules both matter.

The tax difference is large. A corporation that is not a PSB pays 11.2% combined Ontario tax on its first $500,000 of active business income earned from July 1, 2026. Income earned before that date was taxed at 12.2%, and a December 31, 2026 year-end gets a blended 11.70%. A PSB pays 44.5%. It loses the small business deduction and the 13% federal general rate reduction, and it pays an additional 5% federal tax under section 123.5. Section 18(1)(p) also denies most of its expenses.

If you are new to these rules, please start with our post on [what is a personal services business](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/what-is-a-personal-services-business-canada/). For the full cost picture, see our page on [personal services business risk](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-risk-incorporated-contractors/).

### H2: Who is our hypothetical contractor?

**Hypothetical example.** The engineer below is not a client. The facts below are invented to show how the tests work.

Take a data engineer who owns 100% of the shares of an Ontario corporation and has no employees. The corporation signs a contract with a staffing agency, and the agency places the engineer with a large bank. The facts for the year:

- The corporation bills $95 an hour for 37.5 hours a week over 48 weeks: $171,000.
- The bank's manager approves the engineer's timesheet every week.
- The engineer uses a bank laptop, the bank's VPN, a bank email address and a bank access badge.
- The bank's scrum master runs the daily standup and the two-week sprints. Work comes to the engineer as tickets in the bank's system.
- The engineer is on the bank's on-call rotation, set by the bank's team lead.
- The engineer's corporation pays for its own laptop, a cloud account and a certification course.
- The corporation has no other clients this year.

Now let me apply each test.

### H2: Step 1: Who controls the work?

Control asks who decides what work gets done, when, where and how. The question is who has the right to control, not only whether the client uses that right. Skilled IT people often choose the technical approach themselves. That does not settle the question if the client can still direct their time and tasks.

For the engineer, the employee-like facts are strong. The bank sets the hours and approves the timesheet. The bank's scrum master runs the ceremonies. Tickets decide what the engineer works on next. The on-call rotation is set by the bank.

The business-like facts are thinner. The engineer designs the data pipelines without being told how. The bank checks that the pipelines work, not how the code is written.

On these facts, control points toward employment. Fixed hours alone never decide PSB status, though. The way a contract is worded can add to or ease these indicators, which is why we wrote about the [contract clauses that raise PSB risk](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/contract-clauses-psb-risk/).

### H2: Step 2: Who owns the tools?

This test asks who supplies and pays for what the work needs. A worker who invests in the main tools of the job looks more like a business. A worker who uses only what the client hands out looks more like staff.

In IT, banks and insurers often require their own laptop and VPN for security reasons. So we ask a further question: does that rule apply to every vendor, including large consulting firms? If it does, the client laptop says less about the engineer and more about the bank's security policy.

For the engineer, the bank supplies the laptop, the VPN and the software licences used on the job. The engineer's own laptop and cloud account are used for training and testing ideas, not for bank work. The tools test points toward employment, though less strongly than control.

### H2: Step 3: Does the engineer have a chance of profit and a risk of loss?

This test asks whether the worker's own decisions can raise or lower what they earn, and whether they can lose money. A wage has no downside. A business does.

Here is a worked example. It compares the engineer's real contract (hourly) with a version where the corporation agrees a fixed price for the same deliverable. The deliverable is a data pipeline the bank expects to take 600 hours.

**Version A: hourly contract (the engineer's real contract)**

- On plan, 600 hours: 600 x $95 = $57,000.
- Finished early, 500 hours: 500 x $95 = $47,500.
- Overrun, 750 hours: 750 x $95 = $71,250. The extra 150 hours earn 150 x $95 = $14,250.

The bank carries the cost of the overrun. The engineer's corporation cannot lose money on the work, and finishing early only means billing less.

**Version B: fixed-price deliverable**

The corporation quotes $57,000 to build and deliver the same pipeline. It pays $3,000 for its own cloud test environment, so it keeps $54,000 whatever the hours.

- On plan, 600 hours: $54,000 / 600 = $90 per hour.
- Finished early, 500 hours: $54,000 / 500 = $108 per hour. That is $6,500 more than hourly billing for the same 500 hours ($54,000 - $47,500).
- Overrun, 750 hours: $54,000 / 750 = $72 per hour. That is $17,250 less than hourly billing for the same 750 hours ($71,250 - $54,000).

Version B has a real chance of profit: finishing early lifts the return to $108 an hour, against $90 on plan. It also has a real risk of loss: an overrun drops the return to $72 an hour, and the corporation absorbs 150 hours it cannot bill.

The engineer's real contract is Version A, so this test points toward employment.

One caution. Moving to a fixed price only helps if the price, the scope and the risk are real. A contract that says "fixed price" while the bank still approves weekly timesheets and pays for every extra hour tells CRA very little.

### H2: Step 4: Is the work part of the bank's business or the engineer's own?

Integration asks, from the worker's side, whether the work is part of their own business or is done as part of the client's organization.

For the engineer, the work sits inside the bank. The engineer has a bank badge and email address, attends the same ceremonies as the bank's own staff, and has no other clients this year. The corporation does no marketing and does not look for other work. This points toward employment.

### H2: What about helpers, investment and management?

Sagaz adds three more factors.

Hiring helpers: bank contracts often name the individual and require a background check, so the engineer cannot send someone else or hire help. That points toward employment.

Investment: the engineer's corporation has spent a modest amount on its own laptop, a cloud account and a course. That is small next to what the bank provides.

Management: the engineer's corporation issues invoices, keeps books and files its returns. Nearly every contractor corporation does these things, so on their own they carry little weight.

### H2: How do the tests compare side by side?

| Test | Employee-like facts | Business-like facts |
|---|---|---|
| Central question: whose business is it? | The work is done as part of the client's operation | The work is done as part of the contractor's own business |
| Control | Fixed hours, timesheets approved by the client, standups and sprints run by the client, tasks assigned through client tickets, on-call set by the client | Agreed scope and deliverables, the contractor sets the schedule and methods, the client checks the result rather than the process |
| Ownership of tools | Client laptop, VPN, software licences and access badge | The corporation supplies and pays for the main equipment, software and environments used in the work |
| Chance of profit and risk of loss | Paid by the hour, client pays for overruns, no real costs, no way to lose money | Fixed price, the corporation's own costs, overruns and rework at the corporation's cost, more profit from working well |
| Integration | Client email and badge, part of a client team, one client all year | Several clients, work found and quoted by the corporation, a business that carries on between contracts |
| Helpers, investment and management | Named individual, no substitute allowed, little investment | Can hire or subcontract, invests in equipment and training, manages its own business risk |

### H2: What does the full picture say for the engineer?

CRA weighs the tests together and asks the central question. On the engineer's facts, most factors point toward employment. The engineer owns all the shares, has no employees and does not work for an associated corporation, so no exception applies. The PSB risk on these facts is high.

Here is the tax cost for one full taxation year at the current 11.2% rate. Revenue is $171,000 and expenses are $9,000, with no salary paid.

- Not a PSB: ($171,000 - $9,000) x 11.2% = $18,144
- PSB: expenses denied under section 18(1)(p), so $171,000 x 44.5% = $76,095
- Extra tax: $57,951

CRA looks at each year on its own facts. A new contract, a renewal or a change in how the work runs can change the answer.

### H2: What can an incorporated contractor do with this?

You cannot make the PSB question go away. You can understand your facts, document them and reduce risk where the facts allow it.

- Write down the real working facts each year: who sets the hours, whose tools you use and who carries the cost when work runs over.
- Keep records of the business features that are real: other clients, your own equipment, quotes, and fixed-price work you have delivered.
- Have your lawyer review contract wording. We are accountants, not lawyers, and contract wording is for your lawyer.
- Ask us to look at the whole picture. We review your contract and working facts as part of tax planning. You can read more about our [PSB contract review](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/contract-review-psb-indicators/).

You may also hear about Form CPT1. It is used to request a CPP/EI ruling on a worker's status. A CPT1 ruling deals with CPP and EI. It is not a PSB ruling for income tax, though CRA looks at similar facts. We review whether it is useful in your facts.

The same tests apply to FP&A, audit, risk and compliance contractors placed at banks through agencies. If that is your work, please see our page for an [accountant for finance contractors](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/accountant-for-finance-contractors/).

If you would like us to walk through these tests with your own contract and working facts, please [book a consultation](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/consultation/). You can also call 647-212-9559 or email info@gondaliyacpa.ca.

## FAQs

#### Q: What is the Wiebe Door test?

It is a set of factors used to decide whether a worker is an employee or is in business on their own account. The four main factors are control, ownership of tools, chance of profit and risk of loss, and integration. They are weighed together, not one at a time.

#### Q: How does Sagaz relate to the Wiebe Door test?

Sagaz states the central question: is the person performing the services as a person in business on their own account? It also makes clear that no single factor decides the answer, and it adds hiring helpers, investment and management to the factors CRA weighs.

#### Q: Which of the four tests matters most?

None of them decides the answer alone. Control is always relevant, but the weight of each factor depends on the facts of the relationship. CRA looks at the whole picture, year by year.

#### Q: Does a client-supplied laptop make me an employee?

Not by itself. It is a tools indicator. We look at whether the client's device rule is a security policy that applies to every vendor, and at which tools your corporation actually pays for and uses in the work.

#### Q: Will switching to a fixed-price contract reduce PSB risk?

It can help only if the price, the scope and the risk are real. If the client still approves weekly timesheets and pays for every extra hour, a fixed-price label says very little. It is one fact among many, and it does not decide PSB status alone.

#### Q: Is a CPT1 ruling the same as a PSB ruling?

No. Form CPT1 is used to request a CPP/EI ruling on a worker's status. That ruling deals with CPP and EI, not with PSB status for income tax, though CRA looks at similar facts. We review whether it is useful in your facts.

## LinkedIn post (Blog2Social)

CRA does not decide a contractor's status on one fact. It asks one question: whose business is it?

The Wiebe Door and Sagaz tests answer that question with four main factors: control, ownership of tools, chance of profit and risk of loss, and integration.

For an incorporated IT or finance contractor in Ontario, the answer matters. If the corporation is a personal services business, income taxed at 11.2% can be taxed at 44.5%, and most expenses are denied.

In my new post, I apply each test step by step to a hypothetical data engineer contracting through an agency to a bank. It includes a worked example of an hourly contract against a fixed-price deliverable.

Please read it here.

#PersonalServicesBusiness #IncorporatedContractors #CanadianTax

https://gondaliyacpa.ca/wiebe-door-sagaz-tests-contractors/

## Brief

| Primary keyword | PSB employee exception |

| Author and review line | Written by Sharad Gondaliya, CPA (Canada and USA). Reviewed by Sharad Gondaliya, CPA, Founder and Managing Director, Gondaliya CPA Professional Corporation, CPA Ontario Firm ID 61330051. Last updated: October 20, 2026. |
| Publish date | 2026-10-20 |

## Post copy

H1: The More-Than-5-Employees PSB Exception: Who Qualifies and Who Does Not

The PSB employee exception applies only if your corporation employs in the business, throughout the year, more than five full-time employees. That means at least six full-time people for the whole year. Part-time staff and subcontractors do not count. For a solo Ontario contractor facing 44.5% instead of 11.2%, it rarely helps.

**Key takeaways**

| # | Takeaway |
|---|---|
| 1 | The exception needs more than five full-time employees, so at least six, employed in the business throughout the whole year. |
| 2 | Part-time staff, subcontractors, other incorporated contractors and agency workers do not count toward the six. |
| 3 | Whether you, as owner, or family members on payroll count depends on your real roles and hours. It needs review. |
| 4 | Six full-time salaries of $50,000 cost $300,000 a year. The extra PSB tax on $150,000 of retained income is $49,950. |
| 5 | A separate exception covers fees received from an associated corporation. It does not help a contractor billing an agency or an unrelated client. |

### H2: What is the PSB employee exception?

A personal services business (PSB) is defined in section 125(7) of the Income Tax Act. In plain words, your corporation can be a PSB when you perform the services (you are the "incorporated employee"), you or a related person own 10% or more of any class of its shares, directly or indirectly, and you would reasonably be regarded as an officer or employee of the client if the corporation did not exist. I explain the full test on our page about [PSB risk for incorporated contractors](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-risk-incorporated-contractors/).

The same definition has two exceptions. Even if the facts look like employment, the business is not a PSB if:

1. the corporation employs in the business, throughout the year, more than five full-time employees, or
2. the amount paid for the services is received from a corporation associated with it in the year.

This post is mostly about the first one, the employee exception. I cover the associated corporation exception near the end.

Why does it matter? Because the PSB rate is harsh. In Ontario, PSB income is taxed at a combined 44.5%. Income that gets the small business deduction is taxed at 11.2% from July 1, 2026. Income earned before July 1, 2026 was taxed at 12.2%, so a corporation with a December 31, 2026 year-end gets a blended 11.70% for 2026. Our post on the [PSB tax rate in Ontario](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-tax-rate-ontario-2026/) shows how the 44.5% is built up.

When the exception applies, none of that PSB treatment applies to the business. That is why people ask about it. But the conditions are strict, and each word matters.

### H2: What does "more than five full-time employees" mean?

The test has four parts. All four must be met.

#### H3: More than five means at least six

Five is not enough. The law says more than five, so the corporation needs at least six full-time employees. A corporation with exactly five full-time employees does not qualify, however busy they are.

#### H3: Full-time, not part-time

Each of the six must be a full-time employee. Part-time staff do not meet the full-time requirement, even if you have many of them.

The Income Tax Act does not set a number of hours for full-time. So we look at the facts: the hours actually worked, the employment terms, the payroll records and the job duties. A role that is full-time on paper but not in practice is a weak position.

#### H3: Throughout the year, not just at year-end

The count must hold throughout the taxation year. Having six full-time employees in December does not help if you had two in March. If someone leaves and is replaced later, the gap can break the count. So turnover during the year needs review, not just the headcount on the last day.

The test is applied year by year. A corporation can meet it one year and miss it the next.

#### H3: Employed in the business, by your corporation

The employees must be employed by your corporation, in the business. People employed by the staffing agency or by the end client are not your employees, even if you work side by side with them every day. Workers paid through another corporation are not your corporation's employees either.

They are employees, not contractors. If you send overflow work to another developer's corporation, that corporation is your subcontractor. It does not count.

### H2: Who counts toward the six, and who does not?

Here is how I look at the people most contractor corporations ask about.

| Person | Counts toward the six? | Why |
|---|---|---|
| Full-time employee of your corporation, on payroll all year, working in the business | Yes | Meets every part of the test |
| Part-time employee | No | The test requires full-time employees |
| Subcontractor or freelancer | No | Not an employee of your corporation |
| Another incorporated contractor billing your corporation | No | Employed by, or owns, a different corporation |
| Staff employed by the staffing agency or the end client | No | Not employed by your corporation |
| Full-time employee hired partway through the year | Only from the start date | The count must hold throughout the year, so the gap before they started needs review |
| Summer student or seasonal worker | No | Not full-time throughout the year |
| You, the owner | Depends on your real role | Needs review of your actual duties and hours in the business |
| Spouse or other family member on payroll | Depends on their real role | Needs review of actual full-time work, hours and pay |

The last two rows are where most of the questions come from, so I will take them one at a time.

### H2: Do the owner and family members count?

The law does not answer this in a single line, and I will not pretend it does. Whether an owner or a family member counts depends on what they actually do.

For you, as owner, the question is whether you are genuinely a full-time employee of your own corporation working in the business, alongside the others. For a solo contractor, this question rarely matters. Even if you counted, you would still need five more full-time employees.

For a spouse, a parent or an adult child on payroll, we look at real work. What do they do? How many hours? Is the pay reasonable for that work? Are there timesheets, a job description and regular payroll? Putting family on payroll with a title and no real full-time role does not meet the test, and it creates other tax problems. Salary paid to a family member also has to be reasonable for the work to be deductible.

So my answer is always the same: it needs review of the real roles, with records to support them.

### H2: Why does the exception rarely help a solo IT or finance contractor?

Most of the IT and finance contractors I work with run a one-person corporation. A developer, cloud engineer, FP&A analyst or controller bills through an agency on a 35, 37.5 or 40 hour contract. There are no employees. Sometimes there is a bookkeeper or an accountant on contract, and those do not count either.

To use the exception, that corporation would need six full-time employees working in the business all year. That is a different business. It is a consulting firm or a staffing company with a team on payroll. If that is what you are building, the exception can be real. If you are one person on one contract, it is not a practical answer.

#### H3: The worked example: six employees vs $49,950

Here are the numbers. Start with Example A: a corporation with $150,000 of retained income, with a full year at 11.2%.

- Not a PSB, at 11.2%: $150,000 x 11.2% = $16,800.
- PSB, at 44.5%: $150,000 x 44.5% = $66,750.
- Extra tax if CRA treats it as a PSB: $66,750 less $16,800 = $49,950.

Now suppose you hired six full-time employees, each paid a salary of $50,000, just to meet the test.

- Six salaries: 6 x $50,000 = $300,000 a year.
- That is $250,050 more than the $49,950 of extra PSB tax.
- A single employee at $50,000 costs $50 more than the whole $49,950.

Salaries are deductible, but that does not change the picture. Even if every dollar of salary saved tax at 11.2%, the saving would be $33,600. The net cost would still be $266,400 a year, which is $216,450 more than the PSB tax at stake. These figures are before employer CPP and EI contributions, benefits and the cost of running payroll, which all add to the bill.

There is also the work itself. Six full-time staff on 37.5-hour weeks are paid for 11,700 hours a year (6 x 37.5 x 52). You bill 1,800 hours yourself (37.5 x 48). Those 11,700 hours have to be real work in the business, not hours created to pass a test. Hiring only makes sense if those people bring in more revenue than they cost.

With a December 31, 2026 year-end, the not-PSB tax for 2026 at the blended 11.70% is $17,543.84, so the extra PSB tax is $49,206.16. The conclusion does not change. For a solo contractor, the employee exception costs far more than the tax it protects against.

### H2: What is the associated corporation exception?

The second exception is narrower than most people expect. A business is not a PSB if the amount paid for the services is received from a corporation that is associated with your corporation in the year.

Association is a technical test in section 256 of the Income Tax Act. In broad terms, it is about common control, for example two corporations controlled by the same person or group. If your corporation provides services to another corporation you control, the fees it receives from that corporation can fall under this exception.

It does not help in the usual contractor setup. The staffing agency is not associated with your corporation. The bank, insurer or government department at the end of the chain is not associated with it either. So fees from those clients do not qualify.

Two more points. The exception turns on who pays for the services. Fees from an agency or an unrelated client are not received from an associated corporation. And associated corporations share one $500,000 small business limit. A structure built to create association can reduce the small business deduction available to each corporation. Any plan here needs review of the full group, not just one corporation.

### H2: What should a solo contractor focus on instead?

If neither exception fits, the PSB question goes back to the main test: would you reasonably be regarded as an employee of the client if your corporation did not exist? CRA looks at the whole working relationship, year by year. Control, tools and equipment, chance of profit and risk of loss, and whether the work is part of the client's business or your own all matter. Hours alone never decide it.

That is where your effort is better spent. Practical steps include:

- Know what your agency contract and the end client's onboarding rules actually say.
- Keep records that show how you really work: your own equipment, other clients, business costs, and how you find new work.
- Keep your corporation's books, T2 returns and payroll records clean and on time.
- Plan salary and dividends with the PSB downside in mind, since salary paid in the year to you is still deductible under 18(1)(p) if CRA ever reassesses.

Our [PSB risk checklist](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-risk-reduction-checklist/) walks through these steps in more detail. None of them can promise an outcome. They reduce risk and help support your position if CRA asks questions.

If CRA does send a questionnaire or opens a file, our page on [CRA PSB review](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/cra-psb-review-support/) explains what to expect and how we act as your corporation's authorized representative.

### H2: How can we help with the PSB employee exception?

We review your contract and working facts as part of tax planning. If you have staff, we look at who is on payroll, their real roles and hours, and whether the count holds throughout the year. If you work alone, we tell you plainly that the exception is not your answer, and we focus on the parts of your setup that reduce risk. We are accountants, not lawyers, so any change to contract wording is for your lawyer.

We also handle the routine work behind your position: bookkeeping, payroll and T4 slips, GST/HST filings and your T2 return. Our page for an [accountant for IT contractors](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/accountant-for-it-contractors/) explains how that works across the year. We work with incorporated businesses only, on a fixed flat fee.

If you are not sure whether your corporation meets the PSB employee exception, please [book a consultation](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/consultation/). Please bring your payroll records and your current contract, and we will go through them with you.

## FAQs

#### Q: What is the PSB employee exception?

It is one of two exceptions in the personal services business definition in section 125(7) of the Income Tax Act. A business is not a PSB if the corporation employs in the business, throughout the year, more than five full-time employees. That means at least six full-time employees for the whole year.

#### Q: Do part-time employees or subcontractors count toward the six?

No. The exception requires full-time employees of your corporation. Part-time staff do not meet the full-time requirement. Subcontractors, freelancers and other incorporated contractors are not your employees, so they do not count either.

#### Q: Do I count as one of the six employees if I own the corporation?

It depends on your real role. The question is whether you are genuinely a full-time employee working in the business alongside the others. It needs review of your actual duties and hours. For a solo contractor it rarely matters, because you would still need five more full-time employees.

#### Q: Can I put family members on payroll to meet the exception?

Only real, full-time roles can support the count. We look at what each family member actually does, their hours, whether the pay is reasonable for the work, and the records behind it. Family on payroll without a real full-time role does not meet the test and creates other tax problems.

#### Q: Is it worth hiring six employees to get out of the PSB rules?

For a solo contractor, almost never. Six full-time salaries of $50,000 cost $300,000 a year, against $49,950 of extra PSB tax on $150,000 of retained income. Hiring only makes sense if those employees do real work that brings in more revenue than they cost.

#### Q: What is the associated corporation exception?

A business is not a PSB if the amount paid for the services is received from a corporation associated with it in the year. Association is a technical common control test in section 256. It does not help a contractor billing a staffing agency or an unrelated bank, insurer or government client.

## LinkedIn post (Blog2Social)

Many incorporated IT and finance contractors ask me about the "more than five employees" PSB exception.

Here is the short answer. Your corporation needs more than five full-time employees, so at least six, employed in the business throughout the whole year. Part-time staff, subcontractors and agency workers do not count. Whether the owner or family members count depends on their real roles.

For a solo contractor, the numbers rarely work. Six salaries of $50,000 cost $300,000 a year. The extra PSB tax on $150,000 of retained income in Ontario is $49,950 (44.5% vs 11.2%).

I also cover the separate associated corporation exception and why it does not help when you bill a staffing agency.

## Post copy

H1: 12 Contract Clauses That Raise PSB Risk for IT and Finance Contractors

Contract clauses raise PSB risk when they make you look like the client's employee rather than the owner of an Ontario business: fixed hours, a client laptop, a client manager, no substitution, exclusivity and no-risk hourly pay. No single clause decides. CRA weighs the whole relationship, and the gap is 11.2% small business tax versus 44.5% on PSB income.

**Key takeaways**

| # | Takeaway |
|---|---|
| 1 | No single clause makes your corporation a PSB. CRA weighs control, tools, chance of profit and risk of loss, and integration across the whole relationship. |
| 2 | In Ontario, income eligible for the small business deduction is taxed at 11.2% from July 1, 2026. PSB income is taxed at 44.5%, and most expenses are denied. |
| 3 | In an agency setup, the end client's onboarding rules matter as much as the agency agreement. |
| 4 | What happens day to day counts more than the wording. A substitution right that no client would accept carries little weight. |
| 5 | We are accountants, not lawyers. Any change to contract wording is for your lawyer and the agency to agree. |

### H2: Why do contract clauses matter for PSB risk?

Under section 125(7) of the Income Tax Act, your corporation carries on a personal services business if you, or a person related to you, own 10% or more of any class of its shares, directly or indirectly, and you would reasonably be regarded as an officer or employee of the client if your corporation did not exist. If your corporation employs more than five full-time employees throughout the year, or it is paid by a corporation associated with it, an exception can apply. Most one-person contractor corporations meet neither exception, so the working relationship decides it. Our page on [personal services business risk](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-risk-incorporated-contractors/) explains the full rules.

CRA can ask for your contracts in a PSB review, and they show how the relationship was set up. Many IT and finance contractors work through a staffing agency, so there are often three layers of paper: the agency agreement, the statement of work and the end client's onboarding package. CRA looks at whether you would reasonably be an employee of the party you really provide the services to, which can be the end client. Our page on [staffing agency contractor tax](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/staffing-agency-contractor-tax/) covers that setup.

The cost of getting it wrong is large. In Ontario, small business income is taxed at a combined 11.2% from July 1, 2026. Income earned before that date was taxed at 12.2%, so a December 31, 2026 year-end gets a blended 11.70%. PSB income is taxed at 44.5%, and section 18(1)(p) denies most expenses.

### H2: How should you read this list?

Each clause below can point toward employment. None of them decides the question alone. CRA uses the tests from Wiebe Door Services Ltd. v. M.N.R. and 671122 Ontario Ltd. v. Sagaz Industries Canada Inc. The central question is whose business it is. The factors are control, ownership of tools and equipment, chance of profit and risk of loss, and integration. Hiring helpers, investment and management also count.

Two more points. CRA tests what actually happens, not only what the contract says. And the test applies year by year, so a renewal can change the picture. If your main worry is the hours clause, read our post on whether a 40 hour contract makes you an [incorporated employee](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/40-hour-contract-incorporated-employee/).

### H2: What are the 12 contract clauses that raise PSB risk?

#### H3: 1. Fixed weekly hours

**Why it matters.** A statement of work that sets 35, 37.5 or 40 hours a week, often with core hours, tells CRA the client decides when you work. That is a control indicator.

**What can weigh the other way.** Hours alone never decide PSB status. A set number of billable hours can simply be how the client buys capacity. It weighs less if you choose your own start and end times, take time off without needing approval, and the hours work as a billing cap rather than a schedule you must keep.

#### H3: 2. Required onsite days or location

**Why it matters.** Many IT and finance roles require a set number of days each week in the client's office. Being told where to work, and on which days, is a control indicator. A desk on the client's floor beside staff doing the same work also points to integration.

**What can weigh the other way.** Some location rules exist for security or regulatory reasons, such as access to restricted systems or trading floors. A rule that applies to every vendor carries less weight than a rule that applies only to staff. It also helps if you choose your remote days yourself.

#### H3: 3. Client-supplied laptop and VPN-only access

**Why it matters.** At banks, insurers and government clients, you usually get a client laptop, client software and access only through the client's VPN. Under the tools test, the work is done with the client's equipment, not your corporation's.

**What can weigh the other way.** In regulated sectors, data security rules often require this of every vendor, so it can carry less weight there. It helps if your corporation owns and pays for its own equipment, software licences, training and certifications that you use across clients. If the income were treated as PSB income, those costs would be denied, but they still show your corporation invests in its own business.

#### H3: 4. Reporting to a client manager

**Why it matters.** A statement of work that names a client manager you report to, who assigns your tasks, sets priorities and approves your methods, points to control. If you sit in a client team with staff doing the same work, it also points to integration.

**What can weigh the other way.** Every client sets goals and checks results. What matters is whether the manager directs how you do the work or only accepts what you deliver. A defined scope, clear deliverables and your own methods weigh the other way.

#### H3: 5. Timesheet approval by the end client

**Why it matters.** When the end client approves your weekly timesheet before the agency pays you, the client is checking your time. If that approval is used to manage attendance, it points to control.

**What can weigh the other way.** Timesheet approval is often just billing support. The agency needs proof of hours before it invoices the end client. It weighs less if approval only confirms the hours billed and nobody uses it to manage when you work. Invoices issued by your corporation, on its own terms, also help.

#### H3: 6. Exclusivity or non-compete

**Why it matters.** A clause that stops your corporation from serving other clients during the contract, or from working in the same field after it ends, limits your chance of profit. A business can usually serve more than one client at a time. It can also point to control.

**What can weigh the other way.** A narrow non-solicit is a different thing. It stops you from going around the agency to contract with the end client directly, and it is common in agency contracts. It weighs less if your corporation actually has other clients, or markets itself to them, during the year.

#### H3: 7. Termination at will on short notice with no cancellation fee

**Why it matters.** If the client or agency can end the contract at any time, on short notice, with no cancellation fee and no payment for planned work, the client holds the power in the relationship. That fits a control pattern more than a deal between two businesses with protection on both sides.

**What can weigh the other way.** This is a weaker indicator on its own. Employees usually have notice rights, so a contract with no notice either way does not fit employment cleanly either. A mutual right to end the contract, payment for work in progress, or a fee for early cancellation all weigh the other way.

#### H3: 8. Personal services only, no substitution

**Why it matters.** A clause that says only you can do the work, and you cannot send a substitute or hire a helper, is one of the stronger indicators. Personal service is a feature of employment. A business can usually decide who does the work and earn a margin on helpers.

**What can weigh the other way.** A real right to send a qualified substitute, at your corporation's cost, weighs the other way, but only if the client would accept one in practice. Security clearance often makes that hard. If your corporation has ever paid a subcontractor or helper, keep the records.

#### H3: 9. Mandatory client training and performance reviews

**Why it matters.** Required training on the client's internal processes, annual staff courses and formal performance reviews are how a client manages its own people. They point to control and integration.

**What can weigh the other way.** Regulated clients require some training, such as anti-money laundering, privacy and information security, from everyone who touches their systems, including vendors. That carries less weight than staff-style reviews, goals and ratings. Feedback on a deliverable is not the same as a performance review.

#### H3: 10. Client code of conduct, badges and policies

**Why it matters.** Onboarding packages often ask you to sign the client's employee code of conduct, follow HR policies, carry a staff badge, use a client email address and appear on the org chart. Each makes you look like part of the client's organization, which points to integration.

**What can weigh the other way.** A contractor badge that differs from staff badges, a vendor code of conduct rather than the staff handbook, and your own corporate email for your business all weigh the other way. Security and confidentiality policies that apply to every vendor carry less weight.

#### H3: 11. Open-ended term with automatic renewals

**Why it matters.** A contract with no end date, or one renewed again and again for years, suggests ongoing work inside the client's business rather than a defined project. That points to integration. Finance contractors often fill a seat, such as a controller or FP&A role, that staff would normally hold.

**What can weigh the other way.** A defined project with deliverables and an end date weighs the other way. So does a real change of scope at each renewal, or work for other clients between assignments. Long engagements happen between businesses too. What matters is whether the work stays a project or becomes a seat.

#### H3: 12. Hourly pay with no financial risk

**Why it matters.** If you are paid by the hour for every hour worked, with no fixed price, no cost for overruns and no bad debt risk, your corporation cannot really make or lose money beyond your own time. That points away from chance of profit and risk of loss.

**What can weigh the other way.** Hourly billing is normal for many businesses, so it is not decisive. It weighs less if your corporation carries real costs and risks: unpaid time between contracts, the cost of fixing its own errors, insurance it pays for, and investment in equipment and training. Negotiating your own rate also helps.

### H2: Which test does each clause touch?

Here are all 12 clauses in one table, with the main test each one touches.

| # | Clause | Main test it touches | Can also touch |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Fixed weekly hours | Control | None |
| 2 | Required onsite days or location | Control | Integration |
| 3 | Client-supplied laptop and VPN-only access | Tools | Control |
| 4 | Reporting to a client manager | Control | Integration |
| 5 | Timesheet approval by the end client | Control | None |
| 6 | Exclusivity or non-compete | Chance of profit and risk of loss | Control |
| 7 | Termination at will, short notice, no cancellation fee | Control | Chance of profit and risk of loss |
| 8 | Personal services only, no substitution | Chance of profit and risk of loss | Control |
| 9 | Mandatory client training and performance reviews | Control | Integration |
| 10 | Client code of conduct, badges and policies | Integration | Control |
| 11 | Open-ended term with automatic renewals | Integration | None |
| 12 | Hourly pay with no financial risk | Chance of profit and risk of loss | None |

### H2: What could PSB status cost if these clauses describe your work?

Here is an illustration, not a client file. An IT contractor bills $95 an hour for 37.5 hours a week, 48 weeks a year, through an agency. Revenue is $171,000. The corporation spends $9,000 on its own laptop, software, insurance and accounting. To keep it simple, assume a full taxation year at 11.2% and no salary.

- Not PSB: ($171,000 minus $9,000) x 11.2% = $18,144.
- PSB: the $9,000 of expenses is denied under section 18(1)(p), so $171,000 x 44.5% = $76,095.
- Extra corporate tax: $57,951 in one year.

Now say the corporation pays the contractor a salary of $120,000. Salary paid to you stays deductible even under the PSB rules.

- Not PSB: $171,000 minus $9,000 minus $120,000 = $42,000, x 11.2% = $4,704.
- PSB: $171,000 minus $120,000 = $51,000, x 44.5% = $22,695.
- Extra corporate tax: $17,991.

I have left employer CPP out of these figures and not shown the personal tax on the salary. Salary narrows the gap but does not remove it. For a December 31, 2026 year-end, the small business figures would use the blended 11.70% rate.

### H2: What can you do about the clauses in your contract?

Start by reading your agency agreement, statement of work and onboarding package against this list. Then write down how the work actually happens. If the paper and the facts differ, the facts usually carry more weight.

Keep records that support your position: invoices to other clients, receipts for your own equipment and software licences, your insurance policy, and any payment to a subcontractor. Our [PSB risk checklist](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-risk-reduction-checklist/) lists the records worth keeping.

If a clause bothers you, raise it with your lawyer and the agency at renewal. We are accountants, not lawyers. We do not draft or change contract wording. Wording that changes on paper while the work stays the same carries little weight anyway.

We review your contract and working facts as part of the tax planning we do for your corporation. Our [PSB contract review](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/contract-review-psb-indicators/) page explains how we read each document and what we ask about. We can also plan your salary and dividend mix with PSB risk in mind.

If you would like us to look at your contracts, please [book a consultation](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/consultation/). Please bring your agency agreement, every statement of work and your onboarding package.

## FAQs

#### Q: Does one of these clauses make my corporation a PSB?

No. Each clause can point toward employment, but CRA weighs control, tools, chance of profit and risk of loss, and integration across the whole relationship, year by year. Many IT and finance contracts contain several of these clauses, so the working facts matter.

#### Q: Which of the 12 clauses matters most?

There is no fixed ranking. Clauses that require you to do the work personally, or that let the client direct how you work, often go to the heart of the question. Even then, CRA weighs them with everything else.

#### Q: Can I lower PSB risk by removing these clauses from my contract?

Contract wording is for your lawyer and the agency to agree. We are accountants, not lawyers. Also, CRA tests what actually happens. If the wording changes but the work stays the same, the change carries little weight.

#### Q: My agency agreement looks fine, but the end client's onboarding papers do not. Which one counts?

Both. With an agency in the middle, CRA asks whether you would reasonably be an employee of the party that really receives your services. The end client's rules on hours, equipment, training and conduct can matter as much as the agency agreement.

#### Q: Does a client laptop on its own mean my corporation is a PSB?

No. A client laptop with VPN-only access points to the client's tools, and banks and insurers often require it of every vendor. It is one factor, weighed with all the others.

#### Q: If I pay myself a salary, do these clauses still matter?

Yes. Salary paid to you stays deductible under section 18(1)(p), which reduces the income exposed to the 44.5% rate. Whatever profit stays in the corporation would still be taxed at 44.5% instead of 11.2% if CRA treated it as PSB income.

#### Q: Can CRA give me a ruling on my contract?

Not a PSB ruling for income tax. Form CPT1 asks CRA for a CPP and EI ruling on a worker's status. That ruling does not decide PSB status, although CRA looks at similar facts. If you are thinking about one, we first review whether it is useful in your facts.

Your agency contract says a lot about your PSB risk.

Fixed 37.5 hour weeks. A client laptop with VPN-only access. A client manager. No right to send a substitute. A non-compete. Renewal after renewal.

None of these clauses makes your corporation a personal services business on its own. CRA weighs the whole relationship, and what happens day to day counts more than the wording.

The tax gap is wide: 11.2% small business tax in Ontario versus 44.5% on PSB income, with most expenses denied.

I wrote up the 12 clauses I look for, why each one matters and what can weigh the other way. It also includes a worked example with exact numbers.

We are accountants, not lawyers, so contract wording stays with your lawyer and the agency.

## Post copy

H1: Finance Contractors at Banks Through Agencies: The PSB Risk Most People Miss

Finance contractor PSB risk does not go away because a staffing agency sits between you and the bank. Under the personal services business (PSB) rules, CRA asks whether you would reasonably be an employee of the party really receiving your services, usually the bank. If so, your Ontario corporation pays 44.5% instead of the current 11.2%.

**Key takeaways**

| # | Takeaway |
|---|---|
| 1 | The agency contract does not settle PSB status. CRA asks whether you would reasonably be an employee of the party really receiving your services, which is usually the bank. |
| 2 | Finance, risk, audit and compliance are core functions of a bank, so the integration factor often points toward the bank's business, not yours. |
| 3 | Bank-run controls, sign-off authority and supervision by bank managers weigh heavily on the control side. |
| 4 | On $163,200 of billings with $9,000 of expenses and no salary, PSB treatment costs $55,353.60 more in corporate tax for one year at 11.2% vs 44.5%. |
| 5 | No one can promise a result. You can review the facts each year, document them and plan your pay mix to reduce risk. |

### H2: Why do so many finance contractors think the agency protects them?

The reasoning sounds sensible. Your corporation signs with the agency. The agency invoices the bank. The bank pays the agency, and the agency pays your corporation. You never sign anything with the bank. So, the thinking goes, you cannot be the bank's employee.

I hear this from FP&A, controllership, risk, audit and compliance contractors, many of whom have spent years at the same bank through more than one agency. The paper says vendor. The day-to-day work often says something else.

The PSB rules do not stop at the paper. They look at who the work is really done for.

### H2: What does CRA actually ask in a three-party arrangement?

Start with the definition. Under section 125(7) of the Income Tax Act, your corporation carries on a personal services business in a year when two things are true:

- You, the person who does the work (the "incorporated employee"), or a person related to you, is a specified shareholder. That means owning 10% or more of any class of shares, directly or indirectly.
- You would reasonably be regarded as an officer or employee of the person or partnership receiving the services if your corporation did not exist.

Read the second part again. It is not about who signed with your corporation. It is about who receives the services. In a three-party arrangement, CRA looks at whether you would reasonably be an employee of the party your services are really provided to. For a finance contractor placed at a bank, that is usually the bank.

So picture the arrangement without your corporation, and ask who directs the work and whose business it is part of. The agency contract, the bank's onboarding rules and the daily work all matter. None of them decides it alone.

There are two exceptions. The first applies if your corporation employs in the business, throughout the year, more than five full-time employees. That means at least six, full-time, all year. Whether owners or family members count depends on their real roles, so it needs review. The second applies if the amount for the services is received from a corporation associated with yours in the year. In a typical agency setup, the payer is not associated with your corporation, so this second exception rarely helps.

I set out the full rules on our page about [PSB risk for incorporated contractors](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-risk-incorporated-contractors/).

### H2: Why is finance work so hard to separate from a bank's business?

The courts set out the employee-or-contractor tests in Wiebe Door Services Ltd. v. M.N.R. and 671122 Ontario Ltd. v. Sagaz Industries Canada Inc. The central question is whose business it is. Are you in business on your own account, or working inside someone else's business?

Four factors carry most of the weight: control, ownership of tools and equipment, chance of profit and risk of loss, and integration. Hiring helpers, your own investment and how you manage the work are also considered.

Integration is the factor finance contractors most often overlook. A bank is a finance business. Measuring, controlling and reporting its activities is not a side task. It is how the bank runs and meets its regulatory duties.

Compare two engagements. An IT contractor builds a new data pipeline, hands it over and leaves. A finance contractor runs the monthly reconciliations for a business line, prepares variance commentary for the CFO's reporting pack, or tests controls for the annual certification cycle. The first looks more like a project with an end. The second looks like a seat in the bank's finance function. If the bank did not bring you in, it would often give the same work to an employee.

That is the integration problem. Your work can look like part of the bank's own business, not your corporation's.

Insurers look much the same, and so do many government finance roles.

### H2: Who really controls the work: the agency, the bank or you?

Control is the second place where the agency layer gives less cover than expected. The agency usually controls the commercial side. The bank usually controls the work. Here is how it tends to split.

| Area | What the agency controls | What the bank controls | What your corporation controls |
|---|---|---|---|
| Getting the engagement | Presents you and signs the contract with your corporation | Interviews you and decides whether to take you on | Decides whether to accept |
| Rate, invoicing and payment | Sets the rate it pays and the payment terms | Pays the agency under its own vendor agreement | Negotiates the rate and issues invoices |
| Daily tasks and priorities | Usually nothing | Assigns work, sets priorities and changes them | How the work is done, only where the scope allows |
| Deadlines and the month-end calendar | Nothing | Sets the close calendar and reporting deadlines | Plans its own work to meet them |
| Hours, timesheets and time off | Collects timesheets for billing | Approves timesheets and time off through a bank manager | Hours, only where the contract allows |
| Location, access and tools | Passes on the bank's requirements | Sets onsite days, VPN access, laptop, badge and system entitlements | Its own equipment, software and training, where allowed |
| Policies and code of conduct | Requires you to follow the bank's rules | Writes the rules and enforces them | Its own professional standards |
| Approval authority and review | Nothing | Decides what you may approve and who reviews your work | Signs off only within the authority given |
| Renewal or ending the engagement | Handles notice terms and paperwork | Decides whether the role continues | Decides whether to renew and whether to take other clients |
| Business costs and risk | Collection risk on the bank | The budget for the role | Insurance, equipment, gaps between contracts and its own costs |

Look at the bank's column. That is where the work is directed, and where CRA looks.

#### H3: Why do bank-run controls weigh so heavily?

Banks and insurers run formal internal control frameworks, because their regulators expect it. These controls decide what you can see, what you can post, what you can approve and who must review it.

For a finance contractor, the controls are often the job. You may be named as the performer or reviewer of a key control in the bank's control documentation. You may have posting rights in the general ledger, approval limits, or access to the regulatory reporting system. The bank grants and removes each of those under its own policies.

Many of these rules exist for regulatory reasons and apply to every outside firm. I note that on file, because a rule that applies to every vendor reads differently from a manager directing your day. But when the controls place you inside the bank's own approval chain, they point to both control and integration.

#### H3: Why is supervision the strongest signal?

The clearest control fact is supervision by bank staff. A bank manager who assigns your work, reviews it like an employee's work and approves your time off is acting like an employer.

Finance roles add a twist. Senior contractors often supervise or review the bank's own staff. A contract controller may review reconciliations prepared by bank employees, or approve their journal entries. That puts you inside the bank's management structure. Managing the client's people points strongly toward integration.

Fixed hours matter less than people think. A 37.5 or 40 hour week is a control indicator, but hours alone never decide PSB status. The whole relationship is tested, year by year. I go through the wording that tends to cause trouble in [contract clauses that raise PSB risk](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/contract-clauses-psb-risk/).

### H2: What does PSB status cost on a $163,200 contract?

Here is a worked example. It is an illustration, not a forecast for your file.

- Rate: $85 an hour, for 40 hours a week, for 48 weeks.
- Billings: $85 x 40 x 48 = $163,200, before HST.
- Business expenses: $9,000 for software subscriptions, a phone plan, professional liability insurance and accounting fees.
- No salary paid, and a full taxation year at the current 11.2% rate.

If the corporation is not a PSB, it deducts the $9,000. Taxable income is $154,200. Tax at 11.2% is $17,270.40.

If the corporation is a PSB, the $9,000 is denied. Taxable income is the full $163,200. Tax at 44.5% is $72,624.

The difference is $55,353.60 of extra corporate tax for one year.

The 44.5% is 33% federal, which includes the additional 5% tax under section 123.5, plus the 11.5% Ontario general rate. The 11.2% rate applies to income earned from July 1, 2026. Income earned before that date was taxed at 12.2%, so a December 31, 2026 year-end gets a blended 11.70%.

#### H3: How much of that comes from section 18(1)(p)?

Split the $55,353.60 in two parts:

- The rate gap on the same $154,200 of income: 44.5% less 11.2% is 33.3 points, and $154,200 x 33.3% = $51,348.60.
- The denied expenses: $9,000 x 44.5% = $4,005.

Together, $51,348.60 plus $4,005 is $55,353.60.

Section 18(1)(p) limits a PSB's deductions to four things: salary, wages or other pay to you; the cost of your benefits or allowances; certain amounts spent selling property or negotiating contracts that an employee could deduct; and legal costs to collect amounts owed for your services. Rent, software, equipment, travel, phone, insurance, accounting fees and similar costs are denied. Each denied dollar is taxed as if you never spent it.

#### H3: What changes if you pay yourself a salary?

Salary paid to you is one of the few deductions section 18(1)(p) allows. Say the corporation pays you a salary of $120,000 in the same year.

- Not a PSB: $163,200 less $9,000 less $120,000 leaves $34,200. Tax at 11.2% is $3,830.40.
- PSB: $163,200 less $120,000 leaves $43,200. Tax at 44.5% is $19,224.
- Extra corporate tax: $15,393.60.

CPP and personal tax on the salary are not shown. The gap is smaller because less income stays in the corporation, which is why the pay mix matters more when PSB risk is real.

#### H3: Does paying dividends later close the gap?

No. PSB income is not eligible for the small business deduction, so it is taxed at the full rate and adds to the general rate income pool (GRIP). Dividends paid from it can be designated eligible dividends. Even so, $100,000 of PSB income costs $44,500 in corporate tax. The $55,500 left, paid as an eligible dividend at the top 2026 Ontario rate of 39.34%, costs $21,833.70 in personal tax. That is $66,333.70 in total. The same $100,000 paid as salary, at the top 2026 Ontario rate of 53.53%, costs $53,530. The PSB route costs $12,803.70 more.

The cost also repeats for each year that is reassessed. I explain how that works in [PSB reassessment](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-reassessment-denied-deductions/).

### H2: How can you reduce finance contractor PSB risk?

Nothing here makes PSB risk disappear. These steps help you document your facts and support your position.

- **Read the statement of work before you sign.** Scope tied to deliverables, such as a model, a remediation project or a new process, reads differently from an open-ended seat in the team.
- **Ask whether sign-off authority is really needed.** If it is, note what you approve, under whose authority, and how it ties to your deliverables.
- **Keep the bank's onboarding rules.** Note which rules apply to every outside firm, and why.
- **Pay for your own tools.** Equipment, software, training and professional liability insurance, where the bank allows it.
- **Run the corporation as a business.** Keep records of other clients, proposals and marketing.
- **Describe the work on your invoices.** Not only the hours.
- **Review the facts each year.** A renewal, a new manager or new duties can change the picture for that year.

I explain how agency setups fit together on our page about [staffing agency contractor tax](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/staffing-agency-contractor-tax/).

CRA guide RC4110, "Employee or Self-Employed?", explains how CRA approaches a working relationship. You may also hear about Form CPT1, which is used to request a CPP and EI ruling on a worker's status. That ruling covers CPP and EI only. It is not a PSB ruling for income tax, though CRA looks at similar facts. We review whether a request is useful in your facts.

We are accountants, not lawyers. New clause wording is a matter for your lawyer.

### H2: How do we help finance contractors with this?

We review your contract and working facts as part of tax planning. We read the agency contract, the statement of work and the bank's onboarding rules, then ask how the work happens. We tell you which facts point toward employment, which point the other way, and what to keep on file.

That sits alongside the work we already do for your corporation: the T2 return, bookkeeping, payroll, GST/HST filings, and salary and dividend planning. If CRA reviews your corporation, we provide CRA audit support and representation as its authorized representative. You can see how we set up these files on our [accountant for finance contractors](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/accountant-for-finance-contractors/) page.

We work with incorporated Canadian businesses only, on a fixed flat-fee model. Our team includes a second designated CPA (CPA Ontario) and bookkeeping support staff.

If you work at a bank, an insurer or a government through an agency and want to know how your facts read, please [book a consultation](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/consultation/). Please bring your agency contract, your statement of work and the bank's onboarding rules. You can also call us at 647-212-9559, 9AM to 8:30PM, Monday to Sunday.

## FAQs

#### Q: Does working through a staffing agency protect my corporation from the PSB rules?

Not on its own. In a three-party arrangement, CRA looks at whether you would reasonably be an employee of the party your services are really provided to. For a finance contractor at a bank, that is usually the bank. The agency contract and the bank's onboarding rules both matter.

#### Q: Why are finance roles at banks seen as higher PSB risk?

Finance, risk, audit and compliance are core functions of a bank. When your work is part of how the bank closes its books or meets its reporting duties, the integration factor can point toward the bank's business. Bank-run controls and supervision add to the control side.

#### Q: Does sign-off authority at the bank matter?

It can matter a great deal. Approving journal entries, reconciliations or control tests, or reviewing the bank's own staff, puts you inside the bank's control structure. We note what you approve, under whose authority, and whether it ties to a defined deliverable.

#### Q: How much more tax would my corporation pay as a PSB?

On $163,200 of billings with $9,000 of expenses, corporate tax is $17,270.40 at 11.2% if it is not a PSB. As a PSB, the expenses are denied and tax at 44.5% is $72,624. That is $55,353.60 more for one year.

#### Q: Can my corporation deduct its expenses if it is a PSB?

Only a few. Section 18(1)(p) allows salary and other pay to you, the cost of your benefits or allowances, certain costs of selling property or negotiating contracts that an employee could deduct, and legal costs to collect amounts owed. Rent, software, equipment, travel, phone, insurance and accounting fees are denied.

#### Q: Can you make sure my corporation is not treated as a PSB?

No one can promise that. PSB status depends on the facts, tested year by year. We review your contract and working facts as part of tax planning, tell you what to document and help you reduce risk.

#### Q: Would a CPT1 ruling settle my PSB status at the bank?

No. A CPT1 ruling deals with CPP and EI status, not PSB status for income tax, though CRA looks at similar facts. If you are considering one, we first review whether it is useful in your facts.

## LinkedIn post (Blog2Social)

Many finance contractors at banks believe the staffing agency in the middle keeps them outside the PSB rules. It does not, on its own.

CRA asks whether you would reasonably be an employee of the party really receiving your services. For an FP&A, controller, risk or audit contractor at a bank, that is usually the bank. Finance is core to a bank's business, and bank-run controls, sign-off authority and supervision weigh heavily.

Take $163,200 of billings with $9,000 of expenses. PSB treatment means $72,624 of corporate tax at 44.5%, instead of $17,270.40 at 11.2%. That is $55,353.60 more for one year.

I explain what the agency controls, what the bank controls, and what you can document to reduce risk.
