## Post copy

H1: What Happens in a PSB Reassessment: Denied Deductions and Double Tax

In a PSB reassessment, CRA re-taxes your Ontario corporation's past income as personal services business income. The small business deduction is denied, the rate becomes 44.5% (versus 12.2% before July 1, 2026 and 11.2% now), and most expenses are denied under section 18(1)(p). Arrears interest is added, and dividends then carry personal tax on top.

**Key takeaways**

| # | Takeaway |
|---|---|
| 1 | A PSB reassessment removes the small business deduction. In Ontario, the corporate rate on that income becomes 44.5%. |
| 2 | Section 18(1)(p) denies most expenses. Salary and benefits paid to you stay deductible. |
| 3 | In my example, one 2025 year costs $52,455 more. Three years with the same facts cost $157,365, before arrears interest. |
| 4 | For a Canadian-controlled private corporation, the normal reassessment period is three years from the date of the original notice of assessment. |
| 5 | PSB income adds to GRIP, so dividends paid from it can be eligible dividends. That narrows the double tax gap but does not close it. |

### H2: What is a PSB reassessment?

A PSB reassessment happens when CRA reviews corporate tax returns you have already filed and decides that the corporation carried on a personal services business (PSB) in those years. CRA then issues a notice of reassessment for each year it changes, with the tax recomputed under the PSB rules.

Most of the contractors I work with are IT and finance professionals. They bill through their own Ontario corporation, often through a staffing agency, for a bank, an insurer, a government or a large enterprise. They file a T2 each year and claim the small business deduction. A reassessment takes that claim away for the years CRA changes.

Under section 125(7) of the Income Tax Act, a corporation carries on a PSB in a year when two things are true. First, the person who does the work, or a person related to them, is a specified shareholder, which means owning 10% or more of any class of shares, directly or indirectly. Second, that person would reasonably be regarded as an officer or employee of the client if the corporation did not exist. I explain the tests and the two exceptions on our page about [personal services business risk](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-risk-incorporated-contractors/).

A reassessment is the end of a process. It usually starts with a letter and a questionnaire, then document requests, possibly interviews, and a proposal letter. I walk through each stage on our page about a [CRA PSB review](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/cra-psb-review-support/). This post is about what happens to the numbers.

### H2: What changes in the tax calculation?

Three things change at once. Each one adds tax on its own, and together they add a lot.

#### H3: The small business deduction is denied

The small business deduction is what brings the federal rate down to 9% on the first $500,000 of active business income. PSB income is not eligible for it. The Ontario small business rate goes too, and the Ontario general rate of 11.5% applies instead.

#### H3: The full rate applies, plus an additional 5% federal tax

Federal tax on PSB income starts at 38%, less the 10% provincial abatement, which leaves 28%. A normal corporation would then get the 13% general rate reduction. A PSB does not. Section 123.5 then adds an additional 5% tax on PSB income. That makes the federal rate 33%.

Add the Ontario general rate of 11.5% and the combined rate is 44.5%. For comparison, ordinary active business income above the $500,000 limit is taxed at 26.5% in Ontario. PSB income pays more than that.

#### H3: Most expenses are denied under section 18(1)(p)

Section 18(1)(p) limits what a PSB can deduct to four things:

- salary, wages or other remuneration paid in the year to you, the incorporated employee
- the cost of benefits or allowances provided to you
- certain amounts spent selling property or negotiating contracts, of the kind an employee could deduct
- legal expenses to collect amounts owing for your services

Everything else is denied. That includes rent, software, equipment, travel, phone, insurance, accounting fees and similar costs. The corporation still spent the money. It just cannot deduct it.

### H2: How much can a PSB reassessment cost for one year?

Here is one example. The corporation has a 2025 taxation year. It is in Ontario. It billed $159,000 and had $9,000 of expenses for software, phone, insurance and accounting fees. It paid no salary. Because the income was earned before July 1, 2026, the combined small business rate for that year was 12.2%.

| Line | As filed (2025) | As reassessed as a PSB |
|---|---|---|
| Revenue | $159,000 | $159,000 |
| Expenses deducted | $9,000 | $0 (denied under 18(1)(p)) |
| Taxable income | $150,000 | $159,000 |
| Small business deduction | Claimed | Denied |
| Combined Ontario rate | 12.2% | 44.5% |
| Corporate tax | $18,300 | $70,755 |
| Extra tax for the year | Not applicable | $52,455 |
| Cash left after expenses and tax | $131,700 | $79,245 |

The extra $52,455 comes from two places. The higher rate on the $150,000 that was already taxable adds $48,450. Losing the $9,000 deduction adds another $4,005, because that amount is now taxed at 44.5%.

Look at the last line. The corporation keeps $79,245 instead of $131,700 from the same year of work. If that cash has already been paid out, the corporation still owes the extra tax.

The rates have changed since 2025. From July 1, 2026, the combined Ontario small business rate is 11.2%, so the current comparison is 11.2% vs 44.5%. A taxation year ending December 31, 2026 gets a blended 11.70%. I set out the rate history in our post on the [PSB tax rate in Ontario](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-tax-rate-ontario-2026/). To run your own revenue, expenses and salary, please use our [PSB tax cost calculator](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-tax-cost-calculator/).

### H2: How many years can CRA reassess?

For a Canadian-controlled private corporation, the normal reassessment period is three years from the date of the original notice of assessment for the year. Some situations allow CRA to go further back, so the dates on your own notices need to be checked.

PSB status is tested year by year, so each year is reviewed on its own facts. But when the same contract and the same way of working run through several years, the exposure can repeat in each of them.

If the facts and figures in my example were the same in three years, all at 12.2%, the extra tax would be $157,365. That is before arrears interest.

### H2: How does arrears interest add to the bill?

Arrears interest applies to the extra tax. It runs from the date the tax was originally due until it is paid, and it compounds daily. CRA sets the rate each quarter.

That means the oldest year in a reassessment usually carries the most interest, because it has been unpaid the longest. A three-year reassessment is not just three times the tax. It is three times the tax, plus interest that has been growing on each year since its original due date.

### H2: Why do people call it double tax?

The PSB rules are meant to take away the tax benefit of earning employee-like income through a corporation. In practice, they often go further than that.

The corporation pays 44.5% on the PSB income. Then you pay personal tax again when the corporation pays out what is left as a dividend. Here is the effect on $100,000 of PSB income, at the top 2026 Ontario rates:

- Corporate tax at 44.5%: $44,500
- Left to pay out as an eligible dividend: $55,500
- Personal tax on the dividend at 39.34%: $21,833.70
- Total tax: $66,333.70, or 66.33% of the income
- The same $100,000 paid as salary, taxed at 53.53%: $53,530
- Extra cost of the PSB route: $12,803.70

Salary has no corporate layer of tax, because salary paid to you in the year stays deductible under section 18(1)(p). In a reassessment, though, the years under review have already happened. Dividends already paid generally cannot be relabelled as salary after the fact. So for reassessed years, the dividend route is often the only route left.

### H2: How does GRIP narrow the gap?

PSB income is not eligible for the small business deduction, so it is taxed at the full rate. Income taxed at the full rate adds to the corporation's general rate income pool (GRIP). Dividends paid out of GRIP can be designated eligible dividends.

That matters. At the top 2026 Ontario rates, eligible dividends are taxed at 39.34% and non-eligible dividends at 47.74%. On the $55,500 in the example:

- As an eligible dividend, personal tax is $21,833.70.
- As a non-eligible dividend, personal tax would be $26,495.70.

The non-eligible rate rises to 48.89% from January 1, 2027, because Ontario is cutting its non-eligible dividend tax credit. So the eligible designation matters a little more from 2027.

So GRIP saves $4,662 in this example, at 2026 rates. But even with the eligible dividend, the total tax of 66.33% is still well above the 53.53% on salary. GRIP narrows the gap. It does not close it.

If the corporation paid non-eligible dividends in the years CRA reassesses, whether they can now be treated as eligible is a technical question about designation and timing. It needs review on the facts.

### H2: What happens before and after the notice of reassessment?

Before a notice of reassessment, CRA usually sends a proposal letter. It sets out the changes CRA plans to make, the years, the amounts and the reasons, and it states a deadline to respond. That is the stage to point out facts or documents the auditor has not considered and to check every number.

Our CRA audit support and representation covers that part of the process. You authorize us as the corporation's representative with CRA. We organize the documents, respond to CRA requests and questionnaires, communicate with the auditor, and review and respond to the proposal letter. Nobody can promise how CRA will decide, and I do not.

After a notice of reassessment, a notice of objection is generally due within 90 days of the date on the notice. There are later stages in the CRA process after that. Arrears interest keeps running on any unpaid balance during this time.

### H2: Does paying salary limit the cost of a PSB finding?

It can, for years that have not happened yet. Salary paid to you in the year stays deductible under section 18(1)(p). So less income is left in the corporation to be taxed at 44.5%.

Here is an example for a full taxation year at the current 11.2% rate. The corporation bills $171,000 and has $9,000 of expenses.

- With no salary: tax is $18,144 if the corporation is not a PSB, and $76,095 if it is. The PSB cost is $57,951.
- With a $120,000 salary paid to you: tax is $4,704 if the corporation is not a PSB, and $22,695 if it is. The PSB cost is $17,991.

These are corporate tax figures only. Employer CPP is left out, and the personal tax on the salary is not shown, so this is not a full comparison of pay mixes. The point is narrower. Salary reduces how much income is exposed to the 44.5% rate. It does not change whether the corporation is a PSB.

### H2: How can you reduce the risk before CRA asks?

The cheapest reassessment is the one that never comes, but I cannot promise that for anyone. What I can do is help you reduce risk and support your position with real facts.

Here is what I suggest each year:

- Keep the agency agreement, every statement of work and every renewal.
- Keep a record of who approves your time, who supervises your work, and whose equipment you use.
- Keep evidence of your own business: other clients, your own tools, marketing, and any right to send someone else to do the work.
- Think about salary as part of your pay mix. Salary does not change whether the corporation is a PSB, but if it is, salary stays deductible.

We review your contract and working facts as part of tax planning. We are accountants, not lawyers, so contract wording is for your lawyer. For a full list of what to keep and review, please see our [PSB risk checklist](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-risk-reduction-checklist/).

If CRA has written to your corporation about PSB status, or you want to understand your exposure for past years, please [book a consultation](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/consultation/). Bring your notices of assessment, your agency agreement and your statements of work. You can also call us at 647-212-9559 or email info@gondaliyacpa.ca, 9AM to 8:30PM, Monday to Sunday.

## FAQs

#### Q: What is a PSB reassessment?

It is a notice of reassessment in which CRA re-taxes a corporation's past income as personal services business income. The small business deduction is denied, the Ontario rate becomes 44.5%, and most expenses are denied under section 18(1)(p).

#### Q: Which expenses are still deductible after a PSB reassessment?

Salary, wages or other remuneration paid in the year to the incorporated employee, the cost of benefits or allowances provided to them, certain amounts spent selling property or negotiating contracts that an employee could deduct, and legal expenses to collect amounts owing for services. Other costs are denied.

#### Q: How far back can CRA reassess a corporation for PSB?

For a Canadian-controlled private corporation, the normal reassessment period is three years from the date of the original notice of assessment for the year. Some situations allow CRA to go further back, so please check the dates on your own notices.

#### Q: Does arrears interest apply to a PSB reassessment?

Yes. Arrears interest applies to the extra tax from the date it was originally due until it is paid, and it compounds daily. The oldest year in a reassessment usually carries the most interest.

#### Q: Why is PSB income said to be taxed twice?

The corporation pays 44.5% on the income, and you pay personal tax again on the dividend. On $100,000 at the top 2026 Ontario rates, the total is $66,333.70, compared with $53,530 if the same amount were paid as salary.

#### Q: Can dividends paid from PSB income be eligible dividends?

Yes. PSB income is taxed at the full rate, so it adds to the general rate income pool (GRIP), and dividends paid from it can be designated eligible dividends. That narrows the gap with salary but does not close it.

#### Q: What happens after I receive a notice of reassessment?

The notice shows the revised tax, the balance owing and arrears interest. A notice of objection is generally due within 90 days of the date on the notice of reassessment. Please do not wait until the end of that period to get advice.

## LinkedIn post (Blog2Social)

What happens if CRA decides your corporation was a personal services business for a past year?

Three things change. The small business deduction is denied. The income is taxed at 44.5% in Ontario. Most expenses are denied under section 18(1)(p).

In one example, a 2025 year filed at 12.2% on $150,000 is reassessed at 44.5% on $159,000. That is $52,455 more for one year, and $157,365 over three years, before arrears interest.

Paying the rest out as dividends adds personal tax on top. PSB income adds to GRIP, so those dividends can be eligible, but the total still comes out higher than salary.

I wrote a plain, line-by-line explanation for incorporated IT and finance contractors in Ontario.

#PersonalServicesBusiness #CorporateTax #Contractors

## Post copy

H1: PSB Risk Reduction Checklist for Incorporated Contractors

A PSB risk checklist is a yearly review of the facts CRA weighs when it asks whether your Ontario corporation is a personal services business: your contract, how you work, your tools, clients, records and pay. It cannot decide your status. It helps reduce risk and document your position, where the gap is 11.2% vs 44.5%.

**Key takeaways**

| # | Takeaway |
|---|---|
| 1 | CRA tests the whole working relationship, year by year. No single clause or fact decides PSB status. |
| 2 | On $150,000 of income kept in the corporation, PSB status means $49,950 more tax in one year: 44.5% instead of 11.2%. |
| 3 | Paper only helps when it matches how the work is really done. Never create or change a document after the fact. |
| 4 | Salary paid to you stays deductible even if the corporation is a PSB, so your pay mix changes how much is exposed. |
| 5 | Work through this list once a year with your accountant, and again whenever a contract or end client changes. |

### H2: What is PSB risk, in plain words?

A personal services business, or PSB, is defined in section 125(7) of the Income Tax Act. Your corporation is at risk when two things are true.

First, you, as the person doing the work, or someone related to you, own 10% or more of any class of the corporation's shares, directly or indirectly. That makes you a specified shareholder. The law calls the person doing the work the incorporated employee.

Second, you would reasonably be regarded as an officer or employee of the client if your corporation did not exist.

There are two exceptions. One is a corporation that employs more than five full-time employees in the business throughout the year. That means at least six, full-time, all year. Whether owners or family members count depends on their real roles, so it needs review. The other is income received from a corporation associated with yours. Most one-person contractor corporations fit neither.

If CRA treats your corporation as a PSB, it loses the small business deduction and the 13% federal general rate reduction. It also pays an additional 5% federal tax under section 123.5. The combined Ontario rate becomes 44.5%: 33% federal plus 11.5% Ontario. Section 18(1)(p) then denies most expenses. I cover the full rule on my page about [personal services business risk](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-risk-incorporated-contractors/).

### H2: What is at stake? A worked example

Here is Example A at current rates. Your corporation keeps $150,000 of income for the year.

| Scenario | Rate | Tax on $150,000 |
|---|---|---|
| Combined Ontario small business rate, from July 1, 2026 | 11.2% | $16,800.00 |
| Blended rate, December 31, 2026 year-end | 11.70% | $17,543.84 |
| Combined Ontario small business rate, income before July 1, 2026 | 12.2% | $18,300.00 |
| PSB rate | 44.5% | $66,750.00 |
| Extra tax as a PSB, compared with 11.2% | 33.3 percentage points | $49,950.00 |

The current comparison is 11.2% vs 44.5%. A December 31, 2026 year-end gets the blended 11.70% because the Ontario rate dropped on July 1, 2026.

Now the other side. Say this checklist and its records take one hour a month, or 12 hours a year. At a billing rate of $95 an hour, that is $1,140 of your time.

I am not saying $1,140 of record keeping saves $49,950. No checklist can promise that. But if CRA asks, your records are how you show what really happened. Without them, you are explaining from memory, years later.

A review can also reach back more than one year. Take a 2025 year with $159,000 of revenue and $9,000 of expenses. Filed at 12.2%, the tax is $18,300. Reassessed as a PSB, the expenses are denied and the tax is $70,755, which is $52,455 more. If the same facts repeat for three years, that is $157,365 before arrears interest. My post on what happens in a [PSB reassessment](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-reassessment-denied-deductions/) walks through how that builds.

### H2: How should you use this PSB risk checklist?

Work through it once a year with your accountant, before your corporation's year-end. Go through it again when you sign a new contract, renew with a new statement of work, or change end clients. PSB status is tested year by year, and each relationship stands on its own facts.

Be honest about how things really work. A checklist filled in to look good is worse than no checklist. Records must show what actually happened. Never create, backdate or change a document to fit the list.

The tests behind the list come from two court cases, Wiebe Door Services Ltd. v. M.N.R. and 671122 Ontario Ltd. v. Sagaz Industries Canada Inc. The central question is whose business it is. CRA looks at control, ownership of tools and equipment, chance of profit and risk of loss, and integration. It also looks at hiring helpers, investment and management. CRA guide RC4110, "Employee or Self-Employed?", sets out how CRA looks at a working relationship.

### H2: What is on the PSB risk checklist?

The list has seven groups. Each table shows the item, why it matters, and what to keep on file.

#### H3: 1. Contract and paperwork

| Item | Why it matters | What to keep on file |
|---|---|---|
| Read the full agency contract and every statement of work at each renewal | CRA usually starts with the contract. It shows the terms the parties agreed to. | The signed contract, every statement of work, and every renewal or amendment |
| Confirm your corporation, not you personally, is the party to the contract | The corporation is the business that supplies the services and gets paid for them | The signed contract showing the corporation's full legal name |
| Note the clauses on hours, location, supervision, termination notice, exclusivity and substitution | These clauses speak to control and integration, the core of the test | A one-page summary of these clauses, updated each year |
| Keep the end client's onboarding documents | In a three-party arrangement, the end client's rules matter as much as the agency contract | Policies, codes of conduct and access rules you signed or acknowledged |
| Bill through numbered corporate invoices | Invoices from the corporation support that the corporation, not you, supplies the services | Every invoice, numbered in order, with GST/HST shown |

We are accountants, not lawyers. If a clause needs new wording, that is for your lawyer. We review your contract and working facts as part of tax planning, and my page on [PSB contract review](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/contract-review-psb-indicators/) explains how we read them. My post on the [contract clauses that raise PSB risk](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/contract-clauses-psb-risk/) goes through the common ones.

#### H3: 2. How the work is actually done

| Item | Why it matters | What to keep on file |
|---|---|---|
| Who decides what you work on, how and when | Control is a main factor. Being told how to do the work points toward employment. | Statements of work, deliverable lists and emails that set the scope |
| Your hours and schedule | Fixed hours of 35, 37.5 or 40 a week, set schedules and timesheet approval by the end client are control indicators. Hours alone never decide status. | Timesheets, approvals, and notes on any real flexibility you use |
| How your work is reviewed | Sign-off on deliverables looks like a business result. Staff-style performance reviews look like employment. | Emails or notes showing how work is accepted |
| How you appear inside the client | Integration asks whether the work is part of the client's business or your own. Staff titles and a place on the org chart point toward employment. | Your badge type, your title, and how you are listed in client systems |
| Whether you can use helpers or a substitute | The freedom to hire help points toward your own business. A clause that says only you may do the work points the other way. | The clause, plus contracts and invoices for any subcontractor you used |

Contract wording and daily reality must match. If the contract says you control your schedule but the end client approves every hour, CRA will look at what actually happens.

#### H3: 3. Tools and costs

| Item | Why it matters | What to keep on file |
|---|---|---|
| Laptop, software and home office | Ownership of tools and equipment is a factor. Client-supplied laptops and VPN-only access are control indicators. | Purchase receipts, software licence invoices and a list of equipment the corporation owns |
| Training, certifications and professional dues | Costs your corporation carries show investment in its own business | Invoices paid from the corporate bank account |
| Business insurance | Carrying your own risk points toward a business | The policy and proof of payment |
| Travel and onsite costs | Who pays, and whether you carry costs you cannot recover, speaks to risk of loss | Expense records and receipts |

Keep these receipts either way. They support the deductions you claim and show your investment. If PSB applies, section 18(1)(p) denies costs such as rent, software, equipment, travel, phone, insurance and accounting fees.

#### H3: 4. Clients and marketing

| Item | Why it matters | What to keep on file |
|---|---|---|
| How many clients the corporation served this year | Serving more than one client shows a business open to the market | Invoices and contracts by client |
| What you did to find work | Marketing and proposals show you are in business on your own account | Proposals, quotes, your website and marketing costs |
| Exclusivity and non-compete limits | A contract that blocks other work makes an independent business harder to show | The clause, and any other work you took on within its limits |
| Work you bid on or turned down | It shows you choose your clients and your terms | Emails and proposals, including those that did not close |

#### H3: 5. Money and records

| Item | Why it matters | What to keep on file |
|---|---|---|
| A separate corporate bank account | All income in and all business costs out of one account shows a real business. CRA usually asks for records in a review. | Monthly bank statements and reconciliations |
| How your rate was set | A negotiated rate or fixed-price work shows a chance of profit. A fixed hourly rate with no risk points toward employment. | Rate emails, quotes and any fixed-price agreements |
| Risk of loss | Unpaid invoices, rework at your own cost and liability you carry show a business at risk | Records of bad debts, rework and claims |
| Bookkeeping and GST/HST kept current | Current books support every figure on your T2 and GST/HST returns | Books, GST/HST returns and filing confirmations |
| Who owns the shares | The test asks whether you, or a person related to you, own 10% or more of any class of shares, directly or indirectly | Share register and minute book, kept current |

#### H3: 6. Pay mix

| Item | Why it matters | What to keep on file |
|---|---|---|
| Salary paid to you | Salary to the incorporated employee stays deductible under section 18(1)(p), even if the corporation is a PSB | Payroll records, remittances, T4 slips and the resolution approving the salary |
| Income kept in the corporation | The more income stays inside, the more is exposed to 44.5% if PSB applies | The year-end tax plan showing retained income |
| Dividends paid | PSB income is taxed at the full rate and adds to the general rate income pool (GRIP), so dividends from it can be designated eligible | Dividend resolutions and the GRIP balance |

Salary does not change whether the corporation is a PSB. It changes how much income is left to be taxed at 44.5%. Take Example B: revenue of $171,000, expenses of $9,000, no salary, a full year at 11.2%. If the corporation is not a PSB, the tax is $18,144. If it is, the expenses are denied and all $171,000 is taxed at 44.5%, which is $76,095. The extra tax is $57,951.

Now Example C, the same facts with a $120,000 salary paid to you. Not a PSB, the corporate tax is $4,704. As a PSB, it is $22,695. The extra drops to $17,991. Employer CPP is left out, and the personal tax on the salary is not shown. Salary is taxed in your hands, so this is a planning question, not a free saving.

Integration also leaves a gap. Take $100,000 of PSB income and the top 2026 Ontario rates. The corporation pays $44,500 at 44.5%. The $55,500 left is paid as an eligible dividend, taxed at 39.34%, which is $21,833.70. That is $66,333.70 in total, or 66.33%. The same $100,000 paid as salary at 53.53% costs $53,530. The PSB route costs $12,803.70 more.

#### H3: 7. Annual review

| Item | Why it matters | What to keep on file |
|---|---|---|
| A yearly meeting with your accountant before year-end | Facts change, and PSB status is tested year by year | Dated notes of the review and this checklist |
| Every new contract, renewal or change of end client | Each relationship is tested on its own facts | The new contract and an updated clause summary |
| Staff count, if you employ people | The exception needs more than five full-time employees throughout the year. Who counts needs review. | Payroll records for each employee for the full year |
| Any CRA letter or questionnaire | Your answers become part of the file and are hard to correct later | Every CRA letter, your replies and the dates sent |
| Whether a CPT1 ruling is worth asking about | A CPT1 deals with CPP and EI status, not PSB for income tax, though CRA looks at similar facts | Our notes on whether it is useful in your facts |

### H2: What does this checklist not do?

It does not decide PSB status. CRA weighs the whole relationship, and the same facts can be read in more than one way. A clean checklist helps reduce risk and document your position. It is not a ruling.

It does not replace legal advice. If your contract needs new wording, please speak with your lawyer.

It does not fix a relationship that is employment in substance. If the facts point strongly that way, plan for it, including your pay mix.

### H2: How do we work through the checklist with you?

Our office is at 55 Queen St E, Suite 1205. If you are in the GTA, my page for an [incorporated contractor accountant in Toronto](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/incorporated-contractor-accountant-toronto/) explains how we work locally. Meetings can be in person or online.

Each year we prepare your T2, keep your books, run salary payroll, file GST/HST and plan your salary and dividend mix. We review your contract and working facts as part of tax planning, and we go through this checklist with you. If CRA writes, we act as your corporation's authorized representative, respond to requests and questionnaires, and organize your documents.

Please [book a consultation](https://gondaliyacpa.ca/consultation/) and bring your contract, your last T2 and your notes on this list. We will go through them item by item. You can also call 647-212-9559, 9AM to 8:30PM, Monday to Sunday.

## FAQs

#### Q: How often should I go through a PSB risk checklist?

Once a year, ideally before your corporation's year-end, and again whenever you sign a new contract, renew with a new statement of work, or change end clients. PSB status is tested year by year.

#### Q: If I complete the checklist, is my corporation safe from PSB status?

No. The checklist helps reduce risk and document your position. CRA looks at the whole working relationship each year, and no single document or clause decides the outcome.

#### Q: What records matter most if CRA asks about PSB?

Your contract and statements of work, numbered invoices, corporate bank statements, equipment and software receipts, evidence of other clients and marketing, payroll records and every CRA letter with your replies.

#### Q: Does paying myself a salary reduce PSB risk?

Salary does not change whether the corporation is a PSB. It does stay deductible under section 18(1)(p), so it reduces the income taxed at 44.5%. Salary is taxed in your hands, so the mix needs planning.

#### Q: Can I lower PSB risk by changing my contract wording?

Wording helps only when it matches how the work is really done. CRA looks at the actual relationship. We are accountants, not lawyers, so any change to contract wording is for your lawyer.

#### Q: Should I ask CRA for a CPT1 ruling?

A CPT1 asks CRA to rule on CPP and EI status. It is not a PSB ruling for income tax, though CRA looks at similar facts. We review whether it is useful in your facts before you decide.

## LinkedIn post (Blog2Social)

Incorporated IT and finance contractors in Ontario often ask me what they can actually do about PSB risk.

There is no form that settles it. CRA looks at the whole relationship, year by year. What you can do is review your facts once a year and keep the records that show how the work is really done.

I put together a checklist in seven groups: contract and paperwork, how the work is done, tools and costs, clients and marketing, money and records, pay mix, and an annual review. Each item shows why it matters and what to keep on file.

For scale: on $150,000 kept in the corporation, the tax is $16,800 at 11.2% and $66,750 at 44.5%.

Please read the full checklist here:

#PersonalServicesBusiness #IncorporatedContractors #OntarioTax

https://gondaliyacpa.ca/psb-risk-reduction-checklist/

# Rate changes found

| # | Item | Figure in the brief | Current figure | Effective date |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Ontario small business rate | 3.2% | 2.2% (2026 Ontario Budget, prorated by days for taxation years that straddle the date) | July 1, 2026 |
| 2 | Combined Ontario small business rate | 12.2% | 11.2%. A December 31, 2026 year-end blends to 11.70% (181 days at 12.2% and 184 days at 11.2%, exact rate 11.69589%) | July 1, 2026 |
| 3 | Combined PSB rate | 44.5% | 44.5% (no change) | Not applicable |
| 4 | Small business limit | $500,000 | $500,000 (no change) | Not applicable |
| 5 | Ontario non-eligible dividend tax credit | Not in the brief | 2.9863% falls to 1.9863%, so the top Ontario non-eligible dividend rate rises from 47.74% to 48.89% | January 1, 2027 |

## Rate correction project (Monday, October 26 to Friday, November 6, 2026)

About 400 existing URLs still show 12.2% or 3.2% as the current rate. Responsible person: Navneet. Each URL is corrected individually with these rules. Navneet sends the before and after to Sharad in daily batches of 50 URLs from Monday, October 26, Sharad approves each batch within one business day, and every corrected URL is live by Friday, November 6, 2026.

| # | Where the figure appears | Action |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | 12.2% stated as the current combined Ontario small business rate | Change to 11.2% and add "(12.2% before July 1, 2026)" once per page |
| 2 | 3.2% stated as the current Ontario small business rate | Change to 2.2% and add "(3.2% before July 1, 2026)" once per page |
| 3 | 12.2% or 3.2% tied to 2025 or an earlier year, or to income earned before July 1, 2026 | Leave as is |
| 4 | Dollar figures worked out from 12.2%, for example tax of $12,200 on $100,000 | Recalculate at 11.2%, for example $11,200 on $100,000, and send to Sharad before updating |
| 5 | An example for a December 31, 2026 year-end | Use the blended 11.70% |
| 6 | Calculators with 0.122 or 0.032 in the code | Navneet replaces the rate with the day-prorated logic used in the page 7 calculator |
| 7 | Small business limit shown as $600,000 | Change to $500,000 |
